# Consideration of funding proposals – Addendum VII Funding proposal package for FP212

## Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

- a) A funding proposal summary titled "Green Fund: Investing in Inclusive Agriculture and Protecting Forests" submitted by the Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingssamenwerking (FM01).
- b) The submission of the proposal to the national designated authority or local point(s).
- c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure.
- d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment.
- e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment, and
- f) General documentation of the funding proposal.

These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated authority[ies] or local point[s], respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure Policy of the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the Board Members and Alternate Board Members have duty to Board Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. Accredited Entities are required to submit all annexes and any other relevant materials provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the next submission.

* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Any page exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* The reference text is Arial, size 10.
* Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. Accredited Entities are required to inform the IDP-Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

Please submit the completed proposal to:

fundingproposal@unfccc.org

Please use the following format for the file name: 'FP [Accredited Entity Short Name] [Country/Region] (YYYY/MM/DD)'

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

## FMIO, as the Requested Entity, requests GCF support to transform major tropical commodity supply chains to be deforestation free and socially inclusive in a way that is commercially viable and replicable at scale.

### 1. Tropical forests are biodiversity hotspots that moderate water supply and quality, sustain millions of livelihoods, and provide ecosystem services worth $3 trillion annually. They also play a vital role in the global carbon cycle: the 1.2 billion ha of tropical forests constitute the largest above ground terrestrial carbon sink on Earth.

### 2. Deforestation, including below-ground biomass and drainage of peat forests, is a major driver of climate change, accounting for approximately one-fifth of all greenhouse gas emissions globally. This deforestation is compounded challenges of massive GHG emissions while undoing forests' role as a sink and store of carbon. A quarter of the world's tropical forest area has been lost since 1900.

### 3. More than 25 million hectares of tree cover were lost in 2020, almost one hectare per second, driven mainly by deforestation for agriculture and logging. This loss is not only a threat to biodiversity but also to the food we eat as it is driven by growing demand from rising global populations and increasing incomes. Agriculture is the primary driver of tropical deforestation, with 84% of all forest loss in the tropics occurring in countries, with commercial agriculture playing an increasingly large role compared to subsistence agriculture.

### 4. The loss of trees and other vegetation leads to desertification, soil erosion, reduced water retention and flooding, and increased vulnerability to climate change. This is compounded by higher temperatures and lower rainfall when higher atmospheric CO2 concentrations lead to contraction of plant stomata and reduced evaporative cooling from plants.

### 5. The GCF-Fund reduces emissions and increases resilience by financing the transformation of tropical agricultural supply chains into deforestation-free, socially inclusive systems. It does this through technical assistance and by offering finance to producers with conditions that require both the adoption of sustainable agriculture practices and the creation of social inclusion in the landscape.

### 6. By focusing on the most important tropical forested landscapes under threat from agricultural expansion and where the largest opportunities for transformational change exist, The target countries include: Brazil (Amazonas), Colombia, Costa Rica, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Indonesia, Republic of the Congo, Ecuador, Gabon, Lao People's Democratic Republic (PDR), Liberia, Zambia. The programme is implemented through a combination of GCF funding and co-financing from other donors and letters of No-Objection after the date of initial GCF Board approval.

### 7. Each country will focus on one or more major supply chains in tropical commodity supply chains while transforming them to no deforestation and socially inclusive at a landscape level. Investments target forest and peatland conservation, sustainable land management, and social inclusion.

### 8. Economic growth is not the only driver of deforestation. The demand for food, especially meat, is also major supply chains driving deforestation, including livestock, palm oil, soy, rubber, cocoa and forestry.

### 9. Economic development and population growth are key drivers of deforestation in tropical countries but growing demand for food. Producing more food on existing land reduces the demands and incentives for deforestation. The GCF-Fund supports this by investing in sustainable agriculture practices that are climate-resilient, that is, sustainable intensification rather than destructive extensification.

### 10. Significant progress has been made over the past four years, the programme expected investment lifetime impact is approximately 350,000 CO2e of GHG reductions and removals per USD 1 million invested. The GCF-Fund has a strong track record in this area, with a portfolio that includes a combination of satellite data and national Forest Reference Emission Levels (FREL/FRLFs). For a projected investment lifetime impact of approximately 350,000 CO2e per USD 1 million invested (USD 960 million over five years). For details on the mitigation impact methodology, see Annex 22.

### 10.4 Additional benefits include: improved access to health and water security and food and water security and - to Ecosystem and ecosystem services. Based on GCF-Fund's existing portfolio, and investment lifetime impact of approximately 350,000 CO2e per USD 1 million invested (USD 960 million over five years).

## B. Climate Change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The state of biological diversity:

### 11. Tropical forests are biodiversity hotspots that moderate water supply and quality, sustain millions of livelihoods, and provide essential ecosystem services such as carbon storage and regulating climate change. Tropical forests play a vital role in the global carbon cycle: the 1.2 billion ha of tropical forests constitute the largest above-ground terrestrial carbon pool on Earth.

### 12. Approximately 25% of global emissions come from the land sector, making it the second largest source of anthropogenic greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions after energy. Half of these emissions are due to deforestation and forest degradation?

### 13. Deforestation has been a major driver for tropical forests with more than 25 million hectares of tree cover lost in 2020, and a similar amount in 2021. The reasons for the tree cover loss vary between countries (see individual country information). In some cases, deforestation is driven by conversion to agriculture or logging, while in others it is driven by forest-to-agriculture conversion.

### 14. Most of the world's tropical forests are located within developing countries with limited resources and infrastructure, with commercial agriculture playing an increasingly large role compared to subsistence agriculture?

### 15. Most of these countries have a high population density and limited land availability for food production, leading them to convert forest into agricultural land to produce traditional staple crops such as rice or cassava.

### 16. The conversion of forests to agricultural land is often done without proper planning or management, which can lead to unsustainable production. This problem is expected to worsen without a paradigm shift changing how land is managed and used in these countries?

### 17. The economic impact of deforestation and forest degradation is enormous. Around 1.3 billion people, or 20% of global population, rely on forests for their livelihoods, many of whom live in poverty? Without healthy forests and biodiversity, the world will be unable to achieve its climate goals.

### 18. Forests provide up to US$ 100 billion per year in goods and services while providing additional environmental benefits such as carbon sequestration, water regulation, and habitat for wildlife?

### 19. Many agro-community livelihoods are vulnerable to climate hazards; for example, increased temperature and precipitation variability can lead to crop failure or livestock disease.

### 20. The loss of trees and other vegetation leads to desertification: soil erosion; reduced water retention and infiltration; and changes in the hydrological cycle. These effects are exacerbated by land degradation and drought?

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Current deforestation in Brazil

Brazil is home to around 12.5% of the world's forests and holds approximately one-third of all tropical rainforests. Terrestrially, it is also the most biodiverse country on Earth, with more than 43,000 described species of plants, 1,816 bird species, 795 mammal species, and 815 reptiles. About 80% of Brazil's forests are located in the Amazon Basin, a mosaic of ecosystems and vegetation types, including tropical rainforests (the most vast region), seasonal forests, deciduous forests, shrublands, savannas, and grasslands, including the open woodland cerrado.

### Current deforestation

Brazil has experienced high forest loss over the past two generations—more than 70 million hectares of primary forest have been lost since 1950. Brazil's National Space Research Institute (INPE) reports that more than 23 million hectares were cleared in the Amazon since 1970.

Brazil's National Space Research Institute (INPE) reports that more than 23 million hectares were cleared in the Amazon since 1970.

### Key drivers of deforestation

The key drivers of deforestation are:

- **Agriculture:** The largest driver is agriculture, which accounts for approximately 85% of all forest loss.
- **Mining and Logging:** Mining and logging account for about 12% of the total forest loss.
- **Infrastructure Development:** Infrastructure development contributes to around 3% of deforestation.

### Current deforestation in Indonesia

Indonesia is the largest archipelagic nation in the world, with a land area of approximately 755,000 square kilometers (half of which is forested). The country's population is about 268 million people (94.6% urbanized).

### Current deforestation

The average deforestation rate for Indonesia has been around 130 thousand hectares and 228.8 thousand hectares annually.

### Key drivers of deforestation

The key drivers of deforestation are:

- **Agriculture:** The largest driver is agriculture, which accounts for approximately 75% of all forest loss.
- **Mining and Logging:** Mining and logging account for about 10% of the total forest loss.
- **Infrastructure Development:** Infrastructure development contributes to around 5% of deforestation.

# Cameroon

Cameroon has 20 million ha of natural forests, covering approximately 43% of the country’s area.

## Current deforestation: According to the updated NDC, the estimated net annual deforestation rate is 0.6%. This combined with a low reforestation rate of 0.1% is leading to a net loss of forest cover.

### Key driver(s) of deforestation:
Cameroon's forests are under pressure from population growth and associated infrastructure development, illegal and uncontrolled logging, and unsustainable agricultural activities such as slash-and-burn agriculture and all other forms of unsustainable development of any kind that destroy the forest.

# Colombia

Natural forests occupy 59.3 million ha in Colombia, 53% of the country’s area, including the Amazon Forest (shared with Brazil) and the Orinoco Forest (shared with Brazil and Peru) within its territory. Of these natural forests, approximately 280,000 km² are primary forest.

## Current deforestation: According to the national REDD+ Strategy - Bosques Territorios de Vida -, more than six million hectares of Colombian forests were lost between 1990 and 2015. This is resulting in a loss of ecosystem and ecosystem services capacity, biodiversity loss, and climate change.

### Key driver(s) of deforestation:
The main direct causes of deforestation are expansion of the agricultural frontier, encroachment on protected areas, illegal logging, fires. The national REDD+ Strategy also points to indirect, policy-driven drivers such as land tenure insecurity, lack of legal rights, population growth, and demand for forest products.

### Colombia's most recent assessed forest reference level is 146,732,328 t CO2 eq/year in 2022.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## Côte d'Ivoire

### Current deforestation: Côte d’Ivoire’s forests are severely degraded in an early stage of secondary growth.

- **Forest cover:** Only about 10% of the country is covered by forest, down from more than 35% at independence.
- **Natural regeneration:** Currently, only 0.2% of the total forest area is naturally regenerated.
- **Smallholder farmers:** These forests are vital for smallholder farmers who rely on them for food and income.

### Key drivers of deforestation: According to the National REDD+ Strategy (2015), the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation over the period 1996–2014 were:

- **Expansion of agriculture:** 82%, including:
  - Increase in cocoa and palm oil.
  - Logging.
  - Extension of infrastructure: 17%.
  - Biodiversity loss: 3%.
  - Spreading bushfires: 2%.

### Current deforestation: According to the National REDD+ Strategy, Côte d’Ivoire’s average annual deforestation rate rose from about 0.5% in the period up to 1996 to an estimated 4.7% in the period up to 2016 (moving from approximately 350,000 hectares per year to more than 18 million hectares per year).

- **Forest degradation:** The rate of forest degradation is higher than other tropical countries.
- **Primary drivers:** Expansion of agriculture and logging.

### Côte d’Ivoire’s most recent assessed forest reference level is 25.9 million ha (2016).

## Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC)

### Current deforestation: According to the National REDD+ Strategy, DRC’s average annual deforestation rate rose from about 0.5% in the period up to 1996 to an estimated 4.7% in the period up to 2016 (moving from approximately 350,000 hectares per year to more than 18 million hectares per year).

- **Forest degradation:** The rate of forest degradation is higher than other tropical countries.
- **Primary drivers:** Expansion of agriculture and logging.

### Key drivers of deforestation: According to the National REDD+ Strategy (2015), the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in the country are:

- **Agriculture expansion.**
- **Timber exploitation to supply wood market.**
- **Logging.**
- **Biodiversity loss.**
- **Fuelwood.**

### DRC’s most recent assessed forest reference level is 1,717,384,195 ha (2016).

# Current deforestation: Deforestation in Ecuador leads to a significant loss of biodiversity, water reserves and carbon stocks.

## Key driver(s) of deforestation:

* **64.9%** of forest area was due to changes from forest to non-forest.
* **11.8%** due to agricultural mosaics.
* **10%** due to conversion of coverages.
* **3.7%** due to cocoa.
* **2.5%** due to oil palm.
* **2%** due to African palm.
* **0.6%** due to urban areas and dense rural settlements.

Ecuadorian forest area decreased from 14,987,771 hectares in 1990 to 12,753,783 hectares in 2014. The rate of deforestation was highest between 1990-2000: 129,643 hectares per year. For the period 2000–2014, it decreased to an average of 57,871 hectares per year.

## Current deforestation: Nationally, deforestation has been decreasing since 2000. The rate was 0.1% per year - around 25,000-30,000 hectares of forest loss annually between 2000 and 2014.

### Key driver(s) of deforestation:

* According to the national REDD+ Strategy, since 2012, industrial agriculture has been the main driver of deforestation. The establishment of oil palm and rubber plantations is also recognized as a driver.
* Some controlled deforestation of the degraded low-carbon forest landscapes in the Amazon region for the establishment of industrial oil palm or part of the country's economic development (agriculture, infrastructure expansion [roads, cities] are also recognized drivers of deforestation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Key drivers of deforestation:

In Liberia's Forest Reference Emission Level Submission to the UNFCCC, key drivers of deforestation and forest degradation are identified as large-scale drivers of deforestation and forest degradation.

Liberia's Agriculture sector under the Liberia Agriculture Transformation Agenda (LATA) is promoting industrialization of the sector with the aim of increasing agricultural revenues for farmers. The country has prioritized industrial oil palm plantations in order to increase its economic development. The area of land cleared for oil palm plantations in the last five years alone is estimated at 10,500 hectares and it is likely to rise to 200,000 ha based on current industry plans. This expansion will have a negative impact on forest cover and is likely to be detrimental to biodiversity.

Liberia's most recent assessed forest reference levels are (23.63 million hectares (Mha) (PL1) and 10-23,652.98 tCO2e/eyear (GtC/year)) in 2020.

## Zambia

### Current deforestation:

Land-use change and forest fires are the main drivers of deforestation in Zambia with significant rates of deforestation occurring across the country. Deforestation rates are significant in Zambia, with an estimated loss of 195,867 hectares per year from 2009 to 2018.

According to the data from Zambia's Ministry of Green Economy and Environment, the total area of land lost between 2009 and 2018 was approximately 1,915,862.73 hectares which translates into an average loss rate of about 195,867 hectares across the country. In the same period degradation occurred at a rate of 4,200,000 hectares per year.

The provinces with the highest degradation include Northern and Western province followed by Central province.

In the period 2000 to 2018, total emissions attributable to land use change and forest fires in Zambia were estimated as follows: deforestation (total) 356,284,812.29 tCO2e

### Key drivers of deforestation:

Zambian forests are vulnerable to factors such as extensive practices of slash and burn shifting cultivation, ever-increasing demand for firewood, charcoal production, unsustainable commercial utilization of indigenous tree species, and the expansion of agriculture in particular, the low productivity of small scale agriculture and degraded land. These factors put pressure to extract land use for agriculture in forested areas.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## Barrier: Limited capital flow due to high-risk perception by investors

In a low-carbon, climate-resilient scenario, ag-commodity producers, smallholder farmers, government agencies and other stakeholders would identify these climate challenges and invest the time and resources required to shift more of their production away from forests and towards more sustainable land uses. Unfortunately, there are multiple interconnected barriers that prevent a spontaneous response.

### Barrier: Market distortions undervalue standing forests and induce deforestation

Many of the benefits of standing forests, while critical to ecosystem health and local livelihoods, are difficult to monetize in markets. This is because the majority of these benefits are not reflected in the prices of products harvested from standing forests. Payment-for-ecosystem-services mechanisms are incipient or non-existent in most jurisdictions, making it difficult to earn money for carbon storage or biodiversity. Similarly, a forest

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND TOWARDS A NET-ZERO EMISSIONS WORLD

### B

**Barriers: Limited local access to knowledge and technology for non-foodcrop production**

- **Barrier:** Inadequate or poorly presented information on climate hazards and adaptive measures:
  - Reducing climate-related losses can go hand-in-hand with sustainably intensifying agricultural production.
  - Reduced emissions from agriculture are possible through the adoption of climate-resilient production techniques. However, this information is often not shared directly with agri-commodity companies or other buyers who could provide incentives for producers to adopt these practices.
  - The information available is only in a highly technical, undigested form that cannot be absorbed by producers, converted into knowledge and technology, and implemented on the ground.

- **Barrier:** Inaccessible regulatory or policy conditions that support sustainable practices and environmental impact:
  - In regions with significant forest resources, it is necessary for local authorities to be committed to the prevention of deforestation. This requires a strong commitment from governments, civil society organizations, and communities and civil society to achieve this.
  - However, only a minority of local governments in key regions have the capacity or political will to do so. The lack of adequate governance and provision of public resources, together with increased production and inclusive management at a landscape level, sustainable land use is not yet incentivized by the policy and stakeholder environment.

The ESG criteria financial and technical support levels the playing field for sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation-free agriculture. &Green Fund provides capital to new commodity production systems; encourages the adoption of best practices in land use planning and management; internalizes the market price the externalities provided by natural forests; reinforces public policy signals and encourages improved regulation and enforcement of environmental laws; and supports the development of a market for climate responsible producers.

38. This is reflected in our ToC statement, as presented in the attached ToC diagram below: if &Green Fund works across tropical forest landscapes to support agri-commodity businesses with capital, expertise and policy advocacy, it will contribute to the achievement of the SDGs, particularly SDG 15 on life on land; a climate-resilient, deforestation-free and socially inclusive model because private sector investors within this commercially profitable investment blueprint that aligns with the ESG criteria.

39. Official development assistance (ODA) is not intended for individual or household resilience of individuals, as well as ecosystem resilience. Under Output 2.2 companies will receive support to incorporate both mitigation and adaptation measures into their business models on a voluntary basis, and on an ongoing and continuous basis. In particular the 20 country and commodity specific climate hazard assessments prepared under Activity 2.3 will inform the development of sector-specific guidance for private sector actors in different sectors and jurisdictions, and further described section 2.4 of the Feasibility Study. &Green Fund’s approach to supporting companies to incorporate mitigation and adaptation measures into their business models is explained and guidance is given in the template for establishing the investment rationales (part of the Annexes of the EGSF).

40. The Theory of Change operates on several assumptions, which are explained in more detail in Section E – Logical Framework.

- Private sector investors are willing to participate if they can identify potentially profitable opportunities relating to NDPE (no deforestation, no peatland expansion, no exploitation) commodity supply chains.
- The private sector is open and willing to engage with &Green Fund to support its business model lead to replicability of &Green Fund’s business model.
- Public policy actors are supportive of the development of commodity supply chains will develop throughout the period of programme implementation in target countries.
- All projects will be aligned with national country needs and priorities identified in Annexes 7 and 8. See both annexes for more detailed information.

Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 19

Livestock farming: cattle

* Cattle are responsible for 8-4% of anthropogenic GHG emissions (FAO, 2013)
* Livestock use ~15% of global land area (for grazing and pasture)
* Livestock contribute to deforestation in deforestation and degradation of tropical forests (Hill et al., 2021) reducing ongoing carbon sequestration (Lloyd et al., 2021)
* Land that could be allocated for food production, energy production or land abandonment is estimated to reduce livestock methane emissions by one-third of anthropogenic methane emissions (Gurney et al., 2020)
* Livestock is the most extensive and widespread sub-sector to the adverse impacts of climate change

Cattle account for almost two as much anthropogenic GHG emissions as palm oil, soy, rubber, coffee and cotton combined (FAO, 2013)

* Extensive cattle farming leads to deforestation in deforestation and degradation of tropical forests (Hill et al., 2021)
* Livestock methane emissions increased by 5.7% between 1961-2018 with a downward trend continuing (FAO, 2013)
* Rising temperatures and humidity are reducing cattle milk yields in many regions (IPCC, 2014)

The livestock sector is responsible for global human water demand increase (primarily temperature) by a factor of 2.5 (Nemani et al., 2019)

Diversifying the genome can reduce methane emissions; breeds with lower methane tolerance (e.g. Brahman cattle) can reduce methane emissions per hectare by up to 40% (McInerney et al., 2018)
* Livestock genetics can reduce GHG emissions by up to 35% (Molteni et al., 2019)
* Diversity crops and livestock breeds can increase climate vulnerability (Baker et al., 2020)

Diversification of grazing areas can reduce methane emissions while contributing to global food security and continued rural livelihoods (McInerney et al., 2018)
* Livestock diets with no rice-based components can reduce methane emissions by over 50% (Molteni et al., 2019)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## Rubber

- **Expansion of rubber plantations is an accelerant of forest deforestation and carbon emissions** (Baker et al., 2020)
- **The land area allocated to rubber production has increased by less traditional producers**, including Myanmar, Cambodia, Laos, the DRC, Nigeria and the Congo Basin (Tobin et al., 2020)
- **Climate change is already affecting rubber production due to higher temperatures and more variable precipitation** (Pichou et al., 2021)

### As rubber is a never-planted crop, with average temperatures above 30°C, high temperatures will likely have a severe impact on yield.

#### Rubber plantations are carbon sinks but also contribute to deforestation and land degradation. They can serve as an alternative to monoculture as a sustainable agricultural model that can provide shade for other crops, present opportunities for intercropping, reduce the need for chemical fertilizers, and promote soil conservation in forests (Gebauer et al., 2018).

#### **Mitigation measures** include:

- **Reduced tillage**: This reduces carbon emissions from rubber production.
- **Improved irrigation management**: This can increase water use efficiency and reduce evapotranspiration.
- **Breeding for disease resistance**: This can produce varieties that are more resistant to pests, diseases, and abiotic stresses (Liu et al., 2021).

### In China is reducing soy yields - by up to 35% under drought years and 40% under dry years (Baker et al., 2020)

## Sustainable agroforestry: coffee

### Between 2001-2016, coffee plantations in Brazil were the largest source of forest globally, of which 54% were for robusta and 46% for arabica (Fonseca et al., 2020).

### Demand for coffee is expected to double by 2050 unless growers can mitigate climate change effects that increase coffee production costs. With the help of hectaric, the industry will need to expand the area under coffee production in the coming decades (Rath & Mendes, 2019).

### GHG emissions attributable to coffee-driven deforestation amount to ~20 million tCO2/year (Covarrubias & Saunders, 2021).

### Coffee is categorised as a highly vulnerable plant to climate change (Lavigne et al., 2018) extensive

| Brazil | Areca from Brazil |
| --- | --- |
| As coffee production increases in Brazil, so are the areas being driven by soybeans and other crops. | Areas of coffee production under soybeans are increasing. |
| Coffee is the primary driver of deforestation (Baker, 2019). | Coffee can be grown on land that becomes available as soybeans are cultivated to higher elevations; |
| Expected climate change will lead to a decrease in the area suitable for coffee plantations by 2050 (Lavigne et al., 2018). | Climate-driven suitability is projected for Nicaragua's coffee-growing regions, particularly shade-grown coffee varieties. |
| Coffee climate suitability is projected specifically for Nicaragua's coffee-growing regions, particularly shade-grown coffee varieties. | Projected suitability is projected specifically for Nicaragua's coffee-growing regions, particularly shade-grown coffee varieties. |
| Approximately 10% of the world's coffee production requires systematic climate adaptation actions, including: | Adaptation actions are required to produce a diverse set of effective means of reducing emissions and enhancing resilience (Echevarría-García et al., 2021; Lavigne et al., 2021). |

### Coffee production with appropriate agroforestry systems can significantly enhance coffee yields, particularly in areas where coffee is produced under shade trees (Lavigne et al., 2018; Ustin et al., 2019; Echevarría-García et al., 2021). Coffee can serve as a carbon sink and a reforestation tool. Coffee can be grown on land that becomes available as soybeans are cultivated to higher elevations (Lavigne et al., 2018).

### Climate-adapted coffee varieties with appropriate agroforestry systems and management systems can reduce the output in the face of adverse climate change (Kumar et al., 2019; Echevarría-García et al., 2021).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

### B

**Introduction in agro-climate extent (and within existing suitable growing areas) will increase yields (Blum et al., 2015; Thiruvathukal et al., 2016), while reductions in yield (Siddiqi et al., 2023) impaired by drought and lower quality (Gobena et al., 2021).** Uganda will likely benefit from increased yields, but the growing area of coffee will experience climatic change, becoming less suitable for Arabica due to climate change. The best option is to shift to Robusta and/or Catuai (Blum et al., 2019).

In the next 30 years, 75% of available, untransformed land will be suitable for Arabica coffee production in Uganda due to climate change, but only 40% for Robusta and Catuai (Blum et al., 2019).

Coffee is predominantly a family farmer's crop, produced on smallholder farms, of which 75-80% are owned by female households (Bassett et al., 2016; Piment et al., 2020) and a large proportion are climate-vulnerable farmers (Herrera et al., 2018; Sutle et al., 2023).

To remain viable, *efforts* must be made to introduce agroforestry systems that provide shade cover and provide habitat for bees, birds, and other climate-sensitive species according to Ong'era et al. (2017; 2021). This will also introduce incentives for farmers to safeguard their farms, including improved soil fertility, increased biodiversity, and higher farm income.

*Efforts* must be made to introduce coffee plantations with conservation areas around them, as well as incentives for the protection of these areas. This may have significance as well as provide reservoirs to increase water resources (Cochrane et al., 2019; Ong'era et al., 2023).

Processing of coffee results in significantly higher greenhouse gas emissions (+2-5 kgCO2e/kg) than cocoa or palm oil production due to methane emissions from wastewater and waste management (Blum et al., 2023).

*Efforts* must be made to introduce or scale-up agroforestry systems such as the introduction of native trees, shrubs, and grasses (intertops or windbreaks/shading) (Miyata et al. 2018; Blum et al., 2019). This will also increase the area grown under sustainable production standards (Ong'era et al., 2023).

*Efforts* must be made to promote downstream livelihoods generation using coffee residues (+5-6 kgCO2e/kg) for animal feed, mulching and husks, briquettes, etc. (Blum et al., 2021; Martinez et al., 2021).

# Cocoa

* **Cocoa production** was responsible for ~1% of global forest loss between 1986-2006 (Wookey et al., 2020).

* **Cocoa production remains concentrated in West Africa**, primarily Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana, but is expanding rapidly into Brazil and America, contributing to Amazon deforestation (Rustad et al. 2013; Orsini Agapito et al. 2015).

* Low-shade and no-shade cocoa systems are agroforestry-based systems with reduced tree cover and carbon storage (Silva et al., 2016).

* Prolonged dry season, increasing mean temperatures, erratic precipitation, and droughts are expected to significantly impact climate change production (Busch et al. 2015; Lebreton et al. 2017), with negative effects on optimal flowering, pollinators, pests and disease.

* **<10% of Ghana's forest loss since 2000 is attributed to cocoa production** (FAO, 2013; the corresponding statistic for Côte d'Ivoire is ~5%, FAO, 2018).

* Cocoa farming in West Africa is sensitive to changes in specific facets of the environment: climate change (rainfall patterns), rainfall variability, pests, etc.

* **Cocoa and coffee are alien crops with a short history of production**, that can be grown under different conditions than native species. Cocoa can serve as an agroforestry intercrop; e.g., on cocoa farms in Southeast Asia (Schmidt et al. 2015; Midtbøkken et al. 2016).

* Identify agricultural practices that reduce soil erosion and increase soil use and more efficient water production such as mulching.

* Agroforestry technologies safeguard food security, mitigate the negative impacts of climate change (Silva et al., 2020).

* **Effective agroforestry systems can mitigate the effects of climate change and maintain it in a state suitable for coffee production** (FAO, 2018; Silva et al. 2020).

* Agroforestry systems provide high productivity in addition to addressing stressors, including climate change, soil erosion, rainfall variability, pests, etc.

* **Cocoa and coffee are alien crops with a short history of production**, that can be grown under different conditions than native species. Cocoa can serve as an agroforestry intercrop; e.g., on cocoa farms in Southeast Asia (Schmidt et al. 2015; Midtbøkken et al. 2016).

* Identify agricultural practices that reduce soil erosion and increase soil use and more efficient water production such as mulching.

* Agroforestry technologies safeguard food security, mitigate the negative impacts of climate change (Silva et al., 2020).

## Cameroon and Liberia (Suh et al., 2018)
- Climate variability reduces maize yields in Cameroon, particularly in the south.
- Increased rainfall leads to increased incidence of droughts and diseases (Suh et al., 2018).
- Brazil will likely experience a 7% loss and a 73% increase in agricultural productivity due to cocoa expansion under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5, respectively (Bastos et al., 2021).

## Costa Rica & Japan (Suh et al., 2021)
- Incentives for local stakeholders to protect forested conservation areas and promote forest restoration, that may hold carbon stocks as well as provide ecosystem services.
- Introduce or scale-up agroforestry practices such as introduction of native tree species (intercrops or companion planting) (Ogutu et al., 2020).
- Introduce viable low-cost sustainable agriculture (LSA) technologies in low-income countries (Gupta, 2019).
- Broader coverage (through awareness raising), promotion of seedling and training on non-timber forest products (NTFPs) use by use of mobile apps in rural and peri-urban agroforestry systems (Suh et al., 2018; Braza et al. 2020).

## Timber
### Clearing of forests for timber production accounts for ~25% of global deforestation driven by commodity production (Lugo et al., 2013).
- Logging accounts for the largest driver of forest degradation in Indonesia and Malaysia (Curtis et al., 2017; Ken et al. 2020).
- Timber production is a reservoir of GHG emissions from tropical deforestation and land use change.
- Logging accounts for ~24% in Indonesia and ~35% in Malaysia (Parrot et al., 2018).
- The rate of timber production may increase due to the projected 67% by 2050 under RCP 2.5, with the largest increases expected in Southeast Asia.
- Timber production is a reservoir of GHG emissions from tropical deforestation and land use change.

### Logging accounts for ~24% in Indonesia and ~35% in Malaysia (Parrot et al., 2018).

- Application of best practices in white timber production and utilization, which can improve the efficiency of harvesting operations.

### Inefficiencies in primary forest management and under-utilization of timber resources will likely occur in temperate and tropical forests, with significant reductions (Fager et al., 2019). The introduction of national laws to protect these regions (Forest (2021)) can help reduce the impact of logging. These laws can also contribute to avoid or reduce the negative impacts of climate change (Papenfuss et al., 2018; Papenfuss et al., 2019). The introduction of national laws can also help sustain the biodiversity and ecosystem services in these regions (Lasseter et al. 2019; Nentwich, 2019).

### Modelling of tree species diversity and forest structure is essential to avoid or reduce the negative impacts of climate change (Papenfuss et al., 2018; Papenfuss et al., 2019). ## B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### GreenFund - Investing in Inclusive Agriculture, Protecting Forests
1. Note on terminology: "Green Fund" refers to the combination of StichtingGreenfund and the scalable investment vehicle suitable for commercial investors.
2. Stichting and Greenfund ("Stichting") is the existing entity that will be transformed into a public foundation under Dutch law (the "Foundation").
3. The Foundation will be established as an independent legal entity, separate from the Stichting.
4. A scalable investment vehicle will be established within the Foundation to provide investors with access to commercial investments, the form of which is the Green B.V.

5. ACOF's role in this project is to support the transformation of global supply chains by de-linking them from deforestation and exploitation and producing a positive social and climate change impact.
6. ACOF will work closely with GCF and other partners to ensure that the transition towards commodity-free production is commercially viable and replicable, strengthening the case for a rural development urgently needed in many developing countries.
7. The project will be implemented by providing technical assistance and offering finance to producers with conditions that require both the adoption of sustainable practices and the creation of social benefits (e.g., employment opportunities where appropriate) and the socially inclusive approach.
8. GCF Fund transactions create blueprints for sustainable land use, which can then be scaled up through the investment vehicle ("Green B.V.") established by GreenFund and GCF's initial investment in the market transformational.

9. ACOF will work with GCF to ensure that commodity supply chains while transforming them to no deforestation and socially inclusive at a landscape level investments into those forested and agricultural landscapes where the majority of deforestation occurs.
10. The following sectors are the major supply chains driving deforestation, such as livestock, palm oil, soy, rubber, cocoa and forestry.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

### B

#### 45. Green Fund promotes sustainable intensification and higher productivity on existing agricultural land to meet growing demand for food.
- Producing more food from an existing land base reduces the demand and incentives for expansion into new areas, which is a major driver of deforestation in tropical countries.
- That is, sustainable intensification rather than destructive extensification.

#### 46. Green Fund investments are co-financed by public and private sectors, with key investors including Norway (NCFI), FMO, Unilever, the United Kingdom (through the FMO-managed Mobilizing Finance for Forests programme) and others.

- The initial investments of the USD 142.75 million portfolio of Sitching And Green Fund demonstrate that, in large-scale projects, larger amounts of private sector contributions are needed.
- These are difficult to secure until the Fund has established a track record of successful project implementation and is able to leverage its reputation with the Fund to accelerate and reach the scale and private sector co-financing required to achieve transformational change.

#### 47. The Sitching And Green Fund has a dedicated Technical Assistance Facility (TAF), launched in 2010, in terms of which it manages TA services in cooperation with IDB as the “Technical Assistance Interface”.
- The Green Fund will continue to contribute to the TAF and will be responsible for its management and oversight.
- This is because the technical assistance provided by the TAF is a key element of the portfolio of that agreement, and are essential to the implementation of projects in the Sitching And Green Fund portfolio as well being sub-tied to managing the TA component of the agreement.

#### 48. IDH was the incorporator of the Sitching And Green Fund.
- An incorporation of an entity refers to the legal process by which an entity is formed.
- IDH and Sitching And Green Fund work collaboratively on a number of aspects, including the TAF and the JECAs.

#### 49. The Green Fund has established a Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria Approach (Jeca) as part of its responsibility in the operations of the Sitching And Green Fund.
- IDH also has no legal or governance role within the Sitching And Green Fund.

#### 50. Through the JECAs, the Green Fund aims to reduce risk and maximise the impact of the Green Fund (potential) investment projects by supporting investment readiness, monitoring, post-investment client support and sharing of lessons learned.
- The JECAs are a set of criteria that define the conditions under which the Green Fund will provide long-term supply chain landscape convening partner, IDH or other convening organisations” and its client portfolio.
- In addition to the above, in Component 2, the Green Fund may choose to cooperate with other conveners (such as Conservation International) for the implementation of projects that are not yet ready for investment.
- The JECAs are established (please refer to section G.3).

#### 51. A Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria Approach is a long-term basis to finance commodity supply chain projects.
- They include quantitative, output-based, environmental and social inclusion criteria and other conditions that define the conditions under which the Green Fund will provide long-term support.

#### 52. Green Fund catalyses investments into jurisdictions where local and host government authorities are also committed to protecting forests and landscapes, and where there is a strong commitment from private sector and civil society to protect forest and peatlands.
- This Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria Approach (Jeca) promotes the identification of jurisdictions that are amenable and supportive of the transformational changes sought.
- The Jeca is conducted by SAD Ventures under a contract with IDH, which will be used as part of the Green Fund’s JECAs once prospective investments are identified.
- In an initial assessment, the Jeca evaluates the following criteria:

58. Scope: The scale and quality of forest / peatland potential is significant and highly relevant from a global perspective; and

59. Assessment Strategy: There is a clear strategy with targets seeking the reduction of deforestation against historic trends in the jurisdiction. Of the proposed countries, GCF Fund has approved JICA for Para and Mato Grosso. The assessment process for these two jurisdictions will be completed by June 2018. The remaining target jurisdictions (Comoros, Ecuador, Gabon, Lao People’s Democratic Republic (PDR), Liberia, Zambia) are in the process of finalizing. The JICA process is paid for via the Fund's operating expenses (and would not be financed directly by GCF).

70. To prove the viability of the business model, the GCF Fund has already begun building its investment portfolio. As of 31 March 2018, the following investments have been made:

USD 30 million loan to FS Agriculturas in Brazil for the grain supply chain (corn and soy).

o USD 10 million loan to Agropecuaria Roncador Ltda (Roncador) in Brazil for its integrated cattle-and-soy project at scale.

o USD 20 million loan to Marfling Global Foods S.A. in Brazil within the cattle production supply chain.

o USD 7 million loan to Agropecuaria Bumbuca S.A.C. (Hacienda San Jose (HSJ)) to enhance dairy production.

o USD 15 million loan to PT Hutan Duta to reduce deforestation in their palm-oil operations in West Kalimantan, Indonesia.

o USD 20 million loan to PT Dhama Salju for their upstream palm oil supply chain in Indonesia.

o USD 23.75 million purchase of notes for PT Royal Lestari Utama for the development of three sustainable rubber concessions.

For more information on the investment summary, rationale for each investment and the expected environmental and social benefits, please see the appendix should you wish to view it.

58. The GCF Fund aims to invest in countries with significant tropical forests under threat from agricultural expansion and where the largest opportunities for transformational change exist. This includes, during the lifetime of the GCF investments, a focus on countries that are members of the Forest Investment Partnership (FIP) or have signed up to the FIP: Ecuador; Gabon; Lao People’s Democratic Republic (PDR); Liberia; Zambia. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## 54. Green Growth Approach is to finance deferring deforestation from major commodity supply chains by changing the economic incentives available to commercial agricultural players, and through supply chains to smallholder farmers. This will be achieved in two ways: (i) by providing financial support for investments in sustainable land management practices that conserve significant forest resources and preserve forest and other peat protection agri-ecosystems, as identified by the Fund’s jurisdictional mapping; and (ii) by providing financial support to commercial agricultural players through direct investments, by the landscapes program that convenes “Produce-Protest-Include” compact in agro-commodity production areas. These will be achieved through a combination of: (i) public-private partnerships; (ii) blended finance business model innovation and investment readiness initiatives and enabling impact tracking and sharing of lessons.

The project builds a virtuous cycle between GHG mitigation and climate resilience, while reducing agri- commodity deforestation and promoting sustainable land management practices. It will also provide an incentive to advance the agricultural frontier through deforestation.

## 56. The Trust Fund is structured as a blended finance vehicle. A private sector investor (requiring commercial returns) cannot invest in the current structure of Stichting AndCreen, which is focused on financing from UNR is a reasonable grant for example purposes. After the launch of the Fund – this will be the concept for the project and the Dutch B.V., which is the vehicle of GCF of GCF is structuring into a separate entity, which will be wholly controlled by Stichting AndCreen (a Dutch public foundation) and will be structured as a private equity fund. The Trust Fund will be structured as a separate scale. From private sector investors’ perspective, the short track record of GCF, the limited diversification of its portfolio, and the lack of experience in scaling up investments in agro-commodities, the Trust Fund will need to offer risk protection to private sector investors as it further scales to attract such investors. While this will be a challenge for the Trust Fund, it is not expected that the Trust Fund will have to raise new growth in assets. At around USD 1 billion portfolio, AndCreen Fund will have sufficient track record, diversification and cash flows (liquidity) to not need further risk protection (concessional) to raise further funding.

### Proposed program structure

The overall GCF investment is comprised of two main components: Component One focuses on the establishment and operation of the investment vehicle which will secure co-financing and invest directly in commercial agricultural players. The second component, Component Two, focuses on the technical assistance services that will further establish and implement the technical assistance services that overcome the capacity and information gaps for smallholder farmers to adopt sustainable land management practices. Both components are designed with a focus on building the capacity of practice for replication and scale-up for producers in target countries. Both programme components are elaborated below.

Specifically, Component 1, Output 1.1: focuses on setting up the AndCreen Fund’s structure and investment / disbursement process. The AndCreen Fund will be a separate entity from Stichting AndCreen and will be a separate investor vehicle for private sector investors. The investment vehicle will take the form of a Dutch B.V. AndCreen B.V., will be a new and separate legal entity, which will be wholly controlled by Stichting AndCreen (a Dutch public foundation). Once established, co-investment from the private sector, triggered by GCF support, will be secured from commercial banks and other financial institutions to scale capital disbursements to borrower companies.

### Component 1: A Green Financing Facility

#### Outcome 1: Scalable, replicable commercial models developed for deforestation free commodity production

Executing Entity: Component 1 activities will be implemented by Stichting AndCreen B.V. as Executive Entity.

Component 1 drives sustainable investment via three outputs that are presented in sequence, though practically the three outputs may occur simultaneously. The first output is to establish a separate legal entity for the Fund, an independent Dutch asset management firm, which will continue to serve as investment manager for AndCreen tasked with the operational management of the Fund. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Output 1: The G4een Fund is restructured as a Scalable Investment Vehicle

This investment vehicle will include the existing G4een Fund resources and assets and allow investment by the GCF an additional USD $25 million of private sector co-investment, with the ability to leverage insurance and bond issuance (including strategic corporates and family offices), enabling the Fund to launch and operate at scale.

### Activity 1.1: Establish the scalable investment vehicle

The new investment vehicle will be established as a B.V. This new investment vehicle will receive a transfer of assets from the existing Stichting AndGreenFund. An initial contribution of USD $25 million in private sector co-investment is required to establish this new investment vehicle, with the same terms and conditions as those agreed previously for the G4een Fund. The value of this equity is determined at current valuation of the assets transferred from the existing Stichting AndGreenFund (as per the latest valuation report) plus a USD $25 million subscription price (in kind). The G4een Fund is thereby restructured to allow investment by commercial private sector investors.

### Activity 1.2: Evidence of valid certificate of incorporation, bylaws, and bank account for investment vehicle

The documents showing the evidence to show the establishment of the investment vehicle will be provided.

## Output 2: The G4een Fund will recruit private sector co-investment to leverage GCF and existing G4een Fund resources

### Activity 1.2.1: Promote & Green Fund to potential private sector investors

The G4een Fund is already established, and G4een Fund will implement an outreach and promotional campaign to attract private sector investors. G4een Fund will promote the investment opportunity to large private pension funds, insurance companies, foundations, family offices, sovereign wealth funds, and other institutional investors NICEF and United Kingdom. The G4een Fund will engage in discussions with investors to ensure private sector investors are aware of the benefits that can be derived from investing in the G4een Fund. The G4een Fund is a viable investment opportunity that provides exposure to emerging markets and developing countries, which have significant potential for growth and development, as well as a positive environmental impact and provides opportunity for strong financial returns over the medium term.

### Activity 1.2.2: Negotiate and execute investment agreements

For each private sector investor, G4een Fund will negotiate and sign those regarding AML/CFT once investors are successfully identified and progress to the stage of due diligence and adhere to the Fund’s terms and conditions. The executed investment agreement is a binding document that sets out the terms and conditions of the investment agreement. The executed investment documents/contracts provide the evidence for the completion of this activity and set the stage for project-level documentation.

### Output 3: G4een Fund catalysts investments in renewable agriculture projects with robust environmental and social covenants incorporated into lending agreements as events of default

G4een Fund catalysts investments in renewable agriculture projects where local and host government authorities are also committed to providing support, including the incorporation of robust environmental and social covenants.

## B

### Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria Assessment (JEECA)

This process is mandatory prior to investment and assesses investors that the policy context of the investment is presented in a clear, concise manner. The JEECA will be conducted by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and evaluates the following criteria:

1. **Forest/Peatland**: There is a clear strategy with targets backing the reduction of deforestation against historical trends in the jurisdiction.
2. **Agriculture**: There is a clear strategy with targets backing the reduction of deforestation against historical trends in the jurisdiction.

### The Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria Assessment (JEECA) will establish, where in a given jurisdiction:

- A specific commodity drives deforestation,
- and will narrow down the scope of the investment universe that is interesting for forest/peatland conservation and sustainable land management (SLM), including but not limited to: climate change trends (FREEL, GFFI, local government monitoring platforms, governmental and multilateral institutions reports, academic literature, expert opinions, national strategies, international strategies, National Adaptation Plans, strategic National Communications, National Climate Strategies etc.), as well as of the monitoring capacity.

### Areas of concern:

- In highly trafficked forest areas,
- with populations living in or in close proximity to forest,
- where there is a high risk of deforestation (e.g., pockets of 50ha),
- and in highly fragmented forests (e.g., pockets of 50ha).

This is the case for example, in Indonesia, where the case is made around palm oil or rubber leases, cocoa farms, beside mining sites, along major roads, or in close proximity to urban or peri-urban areas. Annex 23(a) describes how GCFenables definition reforestation for the target jurisdiction in a conservative manner, and as such, it is not possible to define reforestation for all jurisdictions.

### As part of the Investment Readiness stage:

1. The Investment Manager will describe, and justify to the Investment Committee, the rationale behind the investment opportunity.
2. During the Deal Note stage:
   - The Investment Manager will establish the historic deforestation rates in the Area of Assessment (i.e., the landscape in which GCFen seeks to create impact), including its causes and a baseline estimate of future deforestation risks.
3. Once the Project Design Document is approved, independent third-party consultants will further assess deforestation risks, and the Investment Manager, in collaboration with the client, will determine in the Landscape Protection Plan the specific mitigation measures that are required for the project to be considered eligible.

### Once part of the GCFen portfolio:

1. The deforestation rate is tracked on an on-going basis using satellite tools and other data sources.
2. Mitigation costs are calculated as a percentage of the total project cost, including as referred to under (a) above, in a way that it can be disaggregated as mitigation and reforestation.

### The process described ensures that the deforestation-related emissions in the project area are typically compensated by the carbon sequestration benefits that are calculated and claimed using the conservative FREEL approach (as described in Annex 23).

### Once established, continued jurisdictional eligibility is dependent on subsequent biennial positive assessment of progress against the mitigation targets.

### Timely progress towards milestones indicated in the strategy of the jurisdiction, on a trajectory towards the long-term goal of zero deforestation.

### A transparent MRV system is operational to monitor, report and verify reductions in deforestation, and where necessary, adjustments to the project.

### Social & Environmental Safeguards: Programs shown towards implementation of the Cancun Agreement.

### 99. Component 2 further contributes to the implementation of the &Green Fund TAF, the services which will be delivered under this component are designed to support the development and implementation of the TAF. The TAF helps to overcome the many information, coordination, technical and policy/regulatory barriers that prevent effective forest protection and climate resilient commodity production in Indonesia. The TAF is a service provided by Stichting &Green Fund This extension of the TAF will focus on Engagement and Amplification, and enable the following:

## Accessing the Green Technical Assistance Facility®

99. IDH has an existing partnership with the Stichting to support the &Green Fund’s TAF where the Project Coordination Unit (established by that agreement) decides whether a project in the development and investment pipeline is suitable for inclusion in the TAF. Under this agreement, IDH will be responsible for the overall management of the TAF. The Stichting has agreed to provide technical assistance services to support the implementation of the TAF. Under this agreement, each of IDH and the Stichting is entitled to appoint one representative to the PCU, and the Stichting appointed representative will serve as the Chairperson of the PCU. The PCU is responsible for identifying and designing TAF projects, and the IDH submits projects for consideration to the PCU.

The PCU is also responsible for reviewing and approving TAF projects, and for overseeing the development of the TAF. For the GCF Proceeds for the TAF, an addendum to the current TAF Coordination Agreement will be concluded between IDH and the Stichting. The Stichting will be responsible for the overall management of the TA Grant. (Note that Stichting &Green BOO is permitted to appoint service providers to implement the TAF on behalf of the Stichting, subject to the terms of the PCU). The Stichting &Green Board shall retain all responsibility, authority and discretion for the implementation of the TAF. Over time the AGreen Fund will be responsible for the overall management of the TAF. This will include oversight of its operating expenses and with the rationale to secure sustainable TA activities. FMO, as AE and a contributor to the TAF, has agreed to provide technical assistance services in support of the TAF.

To start with the process, the GCF and IIS will work to develop engagement strategies for country and per region, including specific actions key per country and priority sector. These Engagement and Amplification strategies will be developed in close collaboration with the Country Programmes (CPs) and other relevant stakeholders.

Fund commodity sectors, plans such as the NDCs and NAPs, sector/country-specific climate hazard reports and the FMRP will provide a basis for developing these strategies. The strategies will include an analysis of the political economy and assessment of political and market dynamics.

1.1 Key elements of the regional strategies will be:

* Analysis of potential enabling environment drivers and limiting factors in the region, and in priority countries
* Analysis of key climate risks (e.g., water stress, extreme weather events, special hazards), mapping of existing platforms for engagement and political and market dynamics.
* Analysis of the current state of play in terms of investment instruments, business and investment models, special attention for gender, and stakeholder engagement methods (JEC, LPP, I4H)
* Identifying key entry points and opportunities to engage
* Actively reaching out to all stakeholders and development partners, to be developed/advanced with existing platforms and institutions, tools and knowledge projects to be developed, and detailed responsibilities and budget.

The country engagement and amplification strategy will be rolled out under Activity 2.1.1 into implementation, and focuses on the need to create and enhance enabling environments in target countries. ### Governance and legal structure

The Stichting is already funded with grants from Norway's International Climate and Forest Initiative (NICFI) and the Global Environment Facility via the UN Environment Program acting as accredited entity), and with concessional loans from FMO, the Mobilizing Finance for Forests (MFF) fund and a private sector investor.

In order to catalyse finance from the public and private sector to scale up the impact objectives of the Agreen Fund, the Stichting will be established as an investment vehicle. The Stichting will be a separate legal entity governed by Dutch law and will have its own board (the B.V. Board) which is independent of the B.V. under the laws of the Netherlands.

The Stichting will provide equity capital to the fund, up to the amount of €10 million. The Stichting will also be responsible for the Stichting will control the existing funds in the B.V., not from outputs 1 and 2 (i.e. The B.V. Board will have responsibility for the Stichting will control the existing funds in the B.V., not from outputs 1 and 2). The B.V. Board will be responsible for the Stichting will control the existing funds in the B.V., not from outputs 1 and 2).

The Stichting will also provide a vehicle to allow for the environmental and social returns as well as the financial and commercial sustainability of the scalable investment vehicle to be established by Green Fund, with Stichting AndGreen Fund including the GCF acting as the lead investor. The B.V. Board will have responsibility for making investment decisions through the purchase of Senior Notes (Tranche B).

The scalable investment vehicle will include the following governance bodies responsible for decision making and oversight:

- A Board of Directors ("B.V. Board") will have legal authority to represent the scalable investment vehicle and be responsible for making investment decisions on behalf of the scalable investment vehicle.
- The B.V. Board will establish an Investment Committee (B.V. Investment Committee) comprising investment, sustainability, environmental & social and credit specialists, and will include independent members. The B.V. Board will have responsibility for making investment decisions for the scalable investment vehicle to the B.V. Investment Committee.

#### Note:
There is no intention in case more equity into the B.V. The Stichting will be the only equity holder. GCF will be part of the Stichting. The entire fund will be managed by the B.V., which will have a separate board and governance structures. The GCF Proceeds will be provided to the Stichting AndGreen via the GCF Accredited Entity, FMO.

The fund activities are unlikely to be implemented at the scale which the GCF Proceeds are required to catalyse without the scalable investment vehicle.

## B

185. GCF will finance its subordinated loan through the AE (FMO) to the Stichting, and therefore will be part of Tranche A investors and governance.

186. The Stichting cataclysmic investors in Tranche A will hold an equity position in the structure of the Adem B.V., that is structured as a series of debt instruments held by private sector investors in tenor and timing of reflows.

187. Tranche A is acting as its principal catalyst to attracting commercial capital into the structure – by providing a front-end guarantee on the GCF loan, which will be used for the first drawdowns.

188. The cataclysmic investors will rank within the Stichting as follows (from junior to senior): i) NCFI (permanent capital provided by the Netherlands Climate Investment Fund), ii) FMO and EBRD for concessional loans, iii) GDF and EBRD for concessional loans) and v) FMO and the private reinsurer (concessional loans).

189. Private sector investors (Tranche B) will hold a senior position in the Adem B.V., as they will be issued with debt instruments (notes) with specific terms. The notes target private sector investors seeking commercial returns at acceptable risk levels. Noteholders are supported by the first loss provision offered by the Stichting and its equity position within the structure.

190. The articles of the BV stipulate that – once operating expenses (including the management fee) and from cash flows generated by the BV, the BV will distribute 5% to the GCF and 2.5% to the Adem B.V., and AndGreen Fund (either by way of dividend or return of share premium) an amount of 1.5% all on the share capital paid in by the BV. The remaining cash flows generated by the BV will be used for the repayment of the GCF loan, and any other financing costs of the Stichting. Any profits made by the Stichting is dependent on the performance of the BV’s portfolio which is (partly) financed by the GCF capital.

104 All of AE’s financing is term. GCF will subordinate to FMO but senior to NCFI and Universe.

105 These are not contractual obligations with their own specific terms. This will not affect the terms of the Stichting (where the GCF investment is agreed on the term sheet).

### B

191. Both the Stitching (Tranche A) and commercial investors (Tranche B) will earn returns financed by the interest earned on investments from the fund's debt investments.

192. Income earned by the GCF Green Fund includes interest income from the debt portfolio, interest income on any derivatives or other financial savings instruments and fee income (excluding upfront fees, structuring fees).

193. The interest earned each year will be proportionate from these portfolio investments; this will be paid out following a structured waterfall.

* For Tranche A: 50% of the interest will go to reduction in transaction costs.
* Noteholders (Tranche B) will receive distribution paid semi-annually and repayment of note principal (after 10 years).

194. Catalytic investors (Tranche A) will receive distributions paid semi-annually to preserve catalytic capital available for future investments. The interest earned on the debt portfolio will be reinvested in GCF Green Fund following the ranking explained above for the Stitching. Concessional loans within the Stitching will receive repayment of principal and interest, followed by a distribution of interest earned from the debt portfolio.

195. Any excess cash in the fund (upside) after the repayment of interest, principal and the catch-up to GCF will be distributed following a structured waterfall: Tranche B then to Tranche A grants and redeemable grants.

196. The Green Fund will have two tranches of investors: Catalytic Investors (from current USD 150 million) and USD 800 million (over the course of this decade) from Concessional loans to GCF by 2030. It will provide senior or subordinated debt to projects in Latin America, SE Asia and Africa (Togo).

197. Dividing the A$6bn GCF into two instruments:

* The Stitching: This is a concessional loan facility for developing countries. It will be targeted at commercial investors who acquire the Notes, while supporting their returns by utilizing risk-limitation, highly additional catalytic capital and other financial savings instruments.
* The Green Fund: This is an investment vehicle that will provide concessional feedback from commercial investors, private sector capital in being reissued with a 10-year lockup at the moment but that might be extended to 25 years. It will have two tranches of investors: (i) Catalytic Investors (from current USD 150 million) and USD 800 million (over the course of this decade) from Concessional loans to GCF by 2030; and (ii) GCF Green Fund seeks to raise long-term grants, which it regards as 15 years or longer. Longer tenure provided by catalytic investors will be attractive for commercial investors. Hence, Green Fund seeks a 10-year tenor from the GCF to provide concessional feedback to commercial investors.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1,000 words; approximately 2 pages)

Support for the GCF Fund will contribute to GCF's strategic mission and results.

- The GCF Fund is a global programme targeting 11 countries including Least Developed Countries and Small Island Developing States.
- Communities and businesses in the target countries are highly reliant on forests and ecosystems for protection, livelihoods, and food security. Forests and ecosystems are also critical to achieving national climate targets and national policies in the target countries as evidenced in their NDCs, NAPs and other strategies.
- The GCF Fund will support projects that address deforestation and degradation while addressing the climate resilience needs of people working at the base of global value chains.

11. The adoption of adaptation and mitigation practices focusing on forests is rarely taken up due to the knowledge and capacity gaps of those most reliant on forest resources (as detailed in Section B.1).

## Minor concessionality

- GCF projects do not have the financial resources to deliver the programme's climate resilience and GHG-mitigation objectives on top of existing development priorities or to prevent alternative, less forest- and ecosystem-friendly development.
- The COVID-19 pandemic has forced countries to increase spending and international borrowing. GCF investment in the AndGreenFund will help countries meet their NDCs by providing concessional financing for projects that reduce deforestation and eliminate all other development and environmental priorities.

- GCF financing to Stichting AndGreenFund will be provided as a concessional loan, with a catch-up for GCF at the end of the loan tenor to match the expected coupon of the senior commercial private sector investors. Grant Exercising comprises a small

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 50

## GCF support will overcome barriers and support a paradigm shift towards low-carbon and climate resilient development

### 214. GCF support for &Green Fund will help to overcome critical barriers to commercially viable, deforestation-free commodity production at scale.

The financial and market barriers outlined in Section B.1 prevent the &Green Fund from scaling up its investments in a manner that is consistent with the Fund’s mandate. These include limitations of other financing sources including other investment funds, financing sources for agricultural commodities, and mainstream banks’ unwillingness to finance projects that are not aligned with their mandates (diversified entities). These traditional sources of finance do not include sustainability commitments and typically offer terms that are inconsistent with the &Green Fund’s mandate. The &Green Fund is unfamiliar with sustainable production practices. Mainstream investors are unfamiliar with sustainable production approaches and this is perceived as a barrier to scaling up investments in these areas. In addition, the policy and regulatory environment surrounding forest-carbon finance has grown significantly more complex since the launch of the &Green Fund. This has created significant uncertainty around compliance requirements to crowd-in commercial investors at scale.

### 215. Lack of access to capital is a barrier for smallholder farmers because they are relatively small and the many family-owned, or smaller agricultural companies or farmers have limited access to patient capital. Without patient capital, these actors will not be able to invest in sustainable production practices that are more costly than traditional practices.

### 216. Economic instruments such as effective financing terms, legally binding loan covenants, technical assistance and policy support to align incentives between farmers, investors, local communities, and governments to drive the transformation of commodity supply chains

#### A critical mass of investment will crowd in private sector co-financing at scale

##### 217. GCF funding will enable the &Green Fund to increase scale, crowd in private sector investment and unlock additional private capital for sustainable commodity production.

Without additional capital from GCF, &Green Fund would grow incrementally, but would not be able to drive impact at scale. The &Green Fund’s business model is designed to scale up its investments (see Section B.1) to stop deforestation in the target countries. &Green Fund offers a market-ready platform and GCF’s contribution will enable it to scale up from private sector investment, and accelerate achievement of impacts that the Fund is targeting.

##### 218. To scale rapidly and achieve sector transformation goals, the &Green Fund must reduce transaction costs by scaling up its investments in a manner that is consistent with the Fund’s mandate. This requires a reduction in transaction costs, which are currently high due to the small size of transactions. The &Green Fund will scale up from private sector co-financing at scale.

##### 219. To scale rapidly and achieve sector transformation goals, the &Green Fund must reduce transaction costs by scaling up its investments in a manner that is consistent with the Fund’s mandate. This requires a reduction in transaction costs, which are currently high due to the small size of transactions. The &Green Fund will scale up from private sector co-financing at scale.

##### 220. GCF’s investment of US$180 million on top of the Fund’s existing US$180 million will provide credibility to the &Green Fund business model; change perceptions regarding the risk-reward of investments in this sector and unlock additional private capital for sustainable commodity production.

area as a significant economic opportunity, which will stimulate money to flow into the Fund and the sector more broadly and achieve transformational change, and large-scale mitigation and resilience impacts. There has been a lot of discussion about how to make this happen, but there is no silver bullet.
