221. Any potential GCF risks related to successful private sector funding is mitigated by phased conditional commitments, which are designed to be flexible in response to changing market conditions and investor exposures. There is a long-term USD 180 million contribution from GCF will catalyse institutional investor capital mobilization. The Fund’s business plan includes an initial USD 25 million commitment to private sector investments at portfolio level totaling around USD 780-870 million (see the programme budget in section C.1 for more details).

This mobilisation does not include the additional private sector capital mobilisation that happens at the project level, for the assets of the tropical commodity supply chain actors. AGRIM Fund’s Lending Guidelines require a minimum 3x leverage ratio on any investment made by the Fund. This is to ensure that there are no credit exposures. Therefore, at least an additional 3x sectoral leverage, or approximately USD 2 billion will be mobilised through private sector investments in the supply chain assets of tropical commodity actors. AGRIM Fund reports this mobilisation, but does not include it as part of the GCF’s own commitment. This is because the budget outcomes proposed to GCF are just part of the budget under C.1.

### B.3.2.4. The Fund’s business plan (pages 50-51)

224. GCF’s subordinate loan to the Fund will have a 15-year tenor. The capital invested by GCF, plus interest, will be repaid in accordance with the Fund’s distribution waterfall. This approach aligns incentives and ensures the Fund is not exposed to any credit risk.

225. GCF’s repayment and exit are built into GCF Fund’s business plan. The GCF Fund structure its loans to ensure that it can repay its capital and interest payments in a timely manner, while also ensuring that the initial client due diligence process includes an analysis of the market, the client’s intended customers and the client’s ability to generate sufficient cash flow to service debt obligations. A sensitivity analysis of the financial model shows that GCF’s successful exit would be ensured even with significant impairments in the Fund’s portfolio investments.

### Sustainability and replicability

226. The &Green Fund is designed from the outset for sustainability and scale-up. The Fund’s sustainability is driven by its ability to generate sufficient returns on investment, which will allow it to attract additional investor confidence. The 15-year commitment from the GCF will catalyse additional investment to provide enough resources to ensure that the Fund can continue to operate in a sustainable manner over time. This applies for all sectors and jurisdictions targeted, and beyond the period of GCF investment. &Green Fund issues highly transparent financial statements on an annual basis, which are audited by independent external auditors with regular updates on portfolio performance, including financial statements status of current investments.

227. &Green Fund is designed to be replicable and expandable in other tropical commodity supply chain projects operating at scale. The Fund will have the confidence and ability well beyond its initial investment period to attract additional investors from a wide range of sectors and geographies. At USD 1 billion scale there is sufficient volume and stability in the portfolio that actual and potential investors can be confident that they are investing with a diversified portfolio (actual investors will有信心在投资的特定市场（商业对商业投资被克服）。

228. Since the recent commitment to reducing and eliminating deforestation made at COP 21 includes 141 countries, it is expected that there will be significant demand for investments in tropical commodity supply chain sectors and scaling-up investments. Other investors will be larger to increase the potential of the Fund to deliver private sector investment. The Fund’s business plan can be scaled up by other investors who are interested in developing and development agencies can be included, complementing the project’s technical, financial and convening components.

229. &Green Fund investment strategy can be scalable by expansion of the businesses it invests in via a range of different mechanisms. These include: (i) scaling up existing investments; (ii) investing in developing and disseminating knowledge and learning products (such as best practice examples and case studies); and (iii) supporting the development of new markets for &Green Fund’s strategy to other regions or sectors.

230. **Green Climate Fund participates increasing interest in its approach and offering from potential Clients as a result of market signals from end-consumers and hence major buyers (multinationals) of tropical commodities.** A significant number of these clients are now looking for ways to integrate climate change considerations into their supply pull for market actors who wish to retain the ability to trade their produce freely. This creates an added incentive to focus, engage and support the development of a more sustainable supply chain.

231. **The initiatives supported under the project will establish a scalable mechanism that uses financial incentives to promote the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices in tropical supply chains.** The impact of this work delivered by these programmes across different geographies increases the likelihood that local actors (public and private) will be able to adopt and scale up these practices, thereby contributing to the achievement of the GCF Fund believes that long-term economic returns are a requirement for delivery of robust and sustainable impact.

232. **In this context, it is important to note that the current climate change adaptation strategies in tropical supply chains will be mislaid to their 2050 climate change targets.** &Green Fund will have played an important role in supporting the development of a more sustainable supply chain and ensuring that mitigation measures achieves results in line with private sector investor demands.

233. **&Green Fund, at this point, is not looking for additional funding to continue its operations.** The long-term goal would be to establish a fund that can operate independently from &Green Fund and that will be able to provide grants to other organizations or governments to support the development of sustainable supply chains in tropical regions. This fund will also be able to provide technical assistance, training and capacity building for &Green Fund as a purely commercial offering to continue its financing of the sector and drive sector investment. The role of &Green Fund is then to focus on mobilizing additional resources.

234. **&Green Fund, at this point, will be inherently replicable, given that it is based on a proven concept that deals with supply chains in tropical regions.** GCF’s Financial Exit Strategy depends on &Green Fund managing its cash flows (including debt interest and principal payments) to ensure that the fund can continue to operate independently from GCF. The fund will be constantly validated as new investments are approved and existing portfolio is managed. After year 15, consistent with GCF’s Financial Exit Strategy, &Green Fund will be able to exit the market by selling its remaining assets (i.e., cash) to a commercial entity or by paying out its equity investors.

235. **As such, &Green Fund will not be looking for additional funding after year 10 and then for equity investments (&Green Fund extends credit with fixed repayment terms), and thus the Asset Liability Management Policy (ALM) is not relevant in this context.** The focus of the ALM should be on ensuring that the fund’s ability to manage liquidity is more important. &Green Fund will manage its business so as to not be taking refinancing risk with respect to the GCF or any other repayment.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Impact Potential

**Mitigation impact:**

238. Based on results from investments over the past four years and potential results from pipeline investments, the project is expected over the lifetime of the investment to result in approximately 930,000 tCO2e GHG reductions and removals per USD 1 million invested, as a result of changes to agri-commodity production practices and land use. The project will also result in avoided emissions of approximately 450,000 tCO2e over the investment lifetime. Table Summary of mitigation impacts below provides an overview of the programme's expected lifetime mitigation impacts. For detailed information on the methodology and calculation of these figures, please refer to Annex 18 (see section for further details).

239. Introduction of agriculture and livestock management can, in some cases, increase emissions from land use change and forest degradation. However, this project is expected to result in a net full-scale GHG inventory because the material GHG impact from its transactions stems from the quantified forest protection and restoration activities. The transaction will be captured as an avoided emission under the transaction; this would be captured in the International Finance Corporation Performance Standard 3 risk assessment process.

240. To document removal estimates for the enhancement of carbon stocks in forest remaining as forest and afforestation, reforestation, and other land use change transactions, the project has used the latest UNFCCC-approved FRELs and FRLs. These removals are not claimed as mitigation impacts in the GCF report, to ensure alignment with GCF approaches. All removals will be transparently reported conservatively estimated against the relevant FREL or FRL.

241. Annex 17 (Multi-country Programme Information) illustrates how the programme's mitigation impact across the portfolio is calculated and presented. The table shows the average expected lifetime mitigation potential/ USD million invested ratio, derived from existing GCF portfolio investments. Precise country or transaction-level estimates are not available for all transactions in the portfolio, particularly for sub-projects that are yet to be identified; relevant downscalded information cannot be collected until pipeline projects have been approved and signed off by the host government authorities, including the NDAs. Annex 22 includes guidance on the approach taken to estimate mitigation impacts at transaction level, including a description of the methodologies, assumptions types and assumptions made in the calculation, and example calculations illustrating how the guidance is used.

The &Green Fund has developed a transformational change model:

245. By financing business models that focus both on generating revenue through commodity production and delivering environmental benefits, the financial conditions of loans can be changed so that lending products are offered to clients where sustainability criteria are intrinsically embedded in the financial conditions of the loan. &Green Fund aims to transform finance and businesses into mainstream markets such that they support forest protection and restoration of forests and the inclusion of smallholders and forest communities in the economy. This contributes towards achieving climate change mitigation efforts and the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) targets by mobilizing private finance at scale.

The GCF investment will be used by &Green Fund to realise the following two outcomes:

- **Outcome 1: Scale replicable commercial models developed for deforestation and peat free commodity production**
- **Outcome 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) supports an enabling environment for forest protection and restoration**

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 63**

### Catalyzing Impact

246. G4Green Fund employs a Production-Protection-Inclusion approach. This is a multi-stakeholder approach, aimed to secure inclusive, sustainable, deforestation-free production within a defined area (the Landscape) that includes private sector actors, indigenous peoples and communities, local governments, civil society. This approach is enshrined in a binding and contractually agreed plan (The Landscape Plan).

247. G4Green Fund generates its impact within supply chains that are active in landscapes where valuable ecosystem services are provided by forests and other natural resources, which are critical for sustainable production. That is, G4Green Fund directly targets the drivers of deforestation, rather than forest areas for conservation or reforestation. The Landscape Plan identifies the key actors involved in the landscape’s agricultural intensification (more production from the same area of land). Communities and partners in the landscape will be supported to adopt sustainable land management practices that are compatible with the landscape. This way, G4Green Fund can finance the process of derisking deforestation from major commodity supply chains.

248. G4Green Fund invests its credit only on jurisdictions that have a progressive forest policy agenda and the capacity to deliver on it. The Landscape Plan is developed in close collaboration with local actors and evaluates transactions with respect to the environmental and social impacts generated by incoming borrowers compared to their baseline performance. This ensures that the financial risk of the transaction is aligned with loan terms are aligned with IFC performance standards for each client; an LPP is developed with quantifiable targets, a monitoring plan, and a reporting framework. The Landscape Plan also identifies and creates a blueprint for sustainable land use and management that the broader market can adopt.

### Impact Model

249. Commodity players work in very low margin markets, where any changes to sourcing are risky and find it difficult to make changes without financial support. This is especially true of smallholder farmers businesses, and therefore there is reduced appetite to deploy it to make major changes to the status quo. Moreover, commodity players are often reluctant to invest in new technologies that could change their shift from fossil finances – therefore reducing these risks for the commodity players to an acceptable level.

250. The Fund offers financing that is structured to achieve transformational changes. Credit terms are flexible and adaptable to the needs of each transaction. This includes a combination of debt, equity, grants and/or repayment periods tailored to the nature and needs of investments. Commodity producers or supply chain actors can access finance for their own operations, as well as for the development of new practices and for opportunity costs associated with forest conservation and other environmental and social targets.

251. G4Green Fund imposes clear requirements on its clients regarding impact targets that must be delivered in order to receive financing. This is done through a combination of Environmental and Social Management Systems (ESMS) that incorporates the necessary process, resources and activities required for each transaction. The ESMS will also include a monitoring plan which allows investors to deliver on and report these outcomes.

252. There are a number of innovative financial products available in the market today, and it is a key objective for G4Green to demonstrate to the financial markets that adapting their funding structures could drive paradigm shift at scale. The Fund will also work with its clients to ensure that any transaction that G4Green investors has, as this serves as an “seed of approval” for high ESG quality and makes projects more bankable.

253. This is particularly relevant in the case of Indonesia, Brazil, Colombia), and increasingly G4Green is working with local banks to structure co-financing transactions. ### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

#### Contributing to climate, land degradation prevention efforts, and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) targets by mobilizing private finance at scale.

251. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will support international climate change mitigation projects by financing business models that focus both on generating revenue through commodity production - and at the same time decoupling revenue growth from deforestation and forest degradation. These business models are designed to be financially viable, scalable, and sustainable where sustainability criteria are intrinsically embedded in the financial conditions of the loans. These criteria are set out in the Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) and/or the National Performance Standards (NPDES), or equivalent) and NDFP policies.

252. The ESAP and NPDES will be customized, each taking into account the client type, jurisdiction, commodity supply chain, and individual landscape conditions.

253. Landscape Protection Plan: A comprehensive, long-term land-use and management plan developed by the project team in collaboration with local stakeholders, covering all relevant areas of high carbon stock and high conservation value forest and peatlands.

254. Environmental and Social Action Plan (a document that defines how the client will implement and monitor projects to ensure they meet environmental and social performance standards). The ESAP and NPDES or equivalent) and NDFP policies, investors will transition their business models from being carbon intensive to being carbon neutral or even carbon negative.

256. Through the implementation of ESAP and NPDES policies, investors will transform their business models into ones that are financially viable, create lasting, irreversible change and serve as blueprints for replication and scale up.

257. The GCF Fund plans to scale up as follows:

- With the first 1 billion USD in GCF in Q3, 2023, the GCF Fund will then unlock 600 million in additional commitments from commercial investors.
- By December 2025, the Fund will meet its initial impact and targets (5 million hectares of forest conserved, 585,000 beneficiaries) and achieve a USD 400 million portfolio, with major climate change mitigation impacts.
- By December 2030, the Fund will meet its multi-year impact and achieve a USD 960 million portfolio, with significant biodiversity conservation.

258. The Fund can expand to other commercially productive landscapes, and other countries, provided they meet eligibility and investment criteria and these opportunities attract additional investors. Other investors will be solicited through the GCF’s existing network of partners and through its website. In addition, international investors and development agencies can be included, complementing the project's technical, financial, and implementation expertise.

259. By 2036, Böhringer’s investments will be on-risk client companies that have adopted sustainable production practices in their supply chains, with a focus on commodities such as palm oil, soybeans, cocoa, coffee, and rubber.

### Potential for scaling up and replication

261. The GCF Fund establishes a blueprint for financing inclusive, sustainable and deforestation-free commodity production through the development of an innovative business model that follows a land-use and development paradigm that protects valuable forests and peatlands and promotes high productivity agriculture.

259. The GCF Fund plans to scale up as follows:

- With the first 1 billion USD in GCF in Q3, 2023, the GCF Fund will then unlock 600 million in additional commitments from commercial investors.
- By December 2025, the Fund will meet its initial impact and targets (5 million hectares of forest conserved, 585,000 beneficiaries) and achieve a USD 400 million portfolio, with major climate change mitigation impacts.
- By December 2030, the Fund will meet its multi-year impact and achieve a USD 960 million portfolio, with significant biodiversity conservation.

### Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

262. The GCF Fund’s transactions stimulate innovation in agriculture business models to take the best growing practices from one jurisdiction and target jurisdictions or other countries and adapting those processes to the target company while incorporating environmental sustainability. Developing integrated supply chains that include producers, processors, traders, and consumers is a key element of this production process - a requirement that is increasingly becoming the standard for international commodity production. The GCF Fund’s transactions will also provide opportunities for producers and processors to form a community of practice among agri-producers and investors about what works, and how to replicate success. These replicable practices are a key element of the Fund's transformative impact.


---
**Page 65**
---

263. To ensure that the Fund’s desired outcome – to create blueprints for sustainable land use and management which others in the market can adopt, replicate and scale up significantly - the Fund reports publicly with high transparency on its progress towards this objective.
264. The first level of learning takes place within the landscapes relating to a particular investor, where there is a focus on strategic issues and critical challenges being faced, both at a project-level and jurisdictional-landscape level. This learning is captured in the Landscape Investment Framework (LIF) and represents the most effective and climate-resilient approaches to intensifying agricultural production. The results of these investments are measured against the Fund’s Key Performance Indicators (KPIs).
265. A critical element of this is the potential and executed transaction with respect to the environmental return and social impact inclusion generated. The Fund’s Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) include the generation of a net positive environmental return, including biodiversity conservation and ecosystem service sharing at the project level mainly benefits those upstream in the supply chain, namely the smallholders, and provides them with an income stream that is more stable than their current situation. This increases their climate-resilient production capacity, this knowledge sharing being the overall effect of improving the long-term sustainability of production and traceability of the supply chain.
266. The second level of learning takes place at a landscape level as well as the Fund itself, engage in an ongoing learning process. As deals are executed, the Fund’s Investment team will capture and share best practices with other investors to ensure that they can replicate these successes. This is captured within the LIF to create blueprint transactions – practices and approaches that can be replicated – therefore knowledge sharing at this level is critical.
### Creation of an enabling environment – through the ‘Green Fund’s Production-Protection-Inclusion approach:
267. The Green Fund takes an innovative Production-Protection-Inclusion approach. This is a multi-stakeholder approach, taken to secure inclusive, sustainable, deforestation-free production within a defined area or landscape. It involves collaboration between private sector companies, governments and national government, and civil society. This approach is enshrined in a binding and contractually agreed plan (WP), which sets out the terms of engagement for all parties involved.
268. Green Fund generates its impact within supply chains that are active in Landscapes where valuable ecosystems require active protection and/or restoration but face increasing pressure from agricultural production, such as palm oil, soybeans, rubber, cocoa, coffee, tea, and timber. The Fund supports sustainable use and management of ecosystem services in the Landscape. This way, Green Fund can connect with companies that are already serving more commonly supply chains.
### Contribution to regulatory policies and practices:
269. Green Fund advances its credit only on jurisdictions that have a progressive forest protection agenda and/or a credible deforestation-free commodity production plan (DCPP). A Green Fund Jurisdictional Evaluation Committee (JEC) conducts evaluations with respect to the environmental and social impacts generated by ensuring businesses are not contributing to deforestation or other negative environmental impacts. The criteria for that are conditions of loan contracts are aligned with IFC performance standards for each client; an LIF is developed with quantitative impact targets that describe and monitor performance. This ensures transparency regarding the Fund’s contribution towards maintaining landscapes that the biodiversity depends on.
270. All target Green Fund countries must have at least a minimum acceptable regulatory environment, or specific mechanisms to adequately value natural/forest assets. Advances made in regulatory and institutional frameworks are critical for ensuring that the long-term sustainability of sustainable commodity production remains in its infancy; as producers struggle to shift towards environmentally friendly practices, they face significant challenges in terms of access to finance, technology, and sustainable management of forests and biodiversity, but incentives for streamlining the participation of private sector businesses relating to forest restoration and sustainable commodity production are generally not in place.
271. On the other hand, targeted countries have financial systems with limited depth, are extremely risk-averse when it comes to financing projects that are outside their comfort zone, and there is a lack of capacity within the private sector to support more rapid development of these business models are still lacking. In general, the

## D

### Note (on potential risks) associated with these transactions, especially in the absence of proper capital raising, hinders credit and investment by private actors. In addition, support is needed to overcome barriers related to access to finance for the deployment and enhancement in technology development associated to the innovative nature of these businesses (see Section 8.2).

**272. The Fund offers financing that is structured to achieve the transformational changes sought. Credit terms can be tailored to meet the needs of borrowers, including flexible repayment schedules, interest-only periods, and extended grace and/or repayment periods tailored to the nature and needs of investments. Commodity producers and other businesses with a high degree of risk may require longer-term financing or support in implementing sustainable practices and for opportunity costs associated with forest conservation and other environmental protection measures.**

### 273. **Green Fund** imposes strict requirements on its clients regarding impact targets that must be delivered (see Section 8.1). The Fund also requires the implementation of Environmental and Social Management Systems (ESMS) that incorporates the necessary process, resources, and systems to ensure compliance with international standards. This makes it practical for investors to deliver and report these outcomes. GCF resources are necessary for deploying mechanisms that can attract private capital and can deliver climate benefits with the maximum leverage possible. These include building the knowledge and capacity within local governments, and linking finance and production value chain.

### Overview of national development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans:

**274. Despite governments (of the target countries) willingness to find ways to reduce deforestation and land degradation, they have not yet developed a comprehensive strategy for scaling up sustainable forest practices that contribute to addressing this issue, although this cannot be achieved by public efforts alone.**

#### 275. Private sector participation is key to produce a fundamental and long-lasting change, specifically via investing the capital needed to scale up sustainable forest management (SFM) activities. SFM can help countries contribute to climate-resilient development pathways. In all target countries, natural capital is substantial, and the development of SFM could provide an opportunity for these countries to generate economic benefits while increasing resilience to climate change. Yet, capital flows to these activities are minimal. Measures are needed to ensure that private sector actors can access finance at competitive rates and that they can scale up their activities at scale.

#### 276. All Green Fund target countries have demonstrated a commitment to reducing greenhouse gas emissions, adapting to the impacts of climate change and safeguarding their natural capital. These commitments are reflected in national policies (e.g., National Forest Strategies, National Biodiversity Strategies and Action Plans and National Adaptation Plans) and through being party to international agreements. The Green Fund strives to ensure that its investments contribute to these commitments by aligning with the countries’ strategies. The Green Fund have undertaken a detailed analysis of the climate policies of each target country and engaged with relevant stakeholders in each country. The Green Fund’s alignment with national climate policies is documented in Appendix 6, while an overview of how the Green Fund’s investment portfolio contributes to these target country documents is Annex 7.

#### 277. **Green Fund**’s investments contribute to transformational change as part of the existing GCF and MRV framework. For example, KPI 1 in the MRV framework requires reporting on contribution to systems change, scale and durability.

### 280.
&Green Fund's borrowers must adopt a "no-deforestation, no peat, no exploitation" policy as a condition of investment. In alignment with SDG 8, these provisions, and the technical assistance provided via Component 2, help to ensure that producers are not responsible for deforestation or other environmental degradation in their supply chains.

### 281.
The &Green Fund makes a major contribution to the achievement of SDG 12 in all participating countries. By promoting sustainable land management practices, the Fund engages large companies to adopt sustainable practices and integrate sustainability information into their reporting cycle.

### 282.
As described throughout this Funding Proposal, the &Green Fund will contribute to SDG 13 by delivering >339 million tCO2e in emissions reductions from the agriculture, forestry and land use sectors over the GCF investment period, while building climate resilience for agricultural producers.

### 283.
The Fund's investments will support SDG 15 by protecting, restoring, and promoting sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems. This includes efforts to reverse land degradation and biodiversity loss in tropical ecosystems threatened by unsustainable agricultural practices.

### 284.
&Green Fund does not quantitatively account biodiversity impacts as co-benefits from investments and forest protection activities. However, the reported number of hectares with increased ecosystem resilience can be considered a proxy for biodiversity improvement. The Fund has adopted best practice approaches to address potential negative impacts on biodiversity decline in biodiversity and has used best practice approaches in this regard. See Section 2.9 of Annex 2 – Feasibility Study for more detail on the Fund’s approach to biodiversity.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

## Gender-sensitive development benefit

### 5.1. The formative research conducted for Annex B (Gender Assessment and Action Plan) revealed that women are more than men in the GCF’s target countries—due to a number of gendered and social barriers and constraints when working in agricultural production and processing.

#### 5.2. These barriers include lack of access to productive resources such as land, credit, networks, infrastructure and education; limited time to take advantage of economic incentives (e.g., by participating in agricultural value chains); and the need to balance multiple roles and responsibilities for household tasks (e.g. child care, housework), also referred to as the unpaid care economy; discriminatory gender norms prevalent in many countries that limit women’s access to resources and opportunities; and limited access of women from having an equal voice in decision-making.

#### 5.3. Addressing these barriers is critical for achieving substantial environmental and social returns, also works on diminishing gender inequality through the invested portfolio. In principle, GCF Fund will tackle gender barriers by ensuring that all projects are designed to address gender equality and inclusion; providing access to financial services; drawing action plans specific to the local context with special attention to SEAH and GBV in communities where women’s participation is low; and promoting women’s economic empowerment and women’s empowerment. Please see Annex 8—Gender Analysis and Gender Action Plan for more detail.

#### 5.4. The GCF Fund will also address gender barriers by ensuring that the project portfolio reflects the vulnerabilities of other underrepresented groups based on gender, age, sexual orientation, race and other characteristics.

## Alignment with existing national strategies and GCF Country Programmes

### Detailed review of the 11 target countries concluded that the Fund is well-aligned with multiple strategies, including National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) of the target countries.

### The Fund has reviewed the GCF Programme for all target countries. With this review, the Fund aims to ensure alignment between its projects and programmes and nearly all of the CbCs for interventions that make agricultural production more climate resilient and reduce deforestation and forest degradation in each country.

### Glasgow Leaders' Declaration on Forests and Land Use: signed at COP26, highlights the need for increased ambition from countries to protect forests and other terrestrial ecosystems.

### The Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) has three main targets: biodiversity conservation, sustainable use of biodiversity and fair and equitable sharing of genetic resources. GCF Fund activities directly align with these targets by supporting projects that conserve and restore natural habitats and incorporating the principles and practices of the TNSF (Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures) bond.

### All countries are parties to the Nagoya Protocol and will support the main objectives of the protocol to prevent over-exploitation of genetic resources through this project by improving local smallholder livelihoods through sustainable forest management.

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 100 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Appropriate financial structure and minimal concessionality

348. The GCF will provide USD 180 million from GCF in the form of subordinate loans, and a further USD 9.35 million in grants to support programmematic activities over a 15-year timeframe. The total funding request is USD 189.35 million.

349. This level of concessionality is necessary because the project requires significant GCF intervention given the income-generating nature of the producers' activities. Grant financing comprises only 4% of total GCF funding, and is essential to overcome policy, technical and information barriers that prevent a rapid transition towards sustainable production practices.

350. GCF financing for Socking-AgGreenFund will be provided as a concessional loan, with a catco of GCF for the loan.

351. GCF capital is subordinate to commercial lenders in the timing of the rollovers and, in the way the cap-up is structured, it is expected that the first USD 20 million of the USD 9.35 million grant will be available from GCF's loan to AgGreenFund have been repaid. These resources will be available once again for GCFS use in funding other projects.

352. As noted in Section B.5, GCF’s loan of USD 180 million on top of the Fund’s existing USD 180 million would provide creditivity to the AgGreenFund business model: change perceptions regarding the risk-reward ratio of investment in sustainable production practices and increase the likelihood that private sector investors will invest.

353. Annex 2 shows that, without GCF support, the AgGreenFund is unable to crowd-in sufficient private sector investment to achieve its target scale of USD 1 billion over a 10-year period. With GCF’s concessional funding, the Fund is expected to achieve a maximum size of USD 350 million over the next 15 years, instead of the USD 1 billion below which it would be unable to crowd-in sufficient private sector investment.

354. It should also be noted that the AgGreenFund will not need more concessional financing at this stage to do so.

#### Leveraging cost-effective co-financing

308. The programme anticipates co-financing in the form of loans and equity from private and public investors within the project area, as well as from international development partners. The loan terms (e.g., maturity and the downside protection currently required by the private sector investor have all been incorporated into the financial model that underlies the funding proposal, based on discussions with prospective investors. In addition, the programme anticipates co-financing in the form of equity investment from private and public investors within the project area.

309. As a result of these assumptions, the total cost of the programme is USD 725 million over the lifetime of the project, but no more than 25% of the total project investment amount. The balance (75%) of the necessary investment will be provided by GCF and other public sector partners. Co-financing may come from commercial banks, equity funds, etc.) and international investors.

310. Project-level co-investments are NOT counted under the cost-effectiveness criteria for this proposal. However, it is expected that project-level co-investments will be made over time. Including the project-level co-investments would yield an effective co-financing ratio of at least 1:15.

311. The Ministry of Agriculture and the Accredited Entity have presented only the Fund-level co-financing in the GCF portfolio presentation.

#### Cost-effective programme impact

310. AgGreenFund is a cross-sectoral programme that reduces emissions from deforestation while making economic opportunities for producers more resilient to the impacts of climate change. GCF’s catalytic support is expected to net in GHG reductions of 329,15 million tonnes over the lifetime of the investment (see Section E – Long-term impact). The project will also provide direct benefits to communities and households that are expected to deliver direct climate resilience benefits to more than 1.9 million people and bring more than 15.7 million hectares under sustainable land management.

## Replicability

### Medium

- **A2Green Fund** has an existing portfolio of investments in agri-commodity producers that are vulnerable to deforestation in tropical countries. The portfolio is replicated by investing in companies with awareness of effective models for social inclusion, sustainable land management, and deforestation-free commodity production.
  
  While there are a number of quality-assured funds that have shifted to sustainable agricultural commodity production, it is critical to also ensure that the capital flowing to sustainable agriculture investments is linked to higher proportions of global financial flows than equity.

The Edénium Fund is an ecosystem-driven fund, and the 15- year investment horizon will allow catalytic additional investments through resources throughout the critical period of transition. It will provide resources for the sectors and jurisdictions that are most in need of GCF involvement. Edénium Fund will publish annual reports, and we will continue to engage with investors with regular updates including financial statements.

The GCF will invest in Stitching Funds (SF) to create a pool of capital that can get exposure to multiple projects. SFs were created for the purpose of mobilizing additional capital in tandem with GCF’s investment. It is designed to finance and scale up commercially viability projects, including large-scale sector players to achieve climate goals beyond business-as-usual growth.

In 15 years’ time (GCF investment period), it will be expected that public and private sector strategies will have been aligned towards the Paris Agreement targets. &Green Fund will have achieved significant deforestation in tropical forests along with supply chains. &Green Fund will have delivered a net positive economic and environmental impact with respect to climate change, financial planning and a funding mechanism that is robust and delivers absolute returns in line with private sector investor demands.

&Green Fund, at the time of GCF’s investment, will accelerate and scale deep decarbonization across the same landscapes, jurisdictions, and supply chains. It will catalyse money (i.e., fully funded or partially funded investments), acting as a multiplier and lever essentially alongside other investors and financial banks. ## Core indicator 1: Degree to which GCF investments in landscapes and market development information at the sub-national/local or national level

### Inquiries/Investment opportunities for low-emission development pathways at a landscape-level
- **Government policy and regulatory frameworks** in each country are critical for promoting protection of biodiversity, increased production and improved livelihoods.
- **Examples include**: the Brazilian Amazon (Brazil), where efforts to reduce deforestation transitions drive additional benefits across all elements; or the Congo Basin moratorium on the intensification of agriculture within the agricultural sector, nor do they consider the potential tax benefits and economic opportunities from such a move. This lack of a robust regulatory environment creates the potential for land use conflicts in situations where land use plans and tenure plans are not transparent or well-defined.
- **Very few investors have been able to identify the sustainable, long-term business models of these sectors** and GCF/World Bank has been required to act as the lead investor when it has worked on the. The lack of a clear pathway for mainstream investors to finance the financing the development of pathways that these investments will take.
- **GCF funding can help AGRiVent Fund develop and strengthen market information chains** for tropical agri-commodities, including NDFP certification, which have an equivalent or greater value than legacy production methods.

### Multi-stakeholder
- **Large-scale land acquisitions** by foreign investors are a significant barrier to the development of commercially viable investments that can be scaled up.
- **GCF aims fund to deepen the capital markets for agricultural commodities. This will be achieved by developing a better understanding of and more efficient agricultural models. This will enable financial institutions such as GCF to be able and willing to provide finance to sustainable agri-businesses into the future**.

E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**Programme level**

313. FMO is responsible for regular reporting to GCF. An annual performance report (APR) will be submitted to the GCF following reporting requirements under the AMA and the FAA. The APR will be prepared by @Green Fund according to the following:

a) summarise the financial performance of the @Green Fund loan facility according to audited and unaudited financial statements. Two key indicators - Outcome 1.2 “Amount in USD mobilized for climate action” and Outcome 3.4 “Amount in USD disbursed to investee companies”- will include information from audits and financial accounts as a reference point, but the APR will also provide additional context on how these amounts are reported to GCF in any case, and it is appropriate to report the same figures as a measure of verification in the annual performance report. The audited financial statements will be submitted with the balance sheet, and the audited financial statements serve as the definitive report for the Board and investors.

b) support the TA/F project supported by IDH (see Annex 7.14 to FA-TA Facility Operating Guidelines)[28], and

c) collect from project owners the disaggregated project programme specific performance indicators outlined in Section E.3 above.

The APR will be submitted to the Secretariat for the period ending on 31 December within 60 days after the end of the relevant annual period. The first APR will be submitted following the end of the calendar year in which the Programme is established, and thereafter annually at the end of each calendar year or the relevant reporting year. The implementation reporting period of the Programme will start from the date of effectiveness of the agreement.

315. In addition to the annual performance report, an inception report, mid-term evaluation report and terminal evaluation report will be submitted to the GCF, as described in Annex 11. The methodological approach for reporting on the KPIs is outlined in Annexes A6, A7, A8 and A9. These annexes provide a detailed Framework (Annex A, Appendix 6), KPI Method (Annex B, Appendix 4) and MGR Guidance Note (Annex C, Appendix 5). The KPIs are designed to be consistent with the GCF’s Strategic Outcome Indicators and specific methodology described for each KPI. The KPIs are then added to create portfolio level statements for Reporting on Climate Change Outcomes (RCCO) as outlined in Annex A9. These annexes provide a detailed Framework (Annex A, Appendix 6), KPI Method (Annex B, Appendix 4) and MGR Guidance Note (Annex C, Appendix 5). The KPIs are designed to be consistent with the GCF’s Strategic Outcome Indicators and specific data that will allow @Green Fund and FMO to report against the programme specific indicator outlined in Section E.3 above. In addition, the annexes provide a detailed Framework (Annex A, Appendix 6), KPI Method (Annex B, Appendix 4) and MGR Guidance Note (Annex C, Appendix 5). | Category | Probability | Impact |

Description

The Fund's inability to meet perceived expectations of its stakeholders in terms of promoting its development objectives and high standards of integrity and social and environmental sustainability.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The Fund implements a comprehensive compliance framework for its service providers and for its borrowers, before and during the investment. The ESMR of GCF Fund explicitly requires its investments to use best practice certification or equivalent (e.g., for timber FSC would be the baseline for GCF Fund). The reason for this is the recognition of the value of certification for managing social and environmental risks. In addition, the Fund has a policy that it will only invest in projects where the borrower may also apply more localised standards, e.g., a Colombia GCF Fund's investment HQ is the very first project to apply the ESMR. The Fund also requires its service providers to have a good reputation and to be able to demonstrate their ability to manage reputational risks. The Fund requires the go over and above certification in its requirements. The Fund applies consistently across all investments a policy that it will only invest where there is a clear link between the project and the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), LPPs, (LPPs) who, where missed, trigger a loan Event of Default, and (a) company-wide NCP, compliance. The LPP in particular requires that the borrower has a good reputation and can demonstrate its ability to manage reputational risks.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 92

## Currency Risk
The value of financial commitments and instruments issued in foreign currencies (other than the Fund's reporting currency, USD) fluctuates due to changes in foreign exchange rates.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- The Fund will hedge all material currency risk, based on pricing. Where a borrower in a target country can hedge the transaction efficiently, the Fund will include it in the price charged to the client. When international borrowers have experience in hedging, they will be encouraged to do so. Otherwise, the Fund will enter into USD, especially at an 80/20 ratio of USD to total currency (fully hedged) over the entire portfolio at maturity.

## Selected Risk Factor B
### Category: Medium | Probability: Medium | Impact: Medium

### Description
Market Liquidity Risk: The Fund may invest in local debt instruments which may not be traded on a liquid exchange or market that provides regulatory authority over borrowers and/or collateral.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- The Fund's general policy is to hold debt maturities until maturity, it does not trade marketable securities.

## Selected Risk Factor 9
### Category: Technical and operational | Probability: Medium | Impact: Medium

### Description
Operational risk: Due to the relatively early-stage of the Fund, there is a risk that the Fund will not achieve its investment objectives & since lending criteria could constitute dead flow. The Fund is also exposed to operational and compliance risks stemming from the processes, people & systems of the Fund's service provider.

## Selected Risk Factor 10
### Category: Other | Probability: Medium | Impact: High

### Description
Emissions risk - project fails to achieve its GHG target because either:
- Additional emissions associated with implementation (e.g. increased energy use).
- GHG emissions associated with intensification (e.g. greater mechanization, more intensive use of fertilizers and other chemicals). These additional emissions may partially offset the GHG benefits of reduced extensification and carbon sequestration in protected forests.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- The Fund will typically engage in direct lending. However, where local capital markets (which tend to be less liquid) are used to acquire a debt position in a company, the Fund will always buy-to-hold until maturity rather than trade.

IDH assesses developmental and financial additionality of Technical Assistance, independently from IDGF Fund. This means that:

- TA is designed to adequately support the project or investment or project in scope, beyond what could be financed by the client itself or should be financed by the Investment fund manager as part of their regular investment process.

| Category | Probability | Impact |

### Description

Risk that the Fund is misused for money laundering (ML) and terrorist financing (TF)/MLT/F or any other financial misconduct or crime.

### Mitigation Measures:

The Fund has an AML/CFT Policy and a dedicated AML/CFT Compliance Officer aiming to ensure that ML/FT risks in the process of fundraising for, and investment by, the Fund are identified, assessed, mitigated and reported in order to prevent the Fund and its Customers, governing bodies and service providers from being misused for ML/FT or any other financial misconduct or crime.

The Fund is only allowed to invest in countries included in the DAC List of ODA Recipients, who also qualify against the criteria set out by the Fund. The Fund’s AML/CFT Policy requires that all investments must be registered and can formally be built into the JEC monitoring process if required. No individual or entity that is listed on any UN Security Council sanctions list may be invested in directly or indirectly; nor shall it be involved in any manner with its project or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer, or beneficiary.

Very limited funding has been mobilized overall from private sector investors to date into the new paradigm of blended finance. The current structure of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the way in which the GCF Fund is exactly to change that: the proposed programme has been designed and operates in a way that specifically caters for revenue streams, by providing solid returns in the form of coupons that are competitive.

Project investments are structured as coupon bonds issued by the GCF. The coupon structure reflects the intention that the Tranche A investors (including GCF) provide, which changes the risk adjusted return perception of the targeted private sector investor and provides a strong rationale for buying into the bonds that establish investor confidence in the space through a blended finance approach.

The coupons offered to investors (including Tranche A investors via the catch up) will depend on the exact timing of the drawdowns. The coupon structure is designed so as not to be competitive with the interest rates offered by private sector investor in this debt fund, and should not be compared to double-digit returns expected from private equity investments or other alternative investment vehicles.

On the asset side, @Green does not provide loans to its investors (borrowers) that are concessionary in the pricing. The coupon structure is designed so as not to be competitive with the interest rates offered by private sector investor in this debt fund and should not be compared to double-digit returns expected from private equity investments or other alternative investment vehicles.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 317. Environmental and social risk classification: The proposed programme is expected to have positive environmental and social impacts as it delinks deforestation from commodity supply chains and builds climate resilience in the region. It will also support countries in implementing their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) by enabling them to meet their commitments under the Paris Agreement, including by supporting commodity producers to commit to “No Deforestation, No Peatlands, No Exploitation” (NDPE) practices. Despite the expected positive impacts, there are risks associated with the programme that need to be addressed in line with the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards requirements (Category 1-3).

### 318. Environmental and Social Management System: The GCF Green Fund is committed to integrating environmental and social safeguards into its operations through a robust Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) throughout the programme. This is operationalised at the investment level through environmental and social action plans (ESAPs). These ESAPs are developed in consultation with stakeholders, taking into account scale of the activities and the magnitude of environmental and social risks and impacts associated with each investment. The GCF Green Fund has adopted a set of best practices for Environmental and Social Management that includes the application of the IFC Performance Standards and best practices of ESG management for funds, such as those provided by CDC Group, FMO, the GCF and IUCN. For efficiency reasons, the ESMS is not described in detail here.

### 319. Framework approach for risk mitigation: The framework of the ESMS includes the following sets of tools used to establish appropriate Environmental and Social Action Plans (ESAPs) for each investment:

- Policy and regulatory compliance
- No Deforestation, No Peatlands, No Exploitation (NDPE)
- Landscape Conservation Planning (LCP)
- Financial Sector Assessment Program (FSAP)
- Key Performance Indicator (KPI) Framework
- Terrestrial

### 320. GCF Green Fund’s approach is determined by applying best practice criteria and requirements based on identified risks to investments during due diligence. The following categories are subject to screening and due diligence, with the aim of mitigating or avoiding adverse impacts:

- Environmental issues: deforestation, peatlands degradation, biodiversity loss
- Social issues: gender and labour-related issues of investors, communities and social conflicts, displacement, human rights, land tenure, indigenous peoples’ rights, access to water, health, education, communities, Labour issues, Child/Slave work, gender issues (including SEAR)
- In case of any material findings, the mitigation measures will be developed in consultation with stakeholders. If no effective mitigation is found for focused due diligence is undertaken, or an action plan to address the risks elaborated and implemented. Further details on how these are addressed can be found in the GCF Green Fund’s Environmental and Social Management Policy (Annex 6).

### 321. Stakeholder engagement: The GCF Green Fund has undertaken a thorough stakeholder engagement process at programme level and requires stakeholder engagement for each sub-project investment. SAIL Ventures maintains a Communication, Engagement and Consultation Plan (CECP) that is updated on an annual basis.

- One member of the GCF Board, rather than SAIL Ventures, is dedicated to monitoring and managing conflicts.
- SAIL Ventures has resources in Southeast Asia and in Latin America and additional in-country resources will be built up as required. The GCF Green Fund’s Environmental and Social Management Policy (Annex 6) requires an effective grievance mechanism is required of investors as part of their due diligence process, which is also covered by the IFC Performance Standards.
- An effective grievance mechanism is required of investors as part of their due diligence as per IFC PS requirements. The stakeholder engagement process is fully integrated in the investment process.

### 323. In order to enable Transformational Change, maximize positive impacts and mitigate E&S risks, GCF Green Fund employs forms of local stakeholder engagement throughout the investment management process. When assessing the transaction, the GCF Green Fund will consider how the project may impact communities or other stakeholders who may potentially relevant stakeholders. These stakeholders may enable the transformational change in the landscape and can be engaged at different stages during the transaction lifecycle. They may include local farmer’s associations, non-profits, governmental bodies, research institutes etc.

### 324. Impact assessment: The GCF Green Fund assesses and manages social and environmental impacts of its investments. The ESMS system operates on two levels: jurisdictional and transactional – and a NUL

## INTEGRATION OF THE GENDER ASSESSMENT INTO THE INVESTMENT PROCESS

The following sections describe each step in the investment process in more detail:

- **The Transformational Change Assessment Phase**, described in an Investment Rationale.
- **The Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF)**, which includes:
  - Defining the Scope: ESMF Risk Screening (including Reproductive Screening, Forest & Biodiversity Screening and Gender Sensitivity Screening).
  - The LPP Design, which is documented in the Credit Application and contains due diligence (EDSA), ESMP, and other requirements.
- **The ESMF screening phase** aims to identify main material risks. In addition to the identified stakeholders, a Green Screen filters those actors that should be considered both in due diligence and LPP design phases from the risk perspective.

## DUE DILIGENCE SCREENING

324. The due diligence scope is defined by the screening results, but depending on the severity of findings, a Green Fund may consult some local actors (e.g., research institutions, civil rights organizations, certification bodies and similar entities) to provide additional information or analysis. This will be done in accordance with the requirements for necessary stakeholder engagement to investigate, provide dimension and mitigation options for the ESMF risks identified.

325. The Green Fund may also engage with other actors (e.g., national governments, international organizations, NGOs, Forest and Biodiversity Frameworks) to provide additional information or analysis on the ESMF risks identified. This will be done in accordance with the requirements for necessary stakeholder engagement by client in future LPPs.

326. The due diligence screening phase is followed by a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page).

## GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN

### OBJECTIVES OF THE GENDER ASSESSMENT

327. Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan informs the programme’s design as presented in this Funding Proposal. The overall objective of the gender assessment is to provide a baseline understanding of the community-level impacts of climate change on women, men, boys and girls. This will be done through data collection, analysis and mitigation SEAH risks.

328. Performance Standard 2: paragraph 15; on Non-discrimination and Equal Opportunity, therefore committing to take measures to ensure that no one is discriminated against or excluded from participating in the project.

329. Performance Standard 4: paragraph 1: committing to avoid or minimize the risks and impacts to community health and safety, and security that may arise from project-related activities, with particular reference to vulnerable groups.

### GENDER ASSESSMENT METHODOLOGY

330. The gender assessment is conducted by a Gender Assessment Team (GAT) comprising SEAH-relevant experts in the countries (via country-specific gender profiles), the context and sociocultural factors underlying gender inequalities, and the specific impacts of climate change on women, men, boys and girls.

331. The GAT will assess the vulnerability of women, men, boys and girls to climate change across all sectors and age groups to build both individual and collective resilience to climate change. This assessment will be used to inform project design and implementation in order to ensure that the needs and priorities of women, men, boys and girls are addressed.

332. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been designed to ensure, through designated actions, that the challenges faced by women, men, boys and girls as a result of climate change, including those related to adaptation and mitigation efforts in line with the GCF, are meaningfully addressed. The GAP describes gender-related activities, baselines, sex-disaggregated indicators, and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework.

333. The Green Fund recognizes that gender mainstreaming is both necessary and relevant for the programme to achieve its objectives. Gender mainstreaming is a process of integrating gender equality considerations into all aspects of policy development and implementation.

# Gender Approach and the operationalization of gender mainstreaming within its institutional management structure (see Section 3 – Annex B). Embedding gender into differentiation commitments offers considerable opportunities for leveraging synergies between restoration goals, climate change adaptation as well as all other SDGs.

## Key Issues Identified in the Annex A

The following key issues are identified:

1. **Gender Inequality**: Gender inequality is inscribed into the social, cultural, historical and political contexts of communities where forest management efforts take place.
2. **Biophysical Limitations**: Women’s access to land and other natural resources (particularly export-facing ones) require upgrading based on the typology of the women in question. While access to land and other natural resources is a key determinant for women’s livelihoods, it also determines their needs or providing intensified services coupled with prompt payment for their produce to allow them to produce more.
3. **Institutionalization**: The institutionalization of gender-sensitive policies in the governance of production groups to enable them to upgrade their access to land and other natural resources is a key determinant for women’s livelihoods.

## Annex B

### Informal Economies and Markets are the dominant source of livelihoods in rural areas – and the engagement with these markets is often crucial for women’s livelihoods and the well-being of their households (IEO, 2015)17

Women’s access to physical security concerns limit their access and use of forest resources and related activities. (CFI, 2017)

See Annex B, Section 2.3 for more information.

### Funded Projects and the Gender Approach

The GEF Fund’s Gender Approach will provide a framework to accelerate gender-transformative and gender-responsive programming in projects funded through the GEF6 Fund structure.

The Gender Approach will be mobilized through the Environment and Social Action Plans (ESAPs) – which are instruments for environmental and social safeguard conditions that are conditions of the loan contracts of the GEF Fund. The ESAPs will also influence the design of project-level gender strategies, which will be defined in the context of each project’s gender strategy, which will be detailed at the project level stage, as reflected in the Gender Action Plan.

## Gender mainstreaming through the TAF

The GEF TAF was established in 2013 through a Cooperation Agreement with IDH and aims to reduce deforestation by increasing the impact of the GEF6 Fund (potential) investment projects, by supporting investment readiness, monitoring, post-investment client support and sharing of lessons and knowledge from GEF6 Fund and its investments.

IDH has identified that equitable and meaningful participation of women as key to driving sustainable forest management. In order to address this concern, IDH has developed a Gender Toolkit, consisting of practical case examples from IDH and similar project experiences in the field. The toolkit is designed to provide guidance on how to integrate gender into programming steps of projects and investments are explored.

The GEF Fund’s Gender Approach will employ these recommendations.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP212

## Proposal name: &Green Fund: Investing in Inclusive Agriculture and Protecting Forests

### Accredited entity: Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMG)

### Project/programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview
- This is the second submission of the full proposal, "Green Fund: Investing in Inclusive Agriculture and Protecting Forests," to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP).
- The accredited entity (AE) is the Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMG).
- This full proposal aims at contributing to low-carbon, climate-resilient development pathways by lowering investors' risks while promoting zero-deforestation, socially inclusive agriculture.
- The ITAP considered its assessment of all documents as submitted on 18 April 2023, the assessment report was received on 16 May 2023 and discussed at the Q&A session on 17 May 2023 and agreed to by FMG. It was re-submitted by ITAP on 25 May 2023 as a result of minor revisions agreed between ITAP and AE after the Q&A session.
- The ITAP has reviewed all documents submitted with this revised version, the full proposal. The ITAP acknowledges the constructive response from the AE to the comments made in its assessment report following the first submission.

#### 1.2 Context
- Commodity production is an important driver of deforestation on its trips.
- This is true for several commodities including palm oil, soybeans and beef.
- The funding proposal is aimed at making possible to scale up an existing investment facility (&Green Fund), which aims to transform the most problematic commodity supply chains (e.g. palm oil, cocoa, rubber, cotton).

#### 1.3 FMG as the AE
- FMG, as the AE, requests GCF support to transform major tropical commodity supply chains in a manner that is economically inclusive in a way that is commercially viable and replicable at scale.
- This paradigm shift assumes that (a) sustainable intensification of commodity production can be achieved while maintaining or increasing socioeconomic and adaptation co-benefits; and (b) the willingness to invest in sustainable intensification is driven by private investors, who are increasingly aware of how private investors perceive its financial risks.
- The &Green Fund will develop blueprints for sustainable commodity supply chains that other players can replicate and/or scale up in future.

#### 1.4 Today's barriers
- Several barriers make it difficult to put sustainable commodity supply chains in place:
    - Lack of access to finance by smallholder farmers;
    - Lack of knowledge and technology by private investors;
    - Market distortions that undervalue standing forests and induce deforestation; (c) limited local access to knowledge and technology for no-deforestation.


---
**Page 125**
---

The Green Climate Fund seeks to overcome these barriers by making deforestation-free commodities a market reality and providing a profitable and viable alternative setting the stage to drive sustainable land use investment at scale. The GCF Investment Facility should reduce private sector transaction costs and risks associated with supply chain traceability, improving existing conditions and capacities in the given areas (countries or subnational entities).

The funding proposal covers Brazil, Cameroon, Colombia, Côte d'Ivoire, Democratic Republic of the Congo, Ecuador, Gabon, Indonesia, La République Démocratique du Congo, Liberia, Madagascar, Malaysia, Mexico, Peru, Papua New Guinea, and Vietnam. The specific commodity supply chains currently identified as drivers of deforestation in the specific areas (countries or subnational entities) are: beef, soybeans, palm oil, rubber, cocoa, coffee, and timber.

The funding proposal is structured around two components: (1) the G4G Lending Facility; and (2) the Technical Assistance Facility (TAF). The G4G Lending Facility (component 1) aims to address barriers (a) to (c) by (i) mobilizing resources for the creation of a scalable investment vehicle (IV); (ii) the mobilization of co-investment by the private sector; and (iii) the development of a transaction framework for commodity transactions. The TAF (component 2) will address barriers (b) to (e) and aims to result in policy dialogue and awards ranging: technical cooperation for capacity-building of companies, governments, and civil society organizations; and an independent verification system that allows for paradigm shift tracking.

It is expected that the AE will contribute to GCF’s results areas on climate mitigation and adaptation impacts of the programme. Based on results from investments over the past four years, FMO, the AE, invests an expectation lifetime impact of approximately 350,000 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) per year, with a total investment cost of approximately USD 1 million invested. This results from emissions reduction from avoided deforestation in Kenya and Indonesia, as well as avoided deforestation in Brazil based on satellite data and national forest reference emission levels/forest reference levels (FRELs). The AE expects that the programme will contribute to GCF’s climate mitigation impact lifetime (15 years) will result in a contribution to climate change mitigation of circa 340 million tonnes CO2e.

Further, the AE expects to contribute to the GCF results areas on health and well-being, food and water security, and ecosystems and ecosystem services. Based on G4G Fund’s experience with the GCF’s investment framework, the programme is expected to yield the following adaptation metrics:

(a) Over 15.7 million hectares of land under sustainable and resilient management; and

(b) At minimum 230,000 people with enhanced resilience per USD 1 million invested (a USD 960 million investment would result in a 184,000 person benefit).

## 1.2 Impact Scale: High

The GCF assessment is guided by decision B.34/12, which explains the principles for demonstrating the climate potential of GCF-supported activities, as well as other internal and assessment guidance for this investment criteria and as approved by the GCF Board.

### Mitigation potential

The proposed contribution to the climate change mitigation results area on forestry and land use of 50 Mt CO₂ eq over the investment lifetime (15 years). This is an indicative figure. Detailed calculations will be done for each jurisdiction (i.e., for each transaction) in order to determine the actual mitigation contribution.

The funding proposal presents a comprehensive methodological approach. It describes three types of mitigation contributions: (1) avoided deforestation in forests protected by GCF incentives; (2) avoided deforestation in forests on supply sheds (landscapes) of GCF influence; and (3) forest restoration in forests protected by GCF incentives. Further, it also includes the contribution from increased carbon sequestration through land management activities. This allows identifying relevant, project-related emissions from intensification of agricultural production while reducing incentives and demand for deforestation.

For all above-mentioned types of mitigation contributions as well as for assessing potential co-benefits related to forest management intensification, the funding proposal includes a corresponding quantification approach. For each quantification approach, the GCF Fund will incentivize the avoidance of deforestation by applying actions for GCF incentives.

That said, the mitigation contribution to the forestry and land use area mentioned in paragraph 14 above only includes contribution type (2) [avoided deforestation in forests protected by GCF incentives]. The other two mitigation types are quantified in order to understand their potential co-benefits related to forest management intensification. This increases the conservativeness of the overall quantification approach.

The GCF Fund will continue working in sectors that are known as drivers of deforestation, such as agriculture and supply chains. In particular, the funding proposal and supply chain-specific level at the deforestation frontier. National FRLs/FRLA will be assessed across jurisdictions to determine the extent to which they are exposed or highly exposed to agri-commodity expansion. Thus, far as the programme implementers have full control over the implementation of GCF incentives in supply chains, the application of FRL/FRLA approaches by GCF in at-risk supply chains can be considered as conservative.

The final proposal states that the GCF Fund will incentivize the avoidance of deforestation through:

(a) Protection of identified forest areas (and avoidance of deforestation) as part of conservation activities;

(b) Intensification of agricultural production while reducing incentives and demand for deforestation, thereby avoiding deforestation. While the specific number of hectares of avoided deforestation cannot be estimated with certainty, the overall impact can nonetheless be estimated.


---
**Page 127**
---

20. The financial proposal includes a quantification method for avoided deforestation (declarations) and emissions reductions (type 1):
(a) From FRLs, the deforestation rate in percentage (% per year) for the relevant biome is extracted from the FRL deforestation rate in proportion to the areas of relevant forests.
(b) The deforestation rate for the reporting year (e.g. 2021) in relevant jurisdictions per transaction unit (e.g. ha per year) is established using satellite imagery.
(c) FRL and satellite-derived deforestation rates are compared and a demonstrably conservative value (% per year) is selected.
(d) The % deforestation rate is applied to GCF's "forest protected" areas to estimate avoided deforestation emissions.
(e) The area of avoided deforestation is multiplied by the emission factors from the FRLs to quantify emission reductions.
(f) The transaction unit is reported annually and reported annually.

FRLs are extracted from relevant FREL/FRLs from national governments for appropriate forest types or biomes, as applicable. The deforestation rate is established using satellite data conservatively established from Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) factors appropriately adjusted to account for the fraction of carbon and biomass fraction in accordance with the approaches used in FREL and national GHG accounting.

### Adaptation potential

During the first assessment of this full proposal, ITAP recommended the development of a methodological approach for assessing adaptation potential at the project level. In the second submission, g4Green Fund has proposed such an approach that includes a climate risk assessment and the identification, planning, implementation, monitoring, and reporting and verification framework.

In order to mainstream adaptation to climate change in its operations, the g4Green Fund will follow a climate risk management process incorporated into the screening, due diligence and loan approval processes of the Accession.

The process proposed by g4Green Fund includes the following eight steps:

1. **Define scope of assessment and economic activity (as of lending scope), including investment time horizon (5 years for the initial phase) and financial instruments included in the loan and time span (30 years as default for the investment and up to 20 years for the loan).
2. **Determine relevant lifespans of the components of the economic activity, differentiating between investments with relatively short asset lives (<10 years) that will be affected by the current climate change and those with longer asset lives (≥10 years) that may be affected by the current and future climate.
3. **Identify relevant climate risks (e.g., extreme weather events, services, functions), which are material to the relevant economic activities. These will include areas of agricultural land with intercropping systems or other forms of mixed farming, as well as livestock associated with these lands. In this step it will be assessed where there is dependence on rainfall and/or irrigation for water supply (agricultural climate risk assessment) and procurement (procurement climate risk assessment). Similarly, when transportation is relevant for the economic activity, the impact of extreme weather events or other hazards will also be included as part of the climate risk assessment whenever alternative routes are not viable.
4. **Screen climate hazards: Identify relevant hazards from prescribed lists, which are based on the European Union taxonomy? This screening step will allow, for each
