1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The funding proposal presents a good overview of the drivers of deforestation in the selected countries: Brazil, Indonesia, Cameroon, Colombia, Côte d'Ivoire, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Equatorial Guinea, Gabon, Ghana, Liberia, Madagascar and Zambia.

Further, it draws on scientific outputs regarding climate risks, impacts, adaptation and mitigation interventions and benefits related to seven commodities: palm oil, livestock farming/cattle, soy, rubber, coffee, cocoa and timber. The scientific references combine meta-analyses of the literature with case studies from tropical countries as well as to other countries in the tropics.

This information, combined with the general barriers presented in the funding proposal, delineates a clear commodity supply chain as a main driver of deforestation in the tropics and why it is so important to involve the private sector in a transformative process.

The funding proposal assumes that lowering the investment risks for private investors can contribute to the transformation of the commodities supply chain and, consistent with this assumption, proposes an investment strategy focused on developing deforestation-free commodity production that is commercially viable and replicable.

The starting point for the investments is an assessment at the national or subnational level aimed at identifying promising opportunities in the agricultural sector, which allow for the identification of promising opportunities at the jurisdictional level.

This Green eligibility assessment uses the following criteria:


---
**Page 131**
---

## (e) Scope: The amount and quality of forest/peatland potential is significant and highly relevant from a global perspective;

### (b) Ambition and strategy: There is a clear strategy with the targets tracking the reduction of deforestation against historic trends in the jurisdiction. Continued jurisdictional eligibility is dependent on subsequent biennial positive assessments of progress.

### (c) Two-year cycle: The GCF Fund will assess the trajectory of the jurisdiction on a trajectory towards the targets for reducing deforestation;

### (d) A transparent MRV system is operational and able to monitor, measure, report and verify forest cover information and, where relevant, forest and peat degradation in the jurisdiction; and

### (e) Social and environmental safeguards: Progress shows towards the implementation of the Green Climate Fund’s social and environmental safeguard framework.

60. This overall approach for identification, selection and investment support has been used by G4Green Fund since its creation in 2017. Currently, G4Green Fund has seven transactions in its pipeline with a total committed amount of USD 35 million. The current portfolio investments account for USD 142.75 million.

61. Currently, G4Green Fund manages around USD 180 million, with key investors including Norway’s International Climate and Forest Initiative (NICFI) FMO. Unilever, the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland (through the FMO-managed Mobilising Finance for Zero Deforestation project), and the World Bank Group through its Environment Hannover Re.

62. The proponents consider that co-investment from the private sector will be triggered by FMO funding and will be secured from commitments made by potential investors to scale capital disbursements to borrower companies. For the funding proposal, the overall amount of USD 750 million is expected to be mobilized through a combination of direct co-funding of over USD 750 million. This growth in co-funding will indeed mark success in terms of scaling up private sector investment and demonstrating market impact.

63. The approach that links continuing GCF support to demonstrated mobilization impacts is proper and aligns GCF program shifts into the actual execution of the G4Green Fund.

64. The repeatability and scalability of the blueprints developed by G4Green Fund depend upon the ability to scale up co-investment in a manner that can be applied across commodities and in new countries and the level of success in mobilizing co-funding, especially from private sector investors. The potential for scaling up co-financing for loans vary significantly among the 11 counties included is the proposal, and mobilizing private investments in zero-deforestation, resilient and inclusive agriculture has proven to be challenging.

65. A mitigating uncertainty on this funding proposal’s overall potential for paradigm change in which it can contribute to a more sustainable approach to land use, landscape approach agriculture and supply chain finance. The transactions in the current G4Green Fund portfolio are with major agricultural market players who typically do not struggle to access alternative sources of financing. However, there is no guarantee that these entities would lead these entities towards more sustainable practices, but it is not clear whether this is a result of their lack of interest or the absence of an enabling environment for finance. Conversely, there may be a risk that other commercial sources of finance would begin to compete on financing terms without additional sustainability obligations being included.

- Goal 12 (Responsible consumption and production) through the transformation of commodity value chains, particularly for smallholder farmers producing agricultural commodities:

1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

With total financing of USD 981,620,000, this funding proposal expects to have a significant impact on forest conservation in Indonesia. The expected return on investment corresponds to less than USD 1/0.3 ha eq., which is considered an efficient ratio. However, the actual figure may be lower due to the inclusion of costs for project preparation and implementation.

The expected ratio is a USD 3.75 invested by the private sector for each USD 1 invested by GCF.

GCF and the AE PMO will contractually agree that GCF will commit USD 189,335 million to the AGreen Stitching® for the funding activity. GCF will be an indirect lender to the AGreen Stitching® through FMO.

Of the total amount from GCF, USD 750,000 will be provided to AGreen Stitching as a non-reimbursable grant for component 1 (funding facility) and outputs 1.1 and 1.2, and USD 864 million will be provided in the form of a non-reimbursable grant for component 3 (investment facility). For component 2 (FAF), USD 864 million will be provided as a non-reimbursable grant.

FMO will make a USD 180.75 million capital commitment to AGreen Stitching in the form of a non-reimbursable grant and a subordinated loan. The Stitching can then remit the full USD 180.75 million to the project company as required by the funding agreement. The “IV” is tranches A-equity—in the form of the current portfolio assets, cash, and undrawn commitments—and B-loan—the latter in the form of the loan request—“for loans to project companies.”

The funding proposal suggests three GCF disbursements to FMO (4.67 per cent, 27.66 per cent, and 68.30 per cent), subject to conditions stated on the financial agreement.

FMO will likewise make a USD 8.6 million capital commitment in the form of a non-reimbursable grant to AGreen Fund for implementing some or all component 2’s activities under the FTA Cooperate Agreement with GCF, via the technical assistance service provider.

In addition, AGreen Stitching will be advised to invest with and/or via a Dutch private company with limited liability (B.V.) under the laws of the Netherlands. The AGreen Stitching will provide equity capital to the B.V., and will be the only equity capital provided by GCF to the B.V. The B.V. will be an investment asset management vehicle, the form of which is the Dutch B.V.

Stitching is the Dutch term for a financial instrument. AGreen Fund will use the available funds from the GCF commitment fund and the available investment vehicle suitable for commercial investments. Stitching and/or funding (the Stitching is the existing legal entity through which the project company will be funded) will be established as an investment vehicle by the Stitching. Available investment vehicles are the financial instruments that the Stitching will establish under clause 1.5 to attract commercial interest, the form of which is the Dutch B.V.

GCF proceeds will be drawn down by the GCF AGreen Fund Stitching and then, for the amounts concerning output 1.3, provided directly to the project company. The Stitching will provide equity capital in exchange for a shareholding in the project company equal to the contribution of additional shares (in the form of share premium). Its further charter is shares will be issued for cash or loans on the capital and will be provided as additional share premium on the charter if shares issued upon incorporation.

# Scalable Investment Vehicle and Technical Assistance Facility Structure

## Figure 2: Scalable investment vehicle and Technical Assistance Facility structure

| Source: Funding proposal |
| --- |

According to the funding proposal, the GCF investment will enable €400m Fund to attract commercial investors at scale to this new complementary scalable investment vehicle (G4een B.V.) because the concessional capital can provide risk coverage for the private sector.

The complementary scalable investment vehicle (G4een B.V.) will issue equity (tranche A) to the Stichting in exchange for the existing funds (in the form of grants, redeemable grants and loans) held by the Stichting. The equity issued by G4een B.V. will be a portfolio transfer. Moreover, G4een will raise additional funds through the issue of debt instruments (tranche B) to finance its investments.

GCF will disburse its subordinated loan to the Stichting, through the AE (FMO), and will therefore be part of tranche A investors and governance.

The Stichting will hold a senior equity position in the structure of the G4een B.V. that is junior to the tranche B debt instruments held by private sector investors and holders of reflows.

Tranche A will act as the principal catalyst to attract commercial capital into the structure by providing a first-loss buffer to further private sector investors in G4een B.V.

The catalytic investors will rank within the Stichting as follows (from junior to senior): (i) FMO subordinated loan; (ii) GCF and REIS MFF concessional loans; (iii) GCF and REIS MFF redeemable grants; (iv) GCF and REIS MFF concessional loans); and (v) FMO and Reemser.

Private sector investors (tranche B) will have a senior position in the G4een B.V. as they will be issued debt instruments (notes) with specific terms. The notes target private sector investors seeking commercial returns at acceptable risk levels.

# Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings

## Proposal name: AgriFonds Investing in Agriculture and Protecting Forests

Accredited entity: Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FM0)

### Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### Impact potential
**Scale:** High

AE answers to TAP comments:

Thank you for your feedback, we agree that the impact potential of the AgriFonds is high. The feedback provided by ITAP in our first submission enabled us to revise existing and create additional documentation focused on Operational Objective 1 (OO1) and OO2. We have also revised the draft project document based on comments from Annexes 22 and an appendix (22a, b, c, d, e and f) that presents, in detail, the method behind AgriFonds' impact calculation.

We have developed and presented additional methodology documents based upon the Panel's initial feedback to provide further clarity into adaptation potential at the project level. Further, the development of a detailed Operational Objective 1 (OO1) document has been completed which provides more information on financial transparency and explanation of the MFIs process relating to adaptation actions. We note the Panel’s request for additional documentation in relation to the impact calculation methodology.

Paradigm shift potential
**Scale:** Medium to high

AE answers to TAP comments:

Thank you. Commodity players work in very low margin markets, where any changes to sourcing are risky and find limited opportunities from a commercial perspective. Working capital is always a limiting factor for commodity businesses, and therefore there is reduced appetite to deploy it into risky trades on the status quo. AgriFonds provides an alternative source of funding that can be used to hedge against the price risk associated with the food/fuel nexus - therefore eliminating these risks for the commodity players.

G4e's early investments have already led to increased interest in MFIs as a counterparty of choice (as date [December 2018] shows). AgriFonds will provide an additional local bank structure to encourage MFIs transactions. All AgriFonds investors must, at project level, over the duration of the transaction catalogue at least be 50% equity funded by G4e.

We believe the risk of competing funding without sustainability obligations to be low, as AgriFonds has already, with the existing portfolio, demonstrated that it is possible to provide financing while environmental conditions are inherently reducing overall investment risk.

The funding AgriFonds provides is not available in the market today and it is a key objective for G4e to demonstrate how this can work by adapting their funding structure on a parallel basis. At scale, the investment process requires detailing of this logic at an increasing level of detail.

Regarding paragraph 46 in ITAP's assessment relating to the market availability of loans as offered by AgriFonds and additional information provided by G4e, we agree that there is currently no such market available for MFIs. This ultimately be the key challenge that this programme seeks to address, together with the structuring of the investment expertise required in order to attract private sector investors. These two key objectives are effective use sides of the same coin and G4e's catalytic funding will be instrumental to realising the proof of concept.

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The IGGreen Fund

This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan have been prepared for IGGreen and FMO by E Co., to inform the project design of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Funding Proposal titled: 'Darren Fund for Sustainable Land Management in Forests'. The proposition aims at removing deforestation from major commodity supply chains, in a commercially viable and replicable manner. # Glossary

## Term Definition

### Gender
A suitably defined set of economic, social, and political roles, responsibilities, rights, entitlements obligations, associated with being female and male, as well as the power relations between and among women and men, boys and girls. The definition and expectations of what it means to be a man or woman vary across cultures and over time, and often interact with other factors such as race, class, religion, age, etc., which may also influence how people identify as men or women, are subject to the same set of expectations and sanctions.

### Gender Equity
The process of being fair for men and women, boys and girls. To ensure fairness, measures must be taken to compensate for cumulative economic, social, and political disadvantages that prevent men and women, boys and girls from operating on a level playing field.

### Gender Equality
The state or condition that affords women and men equal enjoyment of human rights, socially valued opportunities, and resources. Genuine equality means more than parity in numbers or being at the bottom of the available hierarchy and implies an improvement of life for all people. (SIGWG training resources)

### Gender-blind
Project designs and activities that ignore gender factors including roles and relations and cultural norms and values, and do not address intersectional overlapping inequalities. (SIGWG training resources)

### Gender-balanced
Requires that men and women be equally represented - either in equal numbers or in proportion to their presence – in particular settings. (USAID)

### Gender-sensitive
Gender-sensitive investments will acknowledge the different needs and constraints of women and men, boys and girls. This may include, for example, realizing that the work of female smallholders in the supply chain is not sufficiently recognized, which limits their access to training or credit. It also means recognizing that women and men have different skills and abilities; activities they perform and attempt to limit the negative effects of a gendered distribution of responsibilities. Gender-sensitive investments will also recognize that men and women experience differences themselves. Gender sensitive approaches can be easily integrated into existing training structures on other topics since they don’t require underlying gendered behavioral patterns or assumptions.

### Gender-transformative
Gender transformative investments will address gender imbalances, changing gendered power relations, and actively building equitable social norms and structures. An organization is aware of the ways in which gender inequalities are reproduced at work and seeks to change them at work. They may, for example, create equal working conditions for women and men, recognize and challenge stereotypes about what it means to be a man or woman, and management positions or to achieve an environment free from gender based violence (GBV). Gender transformative investments will also address the underlying causes of GBV. These are thus common for interventions that have the primary goal of addressing gender issues and transforming gender relations to promote equality. (adapted from the IOM Gender Guide)

### Gender Mainstreaming
Process or incorporating a gender perspective into organizations policies, strategies, and administrative functions, as well as into the institutional culture of an organization. This process


---
**Page 145**

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4 Fund

## Gender and Socioeconomic Analysis

### Gender-based Violence

In broader terms, "gender-based violence" is violence that is directed at individuals based on their biological sex, gender identity, or perceived adherence to culturally-defined expectations of masculinity or femininity. GBV can include physical abuse (including sexual and psychological abuse; threats; coercion; arbitrary deprivation of liberty; and economic exploitation); sexual abuse and sexual harassment; and other forms of social, and political inequalities between men and women. GBV can occur throughout the lifecycle, from pre-birth to old age, in all settings, including private homes, public spaces, schools, workplaces, and institutions. GBV is a violation of human rights and affects women and girls, and men and boys, including transgender individuals. Specific types of GBV include (but are not limited to) female infanticide; early and forced marriage; child sexual abuse; domestic violence; intimate partner violence; sexual exploitation; trafficking in persons; sexual coercion, harassment and abuse; neglect; domestic violence; and other forms of gender-based violence.

(adapted from UGG Strategy for the Prevention and Response to Gender-Based Violence)

### Sexual Exploitation

Sexual Abuse means the actual or threatened physical intrusion of a sexual nature, whether by force or under circumstances in which the person is unable to give consent. This includes attempted abuse of a position of vulnerability, differential power, or trust, for sexual purposes; the exploitative use of any person under the age of 18 years regardless of the relationship between them and another person who uses the exploitation of their vulnerability to initiate the abuse.

Sexual Harassment means any unwelcome sexual advances, requests for sexual favors, and other verbal or physical conduct of a sexual nature that creates an intimidating, hostile, or offensive environment in connection with a Fund-related Activity, and/or the avoidance of doubt, includes any form of harassment based on gender, race, religion, or other personal characteristics. Sexual Harassment may be initiated by any gender or sex.

(adapted from the GGF SEAH Policy)

# Introduction to Annex 8

## Programme concept of the &Green Fund

This decade, 2021 to 2030, marks the ambitious United Nations Decade on Ecosystem Restoration. This decade will prevent, halt and reverse the degradation of ecosystems - a key driver which is combatting deforestation.

Through the timely &Green Fund: Investing in Inclusive Agriculture and Protecting Forest programme, FMO*, the Accredited Entity (AE) along with respected GCF support* will invest in the &Green Fund to scale up commercial investment in tropical agricultural supply chains. The &Green Fund will be structured as a closed-ended fund that will receive contributions from major commodity supply chains in commercially viable and replicable manner.

To do so, the &Green Fund will scale up commercial investment in tropical agricultural supply chains by providing financial support for investments in sustainable production at a landscape level. Investments, for the Fund, include those targeting forest and post conservation and restoration embedded in landscapes of sustainable and regenerative agriculture. Target sectors, for the Fund, are those major sector supply chains driving deforestation, such as livestock, palm oil, soy, rubber, cocoa and forest products.

The programme is comprised of two main components (see Section E - Funding Proposal for the Logical Framework):

* **Component 1** focuses on the establishment and operation of the investment vehicle (see Section C.3 - Funding Proposal for details on Fund structure), which will secure co-financing and invest directly in sustainable supply chains businesses that embrace zero-deforestation production and forest protection.

* **Component 2** provides a technical assistance facility that overcomes the capacity and information barriers facing commodity producers and neighboring communities, while building a community of practice to support the adoption and scale-up for products as well as incorporating gender-responsive action in the target countries.

# 1.2 Objective of the gender assessment

Given this background, this Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan have been prepared for FMO, to inform the project design of the GCF Investment Proposal and contribute towards generation of gender outcomes. It is the Annex B of the Fund Proposal and has been developed according to the GCF’s Gender Mainstreaming Toolkit, including gender action through climate finance, notably through the Mainstreaming Toolkit1.

The overall objective of the gender assessment will be to provide a tool to mainstream and accelerate gender-responsive and gender-transformative investment through the G4Green Fund Strategy.

Within the overarching objective, the Gender Assessment will have two outcomes: to integrate an analysis of gender-based inequalities that exist in the countries, the context and sociocultural factors underlying gender inequality which are exacerbated by climate change and optimize the presence of women in decision-making processes at all levels, both individual and collective resilience to climate change. Therefore, this assessment will inform the project design formulation, implementation, and monitoring and evaluation.

Lastly, the assessment will inform the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The GAP has been designed to ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into the projects implemented by women and vulnerable groups in accessing and benefiting from the proposed programme, if financed by the GCF, are meaningfully addressed. The GAP will contain gender-related activities, baselines, sex-disaggregated indicators and targets, and a set of recommendations.

The G4Green Fund recognizes that static 'gender mainstreaming' is both necessary and relevant for the programme to maximise its outcomes for zero deforestation. This assessment, therefore details G4Green Fund’s Internal Gender Approach and how it aims to bolster gender mainstreaming within the project design formulation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation phases of responsive processes (including robust gender analysis, including assessment of SEAR-related risks, indicators, etc.), as well as in the development of a gender action plan. The internal gender approach is external investment thesis of the G4Green Fund (see Section 3). Embedding gender into deforestation commitments offers considerable opportunities for leveraging synergies between restoration and climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies to contribute towards commitments to sustainable development goals - SDGs (particularly SGD 5 – Gender Equality; SGD 12 – Sustainable Production and Consumption; SGD 13 – Climate action; and SGD 15 – Life on Land).

1 <http://www.greenclimatefund.org/doing-business/mainstreaming-gender-green-climate-fund-projects>

It is important to note that gender is socially constructed, and gender roles are continually generic and often change in response to altering circumstances. MOER, C. G. N. (1993): Gender Planning and Development: Theory, Practice and Training. New York: Routledge.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The IGreen Fund

The Fund acknowledges that gender mainstreaming will help the IGreen Fund achieve its goals identified for environmental impact and social inclusiveness. Particularly:

- Climate adaptation and mitigation pathways are not gender-neutral;
- Gendered needs and vulnerabilities of marginal groups, such as Indigenous Peoples, need to be mainstreamed into adaptation design, resilience capacity-building and mitigation services;
- Gender-transformative impact can be driven through robust financial means, gender budgets and dedicated resources towards mainstreaming gender action in climate change and delivering the SDGs.

This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (Annex 8 of the Funding Proposal) pairs with the analysis and recommendations of the Environmental and Social Management Plan (Annex 6 of the Funding Proposal), to ensure that the benefits of sustainable finance for commodity chains, in tandem with gender equality, will be maximized. This will lead to better-off households and/or more mainstream producer groups that are able to capitalize on new opportunities and respond better to changes implemented through the programme.

## 1.3 COVID-19: the pandemic's gendered effect on livelihoods

During the development of this assessment, the global community continues to grapple with COVID-19. The pandemic has taken a toll on the lives of millions of people, both regarding to health and wellbeing, and socioeconomic indicators. Despite deforestation slowing down during the initial stage of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, the planet still lost around 4.2 million hectares (according to Global Forest Watch) compared with an average loss of 3.5 million hectares per year.

A large proportion of this loss was driven by agriculture, both smallholder and large-scale commodity cultivation. This forest loss is a major reason why agriculture accounts for one-quarter of all greenhouse gas emissions.

* Current literature on climate change, and its effects and emergent risks, are predominantly produced in scientific circles. Yet, there is increasing evidence that adopting social science methods, and situating resilience and adaptation practices within the context of gender equality, can help to better understand how these issues impact environmental and socioeconomic benefits. Conversely, this could better prepare communities to avoid resource conflicts and improve their ability to adapt to climate change.
* See: Hulme, M., & Pelling, M. (2014). Making climate finance work for women. Sec. oecd.org/environment/making-climate-finance-work-for-women.

* Poor or missing gender analysis, or the lack of gender-responsive action, may lead to planners or personnel decisions that are not in line with the needs and capacities of women and girls, particularly those living in more vulnerable communities. Further, this also goes over the existing burden on women among such groups. See: Ncube, M., & Hulme, M. (2014). Making climate finance work for women. Sec. oecd.org/environment/making-climate-finance-work-for-women.

* OECD (2014). Making climate finance work for women. Sec. oecd.org/environment/making-climate-finance-work-for-women.

## 1.4 The GCF’s Updated Gender Policy

The analytical prerequisites of this Gender Assessment and Action Plan are informed by the stipulations of the GCF’s Updated Gender Policy. The GCF adopted a revised version of its 2014 Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan on June 2018 in Korea*. The revised policy addresses pertinent issues such as the need for gender equality, the importance of ensuring that projects preserve the “women’s issue”; and the identification of synergies with the in-house Indigenous Peoples’ and Local Communities (IPLCs) Programme. The GCF also adopted the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)’s Gender Action Plan (GAP), Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). Since the adoption of the updated policy, the GCF has ensured that all projects are gender-responsive. The revised policy has ensured the requirement of the policy for AEs to consider and submit a gender assessment along with appropriate environmental and social assessments; and a gender action plan (programme) as part of their project design and implementation across the public and private sectors.

Lastly, the programmatic approach of this Annex 8 has been developed in line with previous gender commitments in similar GCF investments. In consultation with the GCF’s Private Sector Facility, namely:

# Applying concurrent triangulation to identify gender barriers for the GGreen Fund

The study employs a concurrent triangulation methodology, and deliberately sidesteps sequential research processes. In sequential designs, either the qualitative or quantitative data are collected first, followed by the other type of data in the second stage.16 In contrast, concurrent designs are characterized by the collection of both types of data during the same stage (which was the case for the development of this assessment).

Within these two categories, there can be three specific designs based on:  
(a) the level of emphasis given to the qualitative and quantitative data (equal or unequal),  
(b) the process used to analyze and integrate the data, and  
(c) whether the theoretical basis underlying the study methodology is to bring about social change or advocacy.17

Since the secondary research was formative in nature and rapid in mandate (to cover all 17 target countries), the assessment prompted a research methodology to establish an inventory of information and a gap analysis that can be operationalized to cover the complex aspects of deforestation supply chains, and end-user products.

Therefore, the concurrent approach is suited because of:  
the flexibility to use secondary quantitative and qualitative findings for the comprehension of the studied phenomenon in the primary research (mainly through existing GGreen works), and the accuracy it can provide to sample size and data collection.18 The study also provides a basis for further research and action planning, able to propose measures that are relevant for the delivery of outputs identified in the results framework.

16 Creswell JW, Plano Clark VL, Gutmann ML, Hanson WE. Advances in mixed methods research: Design. In: Tashakkori A, Teddlie C, editors. Handbook of mixed methods in social and behavioral research.
17 Creswell JW, Plano Clark VL, Gutmann ML, Hanson WE. . Inc.

# Introduction to the t&Green Fund

## Lending Philosophy

The t&Green Fund’s lending philosophy conceptualizes and demonstrates to both public and private actors how it can provide for inclusive economic growth, and forest and peat protection (and potentially restoration) when financing the production of agricultural commodities that are sourced from tropical landscapes. It does so by:

- Providing credit facilities only in jurisdictions with progressive forest and/or peat protection laws and policies and sound land management strategies.
- Adding quantitative output-based environmental and social criteria and targets to the financing of commodity production.
- Improving methods of sourcing timber by including them in high-productivity, sustainable supply chains.
- Attracting commercial investors into such projects by partly mitigating their credit risk (through financial or environmental risk sustainability safeguards).
- Stimulating innovation in business models and approaches to catalyse financially sustainable, deforestation free, socially inclusive, high productivity agriculture in tropical forest landscapes.
- Offering tailored debt instruments, such as: extended tenor, subordination in order to stimulate co-investment from the market, where the market does not or will not at this time invest alone.

The t&Green Fund, especially on land and forest governance, supports and requires its investors to engage with communities and local, regional and national governments. These engagements are often enabled by its long-term supply chain and landscape governing partner IDH<sup>®</sup>, and its clients’ partner implementing organisations on the ground in the majority of t&Green target regions.

## Theory of Change (ToC)

The ToC of the t&Green Fund ensures there is increased gender mainstreaming and social inclusion through the fund structure. This is because t&Green’s financial and technical support aims to level the playing field for sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation-free agriculture. The Fund provides risk capital to new commodity production systems; incentivises the intensification of agricultural practices in a way that reduces negative environmental impacts and externalities provided by natural forests; reinforces public policy signals and encourages improved regulations; and motivates mainstream private sector investors to provide increased capital inflows at scale for responsible producers.

<sup>®</sup> Translated from the original Dutch: “Initiatief voor Duurzame Handel” (IDH)

3.3 Internal Standards

The $G Green Fund provides flexible, needs-adapted structures to finance commodity supply chain investments in $G Green’s approved jurisdictions, all of which have progressive forest and/or peatland protection strategies & policies. Each Investment must, at initial screening stage, demonstrate compliance with these chapters’ content, and provide a clear rationale.

Therefore, $G Green has an intent, embedded within its “do no harm” which can be mobilized towards mainstreaming a gender-aware perspective throughout the Fund structure. Examples from other good practices are:

3.3.1 Exclusion List

The $G Green Fund’s Exclusion List identifies stringent measures to preclude any business portfolios that include:
- Forced labour or child labour - as defined by International Labour Organization standards
- Activities or materials deemed illegal under host country laws or regulations or international conventions and agreements, or subject to international phase-outs or bans
- Cross-border trade in waste and waste products, unless compliant to the Basel Convention and other relevant international instruments
- Destruction of High Conservation Value areas
- Radioactive materials and unbound asbestos fibres
- Pornography and/or prostitution
- Racist and/or anti-democratic media
- In the event that any of these following products form a substantial part of a project’s primary financed business activities: alcohol beverages; tobacco; weapons and munitions; and/or gambling.

3.3.2 Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria Assessment (JECA)

The $G Green Fund catalyzes entry into jurisdictions where local and host government authorities are also committed to reducing deforestation, and are actively taking steps to work with the private sector, communities and civil society to protect forest and peatland. The JECA tool assesses whether a jurisdiction is eligible for funding from the $G Green Fund based on:
- Local authorities are committed to the reduction of deforestation and the protection of valuable ecosystems.
- Progress on sustainable and socially inclusive intensification through $G Green funding is also being demonstrated (e.g., in Brazil, Indonesia, Liberia).
- The jurisdiction must adhere to strong social and environmental covenants - in particular, IFC Performance Standards.

Currently, $G Green has the following approved jurisdictions: Brazil (State of Mato Grosso; State of Para) and country level jurisdictions in Colombia, Ecuador, Gabon, Indonesia, Liberia and Peru.

3.4 IDH Technical Assistance Facility

IDH - the Sustainable Trade Initiative® was the incorporator of the Stichting B|Green Fund. IDH & B|Green work collaboratively and have outlined their joint ambition in a Memorandum of Understanding. Through its country offices, and through the international sustainable landscapes programme, IDH works with governments and private actors to key jurisdictions for B|Green, to develop potential projects of interest for the Fund, and to support local governance in line with the Fund’s Jurisdictional Eligibility Criteria.

IDH manages the Technical Assistance Facility (TA Facility) on behalf of the Fund. The TA Facility was formally launched in 2019, and aims to reduce risk and maximize impact of the B|Green (potential) investment projects by supporting investment-readiness, monitoring, post-investment client support and sharing of lessons and knowledge from B|Green and its investment projects with third party stakeholders.

IDH has identified that equitable and meaningful participation of women is key to driving sustainable development in supply chains. To address this concern, IDH has developed a Gender Toolkit, consisting of practical tools for use on the ground and simple projects in congruent sectors, and a Gender Guide, where opportunities to improve gender equality are explored. The B|Green Fund’s Gender Approach will mobilize these existing tools.

The role of the TA Facility is to both enhance understanding of gender dynamics in the early project cycle and maximize opportunities for enhanced gender equality and empowerment. By considering how women and men participate in interventions, the TA Facility strives to enable

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

## 3.4.1 IDH Gender Toolkit

The IDH Gender Toolkit guides program teams and partners on how to integrate gender aspects in their programs. Following six programming steps, it provides tools on how to integrate gender considerations into project design, implementation, and monitoring, to develop gender-responsive indicators for M&E to support gender mainstreaming through the project cycle.

## 3.4.2 IDH Gender Guide

This guide, part of the IDH Gender Toolkit, presents the six in programming, during which opportunities arise to integrate gender aspects that may positively influence and leverage greater impact of a program or interventions. When planning to integrate gender in a successful investment, the G4Green Fund’s Gender Approach can be used as a tool for guidance. The Fund's Gender Approach builds on the tools and guides developed by IDH. Where appropriate and necessary, the Fund will mobilise IDH's TA facility to identify opportunities and barriers that female entrepreneurs face when accessing finance. This will lead to the identification of investment options envisioned through the proposed GCF programme. This will lead to the implementation of the G4Green Fund’s Gender Approach, when sub-projects/investments (Deals) are identified, should the proposed GCF programme be green-lighted.

# Programmatic Gender Approach for the &Green Fund

## Scope

This Gender Approach will provide a framework to accelerate gender mainstreaming investments through the &Green Fund structure. It informs this Annex B and aligns itself with guidance provided by the GCF on mobilizing gender action through climate finance, notably through the Mainstreaming Gender in Climate Finance (GCF) Programme.

The Gender Approach is designed as an integral part of the proposed GCF programme (in Section E of the GCF Funding Proposal) and identify entry points for the chosen paradigm shift pathway to have gender and social co-benefits, which will be identified through a gender analysis. The approach will also provide guidance on how to leverage existing gender mechanisms, including dispensing gender mainstreaming protocols through the Technical Assistance Facility, and will utilize IDB's Gender Toolkit and Gender Guide.

## Two-Track Approach

The Green Gender Approach consists of two different tracks: gender-sensitive and gender-transformative investments:

* **Gender-sensitive investments** acknowledge the different needs and constraints of women and men without changing the status quo. The client may, for example, realize that their lack of female smallholders is due to a lack of access to land or credit.
* **Gender-transformative investments** address gender imbalances, changing gender power relations, and challenging gender norms. They recognize that women and men do not have equal opportunities in the household, at community level or at work. They may, for example, create equal working conditions for women and men, recognizing their specific needs and constraints.

Gender-sensitive approaches can be easily integrated into existing training structures on other topics since they don't require underlying gendered behavioral patterns to be addressed.


---
**Page 158**
---

# &Green Investment Process

These tracks are built in to the investment process of the fund, which consists of several phases:

1) **Investment Rationale:** In this first phase in the investment process, &Green assesses whether there is a fit between the prospective investment and &Green’s mission. This is assessed through a structured framework called the Character Assessment (CA). The CA includes a gender assessment to evaluate whether the prospective investment requires a gender-sensitive or a gender transformative approach. This assessment then triggers different processes in the rest of the investment process.

2) **Deal Note:** If the &Green team, including the Investment Committee, agrees that the prospective investment has a good fit with the Fund’s vision of transformational change, a first screening will be initiated and presented in a Deal Note. This screening includes a gender-related screening, and informs the scope of the due diligence process.

3) **Credit Approvals:** Upon approval of the Deal Note by the Investment Committee and Credit Committee, a thorough due diligence is conducted with external experts to establish a solid basis for the prospective investment, including a gender baseline. With the information collected during this phase, &Green develops an Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) Impact Plan (LPP) and/or an Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP). The LPP includes the key milestones and deliverables in a clear, time-bound format. The ESAP is more detailed than the LPP and includes the gender outcomes envisioned for the investment. The ESAP and LPP are strict environmental and social safeguarding covenants that are conditions of the loan contracts of the investment.

4) **Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV):** For each investment, an MRV plan is designed based on the targets laid out in the LPP/ESAP. &Green implements a robust MRV framework to assess and report on progress against the various milestones and deliverables, including gender-related indicators.

Specifically, the following indicators for social inclusion are measured by the &Green fund. Each indicator is disaggregated by gender. Note that the KPI framework has recently been revised and the indicators have not yet been retrofitted to existing portfolio companies. &Green is working to create a baseline this year to ensure continued monitoring against these indicators going forward.

| ID | Indicator | Definition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 6.1 | People receiving community services | Number of people whose livelihoods directly benefit from services provided or made possible by the transaction. |
| 6.2a | Jobs supported (direct) | Number of direct jobs supported in full-time equivalent (FTE). |
| 6.2b | Jobs supported (indirect) | Number indirect jobs supported in FTE (LM model). |

# 4.4 &Green Gender Approach

## Investment Rationale: At the outset, a gender screening process (IDH - Gender Toolkit Step 1) will determine whether the investment will have a gender-sensitive impact or gender-transformative impact.

### Deal Note: In the following stage, whether the investment has been assessed to be gender-sensitive or gender-transformative, a high-level gender assessment (based on the IDH methodology) will be conducted. This will involve assessing the client’s fit into the &Green transformational framework and conducting an initial due diligence exercise (desktop/on-scoping, client's document screening, and literature review). This assessment includes a high-check on SEAH-related risks, derived from the jurisdictional context, as well as a check of the company’s policies and practices related to SEAH for the investee and affiliated companies, and an initial check of the policy framework in place to address SEAH-related risks. The results of this assessment (Section A) will be used as a basis for further investigation in the implementation stage.

### Due Diligence: In the case of a gender-sensitive project, the high-level gender assessment will inform the scope of Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP). This is a standard part of the Scope 1 & Environment and Social Action Plan (ESAP) Reference Document. The ESAP Reference will include a focus on gender risks, including SEAH-related risks, and how to mitigate these at an early stage in the project cycle. In the case of a gender-transformative project, during due diligence stage, the high-level gender assessment will be used as a background to set up in-depth gender analysis conducted by technical experts, using the IDH methodology as an entry point.

## Credit Application: In both gender-sensitive and gender-transformative investments, a gender baseline will be included in the Client’s Landscape Protection Plan (LPP). For gender-transformative investments, this will include an in-depth analysis of gender barriers of the context (and opportunities for transformation) and how these relate to the project’s proposed outputs, outcomes and activities, including SEAH-related actions. These will be mainstreamed into the Investment's Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP). These targets will be mapped against the IDH methodology and the &Green Gender Toolkit. The LPP will also be gender-sensitized and included (to the extent possible) in gender mainstreaming processes, to ensure both replicability and sustainability.

## Deal Approval: After the approval of the credit application, responsibilities of the client relating to gender mainstreaming and implementing actions laid out in the ESAP to remove gender barriers and improve upon gender baseline at the investment site/through the project will be codified into the contracts and covenants. Through &Green’s robust monitoring, reporting and verification framework, the progress made by the investee on gender-related outputs, and related outputs, will be tracked and evaluated (see Investment Principles and ESAP Manual Section).

## Monitoring, Reporting and Verification Framework: In this stage, when appropriate, the Fund will conduct deep dive/case studies into the efficacy of the gender mainstreaming steps taken through particular deals and lessons learned/practices garnered by stakeholders (and the clients) in the process. These studies will complement the Fund’s MRR framework (see Investment Principles and ESAP Manual Section).

## 4.4.1 Examples of G4Green Gender Approach Applications

Examples of the application of the G4Green Gender Approach to the fund portfolio could include the following:

- **Affirmative sourcing policies towards women producers:** the gender assessment for the 17 countries conducted during the preparation of the programme revealed that women (in most contexts) are marginalized or side-lined in the different value chains. To ensure that women get equal benefits from G4Green’s supply chain transformation, through improved market access, better quality and quantity of produce, and increased income, as well as greater stakeholder engagement, the fund will ensure affirmative sourcing policies towards women producers.

- **Training and capacity building to improve gender policies and other conductor factors within client companies:** where capacity is lacking on the operationalization of gender equality goals within client companies. G4Green can require that the client company conducts a gender audit and/or provides training for its staff in order to meet specific gender requirements in the ESAP/LPP.

Revising policies and practices such as improving grievance processes and discussing harassment during employee gatherings, as part of the IPC’s COP process (see sub-section 4.3.2), is another example of how G4Green can use its policy framework to promote gender equality. However, the efficacy of these policies may vary, depending on cultural norms and practices within a company, as well as the level of awareness among social groups – in this process has been recognized as critical, and can be a requirement in the ESAP/LPP; This will be an entry point to engage potential client companies on SEAH, and to ensure that grievance mechanisms are maintained as part of company policies and addressed through grievance processes.

- **Targeting for equitable representation of women within the company, also at senior management level:** enhanced involvement in decision-making by women supports an inclusive approach to business operations. G4Green will support companies along their company’s journey towards equitable representations, fair implementation and communication policies, as well as improved outcomes, which G4Green will promote among client companies

- **Proactively training both female and male smallholders on cultivation, processing, and marketing:** through this, G4Green will ensure access to training through culturally sensitive interventions that are tailored for the specific needs of women. This is a key component in the company’s journey towards equitable representations.

- **Community awareness and sensitization for generating consensus on G4Green principles:** through this step, G4Green will introduce an advocacy rubric within its gender approach, particularly by ensuring that women and men participate in workshops on land tenure and financial inclusion. The engagement of both male and female stakeholders is critical to ensure awareness in communities is critical to ensure gender mainstreaming and social inclusion in a company’s (future) supply base.

# 4.4.2 Capacity for Implementation of the B&Green Gender Approach

The partners in this facility will draw on both in-house and external expertise for the implementation of this Gender Approach. This section outlines the various sources of expertise that are available to the facility, and where applicable, plans to strengthen this capacity.

## The B&Green Fund

SAI Ventures, Investment Advisor to the B&Green Fund, has a team of specialists (90% women) that implement investment processes. More than one-third of the team is dedicated to Environmental and Social management, including the implementation of the Gender Approach across all investments. This includes a gender specialist who works with the portfolio companies on a suite of sustainability frameworks across different agricultural sectors globally. This ranges from certified oil palm, with a focus on gender opportunities for pregnant women in Sierra Leone, to cotton in India.

Michael Schupf, head of Impact & ESG, worked in sustainable cocoa supply chains to implement sustainability standards for smallholder farmers, impacting women in the sector.

Natalia Pastukhov, E&S Director, lived and worked in Brazil for 15 years, where she worked to establish a gender lens in the country. She has experience with feasibility of business models for women cooperatives of babassu nut breakers in the Brazilian Amazon.

Marthe Toleman, also an E&S Director, has been active in the gender space throughout her career. Implementing gender mainstreaming and youth inclusion programs across forestry portfolios in Africa and Southeast Asia.

The team adopts a flat management structure where we believe that the team as a whole is responsible for the success of the Fund. Therefore, the SAI Ventures team will ensure capacity building and training to support the implementation of this Gender Approach. This includes public training sessions that are suited to B&Green's needs, and publicly available to benefit other funds as well.

## IDH - Sustainable Trade Initiative

IDH has established a Global Gender Unit, led by a full-time Senior Manager, to ensure gender being mainstreamed across all projects. To drive gender organization-wide, IDH has set up an internal gender champions structure and an IDH staff compulsory must follow a training on gender equality and inclusion. The team is also using the IDH Gender Tool kit. Gender champions, gender business Units experts and the Global Gender Unit are responsible for the implementation of this tool. The Gender Unit will be trained in the tool and on the interventions design. When needed for specific projects, IDH is also working with a pool of external gender experts.

IDH currently offers a range of gender/gender toolkit training available for external partner organizations, like B&Green. To ensure the applicability of the training to further build gender capacity in the B&Green team, the IDH training has already been reviewed by the B&Green ESG team and feedback will be incorporated in the training that will be made available to B&Green.

## Client companies

B&Green requires that its investee companies have the capacity and the resources to implement the gender outcomes laid out in the LPP and ESAP. All client companies will have in-house E&S resources that typically oversee the implementation of ESAP/LPP and facilitate the progress

# 5. Sectoral information for the programme paradigm

## 5.1 Gender in &Green commodity value chains

- Participation of men and women in forestry management is inextricably tied to the social, cultural, historical, and political contexts of communities where forest management takes place. These contexts shape how individuals perceive their rights of control, access, and responsibility that interact with class, race, caste, culture or ethnicity to shape ecological change and sustainable livelihoods (Peach Brown)²⁰¹.

- Women, more so than men – in the &Green target countries a number of gendered and age-related barriers exist. These include: limited equal opportunity to acquire the factors of production such as land, credit networks, infrastructure and education; limited time to take advantage of opportunities for training and skills development (particularly in agroforestry projects) due to women’s existing roles and responsibilities to carry out non-salaried tasks (e.g., child care, household chores); and limited access to credit and other economic resources. Women are also more vulnerable in communities, markets and institutional structures which favour male participation and prevent women from having an equal voice in decision-making. (Hvidt)²⁰².

- The supply chains that &Green seeks to transform will not only provide improved economic opportunities for women but can also provide benefits to youth (17 – 25) by improving livelihoods and skill base in the target countries. Evaluative research on integrated cocoa agroforestry training programmes conducted by the International Cocoa Initiative shows that Ghana is being a leading producer of cocoa and having a burgeoning youth population. It is estimated that the average age of a cocoa farmer in the country is greater than 50 years²⁰³.

- The study found that young people are more likely to be involved in cocoa production if they have access to land, finance, and training. This suggests that there may be potential barriers they face in order to do so; that a multi-faceted skills training programme was developed by the International Cocoa Initiative (ICI)²⁰⁴. The ICI’s integrated cocoa agroforestry training had three key components: i) cocoa academies (which includes agricultural practices, life skills and financial literacy); ii) business incubators (including entrepreneurship development, market access, and value addition); iii) land tenure (access to land and finances). These findings reflect the theory of change being proposed by &Green, and shows the gender-transformative impact the programme can post to on youth in several of the target countries.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

## Page 162

### The cattle value chain displays complex gender factors; some estimate show that women make up about two-thirds of the 600 million substance livestock keepers worldwide, however, men are the primary workers in industrialized cattle value chains. Women's livelihoods and food security are often undermined by their lack of access to land, credit-contracted intra- and inter-relations among household members, intermediaries, cooperatives and private sector companies. G4Green, therefore, will aim to accelerate funding for women-led livestock enterprises that demonstrate driving positive impact on and gender-responsive resilient livelihoods. (RJR)

### In terms of cocoa, women play a key role as producers, particularly in West Africa. In Ghana, women represent 70% of the cocoa farmers and are responsible for most aspects of production and development. This includes: women's access to land for cocoa production; women's rights/tenure to land; women's access to markets and financial services; women's access to inputs such as fertilizers and pesticides; women's access to credit; women's access to education; women in leadership positions; women's access to produce groups or cooperatives; and, women's disproportionate burden of household chores and limited time. These considerations are critical for the development of a gender-sensitive cocoa value chain, with backstopping from IGF on technical assistance to drive impact on for meaningful gender inclusion in commodity supply chains. (Olam)27

### Globally, in the coffee sector and smallholder agriculture in developing countries, there is a direct link between women's economic empowerment and their active participation in and contribution to the coffee value chain and in farm and domestic decisions, such as decisions over credit access, land tenure, and decision-making. Women are often excluded from these decisions.

As with the cocoa supply chain, G4Green has taken cognizance of these gendered barriers, which it will aim to remove by seeking out client companies with gender track record as well as a commitment to gender equality in their operations.

### In terms of palm oil in Indonesia, research revealed that women and children are often forced to work for free to help men meet the production targets that the company sets as condition for them to receive the full wage for the day. Women, often, tend to be restricted to low-paying jobs such as harvesting and transporting palm fruit bunches, which are heavy tasks that are extremely dangerous for women and their reproductive health. Gender-based violence in the palm oil supply chain has remained insidious: women have reported rape and torture in the field; sexual harassment is a common occurrence on plantations; and, in addition, violent arrests or searches have been conducted by private sector entities against those palm oil workers who are suspected of having stolen palm fruit bunches. Women having their traditional oil production taken away or destroyed, in many cases. G4Green will meet IFC Performance Standards to prevent SEAH and other forms of GSV in the prioritized commodity supply chains, as well as design screening tools embedded in the ESG process.

- Plantation forests require intensive management, but they grow faster than natural forests; in 2011, FAO reported that although they accounted only for 5% of the total wood forest area, it produced 35% of all the world's wood product. Foresty and forest management policies, which are often designed by men and implemented without women including land ownership and political underrepresentation, result in women having less access and agents over forest resources and economic opportunities. However, women are powerful agents for change because they have a strong connection to forests as they are in charge with the wellbeing of the household, and tend to adopt a different approach to forest management than men do. Women's knowledge is vast and diverse and more on agroecology, including food security, medicinal plants, and transmission of invaluable knowledge to future generations (UN-REDD).

- This is also the case for say: Brazil; one the largest producing countries, women make up 50% of the workforce in the forest sector but continue to earn approximately one quarter less what men earn and dedicate close to 75 more time to domestic and care work than men. (G4Green will ensure that convenants around equal pay is clear and transparent and that women are included in all decision making processes).

- Strategies for increased participation of women in commercialized and well-developed supply chains (particularly export-facing ones) require upgrading based on the typology of the women - as well as the social group they are in. In question: While women in formal sector employment have access to training, information, credit and markets, their needs differ from those of women in informal sector employment. Women in male-headed households need institutionalization of gender-sensitive policies in the governance of producer groups to enable them to upgrade as value chain integrators and value chain actors. In addition, women in these households also need specific expertise on gender (if needed) and rely on existing capacities in-house as well as IOM to assess gender dynamics - as they relate to intersections - and mainstream the findings into the project design.

- Informal economies and markets are the dominant source of livelihoods in rural areas - and the engagement in forestry value chains is often critical for rural women's livelihoods and the well-being of their household. (IRED, 2015) Women's use of forest resources tends to center on subsistence needs rather than commercialization. However, there are opportunities for women to participate in the commercialization of more profitable forest resources. Women are the main caregivers of children and the elderly and have a strong role in household decision-making. Given their care work responsibilities of cooking for the household, women's tree selection is based on their use and relevance for subsistence. Women prefer trees that provide fruits, firewood, fodder and increase soil; while men may prefer for commercial species. This disparity needs to be managed through project design, to ensure benefits of the project

**Women’s time poverty and physical safety concerns limit their access to and use of forest resources and related activities. Time poverty is used as a very particular sense in relation to women, because they are more likely than men having the choice to work or not: an individual is time poor if he/she is working long hours and is also monetary poor, or would fall into monetary poverty if he/she were to reduce his/her working hours below a given time period.**

First, the individual does not have enough time for rest and leisure once all working hours (whether paid or unpaid) are accounted for. Second, the individual cannot reduce his/her working time without either increasing the level of poverty of his/her household (if the household is originally poor), or incurring a loss in income or consumption (if the household is not originally poor). (CIF, 2017)**^3^**

**Invisible labor in the value chain needs to be recognized and accounted for. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) finds that women / vulnerable sections from marginalized groups are frequently engaged in activities that are undervalued, including informal sector work, unpaid domestic work, and child care. In particular, these different value chains, Include project design needs to, therefore ensure women’s time burden are not exacerbated by proposed investments, and their participation is not stymied by prevailing sociocultural norms, which G4een will wish to assuage through its Gender Framework**

**Women and members of indigenous communities need to be recognized as important stakeholders in forest governance, production chains and project design: The International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) has developed the Forest Gender Index (EGI), which showed that women as well as Indigenous Peoples who rely on forests are often underrepresented in forest governance at the local, national and international levels. (IUCN)**^4^**

# 5.2 SEAH and GBV in The G8 Green Programme

In 1993, the Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women (A/RES/48/104) defines violence against women as "any act of gender-based violence that results in, or is likely to result in, physical, sexual or mental harm or suffering, whether occurring in public or private life."

More recently, as both theoretical and practical work on GBV evolved and became enshrined in numerous conventions and declarations, article 3 of the Council of Europe's Convention on Preventing and Combating Violence against Women and Domestic Violence (2011) explicitly offered the following definition: "gender-based violence against women shall mean violence that is directed against a woman because she is a woman or that affects women disproportionately."

A critical issue identified in this assessment is Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Sexual Harassment (SEAH). The G8 Green Programme has prioritized SEAH as an important area for action. Different measures in the commodity supply chains (G8) are prioritizing in the target 17 countries where they operate. These include: gender equality, women's empowerment, and broader GBV is both caused and exacerbated by existing societal gender norms and roles. These are symptomatic of gender inequality and a tool to reinforce this inequality, including maintaining or exerting control over natural resources, further entrenching gender inequality in a cyclical manner.

Communities and indigenous people in rural areas—where many of the commodity supply chains will be located—often face GBV without access to the redress mechanisms that may be available for women living in urban areas. These communities are also more vulnerable to water and fuel, traveling to remote locations, lack of reliable energy sources, traditional harmful practices such as female genital mutilation (FGM), and other forms of violence against women and girls, adding to their exposure to the risk of GBV. In addition, the potential for violence related to natural resources is particularly augmented in the face of environmental stressors, where access to water, fuel, or land becomes increasingly scarce or degraded resources is increasingly fraught, resulting in higher tension, increased

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Gene Fund

Page 106

negative coping strategies and reduced resilience for families and communities thus affecting lives and livelihoods.[4]

SEAH and G4V, therefore, is widespread in the agriculture sector. The IFC[5] has broken down how it can be found at all levels of the value chain:

- In farming and primary production: women experience high levels of abuse in the fields. Agrochemical exposure increases the risk of G4V and SEAH. As stated by ILO,[6] non-standard forms of work, including temporary work and informal work, are key factors in creating potential vulnerabilities for women workers to be exposed to sexual harassment against women workers. For instance, almost 9 out of 10 horticultural workers in Ethiopia reported personally experiencing some form of sexual harassment on the farm.[7]

- In processing: women are concentrated at the harvesting and packaging stage, as women are mostly concentrated in low-income, often informal roles, and are pressured into accepting abuse by their male supervisors in order not to lose their remuneration.

- In logistics and distribution: male drivers transporting agricultural goods over remote roads and long distances can perpetrate G4V and SEAH. Research in Brazil[8] found that one in five women working as loaders or drivers said she had been sexually harassed while being transported goods.

- At home: domestic violence is widespread (at the global level), but here it is exacerbated by the fact that, across Europe, women working in agriculture and fisheries have the highest rates of partner violence of any occupational group: 14% compared with 4% across all industries.[9] Violence in family farms is therefore of particular concern.

G4Gene recognizes that this is a crucial issue for the implementation of the programme, and is committed to understanding how SEAH and G4V are entrenched into the overall society and specifically into selected value chains of the 17 target countries, to be able to address it with specific actions:

- G4Green will meet the IFC Performance standards. These were updated in 2012 to strengthen gender considerations. Most importantly, G4Green will comply with:
    - Performance Standard 2, paragraph 15 on Non-discrimination and Equal opportunity, thereby ensuring that women are not discriminated against and address harassment, intimidation, and/or exploitation, especially in regard to women.

## G4S is aware that GBV and SEAR are global phenomena but that each country has its specific contexts, linked with wider conditions of women, gender norms, intersectionsality, and more. The gender assessment undertaken in some countries included special cases such as the case of Colombia where high levels of GBV were found to the extent that 40% of women in Colombia have been victims of sexual violence.

## G4S acknowledges these are starting points that will be used to inform the assessment phase of each investment in the implementation countries, so that a full picture of SEAR and GBV can be developed and addressed appropriately, both generally and at the level of specific value chains.

Additionally, IGH has been focusing exclusively on addressing gender in its programmes, and on reducing GBV specifically. The guidance provided is not merely about empowering women to empower themselves, but by training producers on gender-balanced practices, it is possible to create safer, happier, and therefore more productive, working environments. By training farming families on the division of responsibilities within their households, it is possible to improve the responsibilities in the household, so it is possible to promote improvements to the livelihood of the farmer that also have gender-positive effects.

Two example of this holistic approach include IGH and G4S (which do not require GBV technical assistance).

- In the tea sector in Kenya, IGH started work on gender in 2012 through its partnership with The Ethical Tea Partnership and the Kenya Tea Development Agency. Focusing on gender discrimination and harassment, IGH has trained over 350 women from various levels of management, supervisors, and staff members. This led to the following concrete achievements:

    - **Safe**: 76% in supervisor positions and 33% of women in management positions;
    - **More equal employment opportunities**: Women taking on roles that were traditionally reserved for men (e.g., truck drivers);
    - All factories have a gender policy, with anti-discrimination policies in place; grievance complaints and disciplinary procedures are included in induction training for new employees.

- In 2016, IGH convened a platform in Kenya with seven tea companies to create a joint agenda for action to address gender-based violence (GBV) in the sector and share best practices. The platform is also working on addressing GBV in the Kenyan tea industry, and it is developing a roadmap for plantation management on addressing GBV alongside field-level projects to implement the roadmap.

# Gender and socioeconomic baselines for the target 11 countries

## Composite indices, presented in tables

Gender-transformative climate adaptation and mitigation action requires a thorough and context-driven gender baseline with appropriate data and indicators. Since this will be a funding facility and specific projects have not been identified, this study develops a derivative baseline.

Gender assessments will be carried out in the JEGA process as needed to add to the country profiles included in this document, as well as at the investment level. As described in section 3.4, a gender assessment is conducted for each of the 11 countries using the same methodology and indicators as part of the ESIA, where we adopt a gender sensitive approach, or a separate more detailed assessment where we consider a gender transformative approach.

At the outset, composite indices are used as indicative (and not conclusive) measures of current level of development. As the World Bank's research[2] shows, these indices present challenges as well as advantages. For example, numerous fallacies have been identified in the methodology and interpretation of composite indices. These indices are often based on empirical and aggregate measures of complex phenomena - making values apparently objective, at the cost of subjective nuances. Yet, these also remain invaluable as useful supplements to income-based development indicators - to understand relative degrees of development. Section 6 presents each index as a table, citing their relevance information for all 11 target countries.

## National aggregate statistics: Poverty, Education, Labour and Health, presented per country

For these Indicators, the data is presented in each country profile to ensure coherence and capture the full socioeconomic landscape where GCF, FMD and B4G investments may be directed. Each country profile includes short interpretation of the data, to contextualise the investment landscape for each country. For example, Brazil (see Section 7), the country profile includes available information on gender in health, education and labour markets. The country profile also includes brief gender analysis for the priority agro-commodity, such as cattle and soy in Brazil. The GCF, in its Mainstreaming Gender toolkit for climate change interventions and climate finance (developed with UN Women), also spotlights the chosen indices as well as the above indicators

# Composite Indices

## HDI - UNDP (2019)

The HDI scores presented here is from the 2020 Human Development Report. The Human Development Index (HDI) is a summary measure of achievements in three key dimensions of human development: a long and healthy life, access to knowledge and a decent standard of living. The HDI is the geometric mean of normalized indices for each of the three dimensions. This technical note describes the data sources, steps to calculating the HDI, and the methodology used to estimate missing values.

The HDI data presented here should be treated as indicative, not conclusive. It provides an overview of relative development status in a particular country but remains a "synthetic indicator". Indicators of human development have shown the need for consideration of other indicators associated with economic and social cohesion, sound development strategies, and sustainability in growth models.

# 7.2 Gender Inequality Index (GII)

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) reflects gender-based disadvantage in three dimensions—reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market—for many countries as data of reasonable quality allow. It shows the loss in potential human development due to inequality between female and male achievements in these dimensions. It ranges from 0, where women and men fare equally well, to 1, where there is no equality.

GII values are computed using the association-sensitive inequality measure suggested by Seth (2009), which takes into account the fact that gender inequalities are not independent across orders—the first aggregation is by a geometric mean across dimensions; these means, calculated separately for women and men, are then aggregated using a harmonic mean across genders.

GII, however, should be treated as indicative, not conclusive. Permanyer finds that the functional form of the GII is sensitive to the choice of weights used in its construction, and of relative performance of women vis-à-vis men, along with absolute women-specific indicators.

# 7.3 Gender Development Index (GDI)

The Gender Development Index (GDI) measures gender inequalities in achievement in three basic dimensions of human development: health, measured by female and male life expectancy at birth; education, measured by female and male expected years of school for children and female and male mean years of schooling for adults aged 25 years or older; and economic resources, measured by female and male estimated earned income. This further reiterates the results from the GII that gender inequalities are not independent across dimensions of human development achievements. Geiske Dijkstra and Hammer find that although gender-related development indices have increased attention towards “feminization of poverty and underdevelopment”, more robust
