# 8. Country Profiles

## 8.1 Brazil

Brazil is widely recognized as a key player in sustaining global food security, with the capacity to increase production of important agricultural commodities, such as soybean, sugar, cattle, and coffee—to meet surging international demand. In terms of gender, Brazil has taken concrete actions to integrate gender equality into the broader policy-making agenda in a participatory manner, through the National Council for Women (CNF), which is responsible for this agenda.

Given that Brazil is a geographically vast and diverse country, intersections of gender, ethnicity, and location are complex. This diversity can lead to significant disparities across groups, which has a cascading effect on agro-commodities and value chains.

A key gender barrier in Brazil is limited ownership and lack of control over land exercised by women and girls. The magnitude of the gender asset gap in Brazil, at the national level, is unknown. However, studies have shown that women are less likely to own or inherit land than men. Consistent advocacy from feminist organizations, such as Espaço Feminista—Municipal-level studies10 in Brazil have assessed gender-disaggregation of land ownership and found that only 12% of total agricultural holdings were owned by women.

In the state of Mato Grosso, a study was conducted to assess the extent to which land is found to be registered under women’s names. This implies, when women own land, their plots are smaller than those held by men. From this research in the municipality of Cuiabá, it was found that only 11% of female respondents were landowners as compared to 89% of male respondents. Enhanced gender inequality in land ownership can be posited to relate to male preference for land inheritance practices and lack of women’s rights in community.

For the B4Green Fund, particularly in the context of priority value chains: cattle and soy, these issues are relevant. Further, the Fund has two active JECAs11:

### Mato Grosso

Mato Grosso is Brazil's third largest state by area and the leading producer of soy, corn, cotton and cattle. The state is the main grain producer in the country and is expected to contribute with 30% of Brazil’s total agricultural exports (2018). In terms of gender equality, Mato Grosso ranks low in Brazilian states12, using the Global Gender Gap Index methodology (see section 6.4 for GGGI scores of the target countries). Mato Grosso faced in the bottom quintiles for economic participation and

## 8.1.1 Supply chain sectors Soy

The importance of the soy value chain to Brazil is demonstrated through a variety of data points:

Since 2000, Brazil has doubled its total area of soy plantation to 36 million hectares and is the reigning global leader in soybean production. In 2020/21, soybeans, along with other crops such as beans, animal feed and oil, generated a revenue of approximately $30 billion dollars, which was equivalent to 13% of the total annual export revenue. At the outset of the pandemic, demand for soy remained high and soya bean production in Brazil hit 133 million metric tons in the crop year of 2020/2021.

Demonstrably, soy is a crucial agro-commodity for the 250,000 soy producers in the country (as estimated by the Brazilian Association of Soybean Producers). The soy value chain generates revenue across 1500 municipalities. Federal, state and municipal governments benefit from soy revenues through different taxes, and soy is an important contributor to the Brazilian economy. Successively, soy production has been associated with a reduction in poverty rates and inequality; it was considered a precursor of socioeconomic development under certain conditions; however, it also tends to be associated with an increase in income inequality, especially in the agricultural frontier.

In the sub-index "economic participation and opportunity", three variables were adjusted because there is no data available for all municipalities. The first variable (female salary divided by male one) was served by the WEF to measure the ratio between women and men within the same position; the average female salary divided by the male one was used as a proxy of the gender pay gap in Brazil, which is 20% lower than that observed for the United States. The second variable (female employment rate divided by male employment rate) was served by ILO to measure the ratio between women and men within the same position; the total number of women in employment or self-employment divided by the total number of men in employment or self-employment represents the female employment rate for each municipality managers divided by the male counterpart.

In the sub-index "political empowerment", the variables of the variety of political opportunities, where parliamentarians were divided into three new variables, which represent the legislative power in Brazil: federal deputies and senators; state deputies and senators; and municipal deputies and senators. This same sub-index also included the presence or absence of a secretary dedicated exclusively to gender issues in each municipality. The variable "gender equality index" was served by UN Women to measure the greater role with other guidelines and it (dis)applies for units that do not include the gender role in any Secretary, which is the case of 10% of municipalities.

# Cattle

Brazil is the world’s largest beef exporter, exporting approximately one-fifth of its production, and the sector has a notable environmental footprint, linked to one-fifth of all commodity-driven deforestation across the tropics. Brazil is also the world's second largest producer of beef, with 2.5 million head slaughtered per month. Beef in Brazil can be divided into two categories: cattle that are finished on pasture and approximately 10–13% finished in feedlots. Although the cattle value chain has been one of the key drivers of improving rural livelihoods and developments, it is also associated with environmental degradation.

With some estimates showing that women make up about two-thirds of the 600 million subsistence livestock keepers worldwide, women’s roles in livestock production and the cattle value chain are critical. Women's livelihood activities are and can be influenced by the mutually constructed inter-relationships between their own gendered identities and those of other actors within the private sector companies. In Brazil, the cattle value chain is traditionally male-dominated and patriarchial (machismo culture), as industrial agribusinesses tend to employ men as skilled labor.

It is important to note that the growth will affect areas which are not being targeted by the 8Gm Fund. Farmers will expand north into the North and Northeast, especially in the Amazonia region formed by the Brazilian State of Transamazônia, Para, Amapá, Roraima, and Maranhão.

# 8.1.2 Latest Global Gender Gap Index score

Brazil has closed 9% on its last gender gap index and is now ranked 93rd globally. Only 1.25% of the Political Empowerment gender gap has been closed to date, ranking Brazil 108th on this edition's index, with a 4-rank drop since last year. There are very few women parliamentarians (11%), and only one woman in the cabinet. In 2021, there were no female ministers of finance and a woman has been in a head-of-state role for only five years of the last 50 (12%) gap closed to date.

Brazil's GGDI score in 2021 was 0.695, which means it ranks 25th at the regional level and 93rd globally. Sub-scores are as follows:

The World Bank* finds that Brazil’s 2014–2016 recession ended after eleven years of rapid poverty reduction. Recent poverty data show that it has disproportionately affected poor Brazilians, and an uneven recovery is evident in the fact that Brazilian women fared worse than they were before the crisis. Between 2014 and 2016, over 5.6 million Brazilians fell into poverty (defined as having less than $3.20 per day). The share of poor people living below $1.90/day increased from 17.7% to 20.1%. These figures are reflected in the analysis conducted for multidimensional poverty* in Brazil as well, which reveals the incidence and intensity of poverty in the country.

• Inequality is pervasive in Brazil, as indicated by the Gini coefficient*. Between 2010 and 2016, Brazil’s data on the degree of inequality in wealth distribution (based on the Gini coefficient) reached 53.9%, which is currently the highest in Latin America. Prior to 2010:

The OECD* finds that Brazil exhibits a relatively high share of its gross domestic product (GDP) in education, however, expenditure per student remains below the OECD average. Education and Literacy in Brazil have notable gender dynamics, as OECD data below reveals.

- Although women in Brazil are more likely to have attained tertiary education and vocational qualifications than men, this is not the case for OECD countries; men are more likely than women to pursue a vocational track. This is not the case in Brazil, where 56% of upper secondary vocational graduates in 2019 were women compared to the OECD average of 43%. Women also have better access to higher education and training programmes in Brazil, where they made up 54% of graduates (compared to 55% on average across OECD countries). In addition, women are more likely than men to pursue tertiary education and achieve tertiary education in all OECD countries. In Brazil, 27% of 23–34-year-old women had a tertiary qualification in 2018 compared to 28% of their male peers in the same age group.

In Brazil, the establishment and roll-out of the Sistema Único de Saúde - SUS is considered a success story in extending health care coverage to disadvantaged population groups. In principle, the SUS ensures that the entire population is formally covered by the public health sector, with equal access to services regardless of income or social status. However, despite the universal health coverage mandate of SUS has been a constant challenge in the country, the OECD finds, not helped by persistent inefficiencies in the use of resources in the Brazilian health system. Further, while Brazil has made significant progress in reducing maternal and child mortality rates, maternal health, Brazilian women continue to face significant challenges to their health, safety, and rights.

* Maternal mortality ratios or MMRs, depending on the region of Brazil, remain five to ten times higher than in countries with similar economies.** Brazil's MMR is 60 deaths per


---
**Page 185**
---

Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4S Fund

Page 181

100,000 live births, far above the rate of twenty maternal deaths per 100,000 live births that the WHO deems acceptable.

Abortion and reproductive rights: Brazil's strict abortion laws allow abortions in only certain cases (rape, incest, abnormality, or life-threatening pregnancy). Illegal abortions, which tend to be unsafe, accounted for the majority of the over 200,000 hospitalizations that were related to abortion-related complications in 2019.

COVID-19 and other disease outbreaks: Brazilian women have faced compounded risks during the COVID-19 outbreak. These include difficulties with childcare, poverty, domestic violence, and lack of access to healthcare services. Women working in the informal workforce are particularly made vulnerable, as the majority of workers on the front line of the public health system are female. Intersectionalities of class, race and ethnic inequalities have exacerbated these risks for women.

The COVID-19 pandemic has reproduced these dynamics. Additionally, Brazil has ongoing multiple epidemics such as dengue, Zika, measles and others, which continue to increase health burden and unpaid care work in a gendered and unequal fashion.

**Page 186**
---

Given the breadth of the Brazilian economy and geography, the labour market exhibits complex dynamics. The World Bank finds that the over-all gender gap in labour force participation and employment has not changed substantially over the last decade, but there are important differences by region. For example, labour force participation rates for women are highest in the South (60.7 percent) and lowest in the Northeast (43.7 percent). In 2008, a CEPAL study (see also Lottina y et al.) reviews found that discrimination against gender wage gap in Brazil is wide- and is linked to worsened poverty in the country. Additionally, the gender wage gap is racialized Afro-Brazilian women are more likely to be discriminated through lower pay for the same work.

• Linkage of household wealth and women's income opportunities has a significant impact on the performance of socioeconomic indicators at the household level, while introducing limits to decision-making and power within these contexts. For example, women make up 10% of the labour force in Brazil, but they earn only 42% of men’s earnings. Women work for more than a quarter less than men's earnings, and dedicate close to 75% more time to domestic and care work than men.

• Equality at work and invisible work: The sexual division of labour is also reflected in differences between men and women in the number of hours worked in paid work and domestic work. Women spend an average of 10.8 hours per week on paid work, compared with 7.5 hours for men; however, they spend more time (23.4 hours) on domestic work, which is four times as much as that reported by men (-5.5 hours per week).

In a time use study, the World Bank pegged unpaid care work at 11.6% of total life time for women and only 0.8% for men. This means that women spend more than twice as long on domestic work as men. The gender gap in unpaid care work is also reflected in women's earnings by limiting the time they spend on paid activities, and (often) by leading women to seek employment that has flexible or shorter hours (which, in turn, is likely to be found in the informal sector). This is particularly true for rural women: while 50% of urban women in agriculture is ostensibly lower than men (42.6% as compared to 12.8%), IFAD reports that 73% of rural women's labour is often unpaid. Therefore, women’s contributions to their households and terms of production and raising children as well as their work in agriculture and other economic sectors - are immense, but often invisibilised and not remunerated.

# 8.2 Cameroon

Cameroon is a major exporter of several agricultural commodities, which account for 34% of its total exports including cocoa beans, sawn wood, rubber, palm oil, cotton, bananas, maize and cassava. The primary sector contributes to approximately 17.4% of the country's GDP and employs about 43% of the country's active population. For the BGreen Fund, palm oil, rubber and plantation forestry are priority value chains in Cameroon, where the fund aims at establishing a JCEA agreement.

According to the National Gender Policy Document 2011-2020 by the Ministry of Women’s Empowerment, Social Affairs and Family, women make up about 54% of the total population. However, regional, ethnic and religious factors dominate and introduce variations in the gender status quo in Cameroon. There is, however, a persistent value of gender division of labour in which men take the primary responsibility for income generation while women are responsible for household chores. Women’s labour force participation rate is high and they contribute to household income. However, women's work tends to be undervalued as it is perceived as supplementary income of the husband. Women also face challenges related to access to land, credit, markets and income and tenure rights also remain a challenge - despite the role women play in ensuring national food security. The BGreen Fund will address these issues by providing financial support, while mainstreaming gender into established commodity supply chains in the country.

The BGreen Fund will address women’s specific challenges in palm oil, rubber and plantation forestry sector, including access to land, access to finance and to labour and non-labour inputs, to ensure smallholder women farmers can contribute to and benefit from sustainable intensification within these supply chains through its investment thesis.


## 8.2.1 Supply chain sectors

### Palm oil

Cameroon is the largest producer of palm oil in Central Africa yielding over 450,000 tons in 2020 with over 170,000 ha of land dedicated to this activity. The expansion of production in recent years has accelerated disproportionately with a recent study showing that 67% of all palm expansion from 2009-2014 occurred on previously forested land. Cameroon is the world’s third largest producer and a significant producer of palm oil, producing more than 2.6 million tonnes in 2020. It cannot keep


---
**Page 188**
---

# Rubber

Rubber exports account for around USD 38 million of Cameroonian exports (USD 28 million from natural dry rubber and USD 10 million from natural rubbers). The main private agro-industrial entities contributing to Cameroon's rubber production are Hevecam (54,000 ha) and Sudcam (45,000 ha), both owned by Halcyon Agri. Taking together, these represent the largest rubber plantations in the country.

Major export destinations are Europe and the U.S., and as production has expanded in recent years, so too has deforestation to clear the way for new plantations. In 2021, the national government launched a reforestation programme called "Planting for Tomorrow" with the aim of greening the country by providing support to small farmers and restoring and redoubling existing plantations to boost yields per hectare. This programme is expected to yield an additional 6,647 tonnes in 2023 and 15,890 tonnes in 2024.

Although specific case studies/reports on rubber and gender dynamics in Cameroon are not currently available, the evidence on women's participation and eventual benefits depending on diverse factors such as production systems, cultural norms, laws, policies and regulations has been mounting. The diversity of important roles that women play in rubber production and women coupled with the power relations in terms of access to and control over resources, pose a major challenge for Cameroonian women of the production of and benefits from cocoa, palm oil, rubber and timber.


---
**Page 189**
---

# Plantation forestry

Cameron has about 23 million hectares of dense rainforests, i.e., 46.25% of the national territory.[1] With this potential, Cameroon is one of the four main countries with forests in the Congo Basin. However, mining, agro-industries and other supply chains have affected extensive damage to these forests. The Ministry of Forests and Wildlife (Ministère des Forêts et de la Faune: MINFOW) recognizes that - to stem the above predicament - there is a crucial need for more innovative measures that will likely enable the country to achieve its sustainable development goals.

The National Forest Plantation Development Project (PNPFF) aims at reducing deforestation from supply chains important for the Cameroonian economy. For example, the two main timber markets in Cameroon are China and Europe. In 2019, Cameroon exported USD 400 million in sawn wood, mainly going to China (USD 76 billion), Belgium (USD 61 million) and Viet Nam (USD 56 million). To that effect, the National Plantation Forests Development Project (PNPFF) aims at reducing deforestation from supply chains important for the Cameroonian economy.

Research conducted by Pierre et al. (2002) presents a gender analysis of forest product markets in the Humid Forest Zone of Cameroon, which demonstrate that communiton benefits from a range of non-timber forest products as well. The study describes the functioning of the market and analyzing micro-economic factors influencing the production and marketing of forest products, including product specialization, and market strategies among traders.[2] Priorities to improve trade also show some gender differences in terms of access to markets, income levels, and profit margins between genders, indicating that trading efficiency is similar, and that, given the right conditions, women entrepreneurs can be as successful as men. The results also indicate that the gender gap could be closing among the younger generation entering the market - which is an important dividend for improving supply chains in the country.

## Latest Global Gender Gap Index score

Cameroon has closed 69.2% of its overall gender gap, achieving a rank of 96th globally. Only 20.2% of the Political Empowerment gender gap has been closed to date, ranking Cameroon 74th on this edition.[3] Women represent only 18.5% of parliamentarians (51 out of 283 seats). While women parliamentarians represent 33.9% of parliamentarians (51 of the 306 districts), there are only 14.9% of women in ministerial positions (17.5% of the gap closed) and no woman has been in a head-of-state role since 2018.[4]

The World Bank finds that the pace of progress on poverty reduction in Cameroon between 2001 and 2014 is slow largely due to the high rate of population growth.179 Poverty incidence is a largely rural phenomenon with a high degree of regional differences. While poverty incidence in the

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 187

North, North West and South West conflict-affected regions have slowly improved, it has largely stagnated in other areas of the country. An influx of shocks, domestic and international crises, including those caused by COVID-19, inflation, the Ukraine crisis and conflict (Boko Haram in the North), as well as climate change, have had a significant impact on the economy. Multidimensional poverty remains high in Cameroon with 89.5% of the population having limited access to basic services such as education, housing, clean water, and limited access to drinking water. Other features of poverty in Cameroon include:

- Inequality in Cameroon is high as indicated by the increase in the Gini index between 2007 (42.8) and 2014 (46.6).
- Women are largely confined to rural areas where they are smallholders and may find it difficult to improve their level of income because of being disadvantaged in terms of educational attainment, skills, knowledge, and access to lands and means of production. In addition, women have limited decision-making power within the household; they often lack autonomy to make decisions independently from their male family members and are burdened by time poverty (i.e., they have to cover agricultural work and domestic responsibilities).12

A World Bank study on Cameroon's health care system shows that public spending on health in Cameroon is low (1.5% of GDP) which is below the average for countries in sub-Saharan Africa.[19]

As a result, many of the health-care costs are borne by households out-of-pocket with high regional disparities and significant differences between urban and rural areas, reflecting the uneven wealth distribution. These regional disparities broadly reflect the poverty profile of the country and have distinct effects on the health of women:

- **Gender-based violence:** Cameroon has an estimated 34% of Cameroonian women between the ages of 15 and 49 having experienced physical violence[20], while 21% have experienced sexual and physical violence.[20]
- **Maternal mortality rate:** The maternal mortality ratio in Cameroon is at 529 deaths for 100,000 live births, which is above the 20 which the WHO uses as a threshold. There are also important differences with the North of the country hosting several displaced populations (who usually have lower levels of education and health care access). Women who had higher levels of education and being Muslim were protective against maternal mortality while 'domestic violence and ethnicity were associated with maternal death in the South' (increasing age and educational level decreased maternal mortality risk) [20].
- **Under-five mortality:** is largely driven by diseases such as malaria, pneumonia and diarrheas.[21]

Cameron spent 14.4% of its total government expenditures on education in 2020™ with progress occurring regarding decreasing illiteracy over the last decade. Despite these changes, an educational gap still persists between boys and girls and other challenges along the education pathway:

- Gross enrollment ratios are highly gender-differentiated with 81% of girls between 6 and 14 attending school against 94% of boys™. This can be attributed to a number of factors including poverty, which leads to women leaving school earlier in order to join the labour force; high rates of youth pregnancy and child marriage as well as traditional values which generally discourage girls from continuing their education beyond primary level.
- Schooling is intimately linked with data by UNFPA showing that 71% of child brides come from poor households while UNICEF shows that 31% of teenage girls are married before the age of 18™.

- Gaps in enrolment rates between the genders are still large for secondary education (64.6% for boys versus 55.4% for girls in 2016) but decreases when looking at tertiary education (13.1% for men and women respectively).

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The IEG Green Fund

Page 190

Access to employment particularly in the formal sector whether public or private remains challenging for Cameroonian women:

* In 2010-2011, the informal sector represented approximately 60% of jobs followed by the public sector (35%) and the private-sector form (3.7%). Women tend to be overrepresented in the informal sector (93.8%) against 87.5% for men particularly in the agricultural sector where wages and crop yields are lower.
* Men earn on average 20% more than women, a gap higher than those of women in other countries where wage gaps are less pronounced in the formal sector than in the informal sector. However, women tend to be under-represented in the formal private sector where there are twice as many men as women. In the public and private sectors, there is no gender gap.
* Unemployment as a percentage of the labour force is also higher for women (4.5%) than for men (3.8%).
* Results from a 2014 survey show that women spend 212 minutes a day on unpaid care work as opposed to 74 minutes for men with a large portion of this spent on unpaid domestic services for their own final use within the household.

# 8.3 Colombia

Colombia has the third largest economy in Latin America, and 80% of its 50 million inhabitants live in urban areas. In addition to deep-rooted social, economic and gender disparities, Colombia is currently the most unequal country in Latin America in terms of land distribution: 1% of landowners own 80% of the land. Women are also among the highest levels of gender-based violence in relation to the environment, particularly a track record of sexual violence against women defenders environmental human rights defenders, particularly women, which continues to increase since the peace process began.

Women defenders are actively social in the country, and they often tend to be small farmers, Indigenous or Afro-Colombian. They are concentrated in rural areas where households are under the poverty line, affected by the long conflict that has lasted for more than five decades. Gender identity is a significant factor in determining their vulnerability as an important factor according to gender assessments at localised programme and investment sites.

It is estimated that 70% of the food eaten in the country is produced by small farmers who can play a key role in adapting to climate change and climate adaptation through the efforts of the country’s agro-food chains including bananas, rice, maize, coffee, cocoa, palm oil and forestry. For the G4een Fund, cattle, coffee, cocoa, palm oil and forestry are priority value chains in the country, where the Fund has an active JCA.

## 8.3.1 Supply chain sectors

Colombia’s cattle sector is very broad, with a cattle inventory of approximately 28 million heads, distributed across 621,798 farms**, the fourth largest herd in Latin America. In 2017, livestock raising contributed to 1.4% of the country's GNP, generating 6% of national employment and 19% of agricultural exports. The cattle sector is dominated by small farmers (53%), followed by medium-sized farms (28%) and large farms (27%). 29% were classified as unmanaged**. While the sector holds a great economic value, studies suggest that expansion of cattle represents one of the biggest drivers of deforestation in the

## Country in 2019, approximately 85% of the total farmland was devoted to cattle (on average, 0.7 head of cattle per hectare). Cattle is also tightly linked with the land grabbing phenomenon, as reported by a recent investigation led by the Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (DARD), the National Institute for Hydrology, Meteorology and Environmental Studies (IDEAM), in the Northern departments of Guaviare, Vaupés, Putumayo, and Caquetá. According to IDEAM's report, published on 10 March 2020, an 80% increase compared with the same period in 2019. Moreover, Tapasco et al. (2019) note that the livestock sector produced 26% of Colombia’s total GHG emissions, with cattle accounting for approximately 75%.

There is also a key gender dimension to the cattle and livestock sector in Colombia: there seems to be a general gender pattern of labour allocation in the country, due to which men are responsible for farm and livestock activities, and women are assigned to household and care work. The study by Tapasco et al. (2019) found that 85% of men reported working on their farms, while only 37% of women reported livestock as their principal activity, indicating care work in their principal occupation. Women were more likely than men to report secondary occupations, such as domestic service and small-scale farming.

The impact on labour allocation was not gender neutral; with 80% of men reporting a negative trade-off between work and leisure time, against 60% of women reporting a negative trade-off between household and farm work, therefore juggling between different responsibilities. However, the results from the G4C Fund’s Sustainable Cattle Ranching project, have shown promising results of how the silvopastoral approach can improve the income and wellbeing of rural families, increasing women and internally displaced people, while also improving the sustainability of cattle practices.

It is, therefore, essential that investment and reforestation programs engaged in this value chain, such as the G4C Fund, consider the gender disparities that affect the sector. This includes addressing the significant disparity in terms of time spent, and by enhancing awareness and equal access to technical knowledge of sustainable cattle practices, to improve environmental as well as gender outcomes.

# Palm oil

Colombia is the largest producer of palm oil in Latin America, as well as the fourth largest and fastest growing producer in the world. According to the National Federation of Oil Palm Growers (FEDERPALMA), palm oil is the fourth largest export in the agricultural sector and creates 10% of Colombia's GDP. These data points underscore the importance of this value chain for the Colombian economy.

The palm oil value chain is, apparently, less responsible for deforestation in Colombia as in other countries (Indonesia and Malaysia) according to the Colombia’s Institute of Hydrology, Meteorology and Environmental Studies (IDEAM). Only 1% of the total area under palm oil production was affected by forest fires in 2017. This is related to palm oil production, and overall share of certified palm oil increased from 5% in 2014 to 38% in 2019. Moreover, after a complex tripartite label system (FSC, RSPO) there has been a significant increase in the number of producers and job formalization within the sector.

In addition, palm oil companies have been accused water and air contamination[1], with severe negative effects on the health of nearby communities. Contamination has a significant, negative cascading effect on women, generally tasked with collection of water and household care, increasing their workload.

In addition, women are generally discriminated against in the palm oil sector at the global level; they have fewer opportunities and on-plantation compared with men, they usually work in lower status and lower paid positions, and are much more likely to be employed informally. Limiting access to education and training for women also limits their ability to participate fully in the sector also causes them to undertake dangerous tasks with little or no protection, exposing them to risks and harm.[2]

Considering these factors, the BGFund will activate solutions to address these disparities and gaps, strengthening inclusion in the palm oil value chain. This will ensure that current inequality is not further exacerbated, and, in fact, will be committed to advance the human rights of all workers and communities involved in the palm oil value chain. # Coffee

Coffee is the third largest coffee producer in the world, behind Brazil and Vietnam. Around 86% of the coffee is exported as green coffee, while the remaining production is roasted and consumed locally. Colombia is also an important producer of certified coffees with two certification programmes, among which Colombia is the second-largest producer of Rainforest Alliance-certified coffee in the world.

The coffee sector has been a key player in the Colombian economy, but it has been facing increasing economic challenges: between 2017 and 2019, Colombia lost nearly 100,000 acres of coffee plantations (more than 5% of its total area) due to extreme weather events. The Coffee Producers Federation of Cauca Growers. The decreasing sunlight time, extreme weather events and spread of fungal diseases and insects have hit the sector. Out of approximately 500,000 farms, 95% are smaller than 10 hectares.

A study conducted on gender relations in the coffee value chain highlights strong perception of gender roles, with ingrained conceptions about femininity and masculinity, identified by respondents as sound reasoning to assign "frighten" tasks to women and to justify "belonging" of women to the household. Women are responsible for most of the domestic work, such as preparation of meals for workers, small livestock breeding. Other tasks include: coffee bean selection; record-keeping, with the perception of women being more organized and having more time.

In other words, the roles in the farm mirror the roles in the household as well as within the community. It is also reported that, while the farm is considered family property, the formal owner is almost exclusively a man, unless the woman received inheritance or were specifically assigned by her husband. The study concludes that women are also less involved than men with access resources and income generated by the production, as well as their involvement in decision-making processes related to farming practices; women are also less involved in technical and training activities and opportunities.

However, there is evidence that women-centred projects linked with coffee production (such as the positive case of the Asociación de Mujeres Cafeteras del Occidente del Cauca, or the Association of Rural Women Agriculturists, supported by UN Women) have a positive impact on

## Cocca

While cocoa has long been cultivated and consumed domestically, Colombia is the 10th largest producer globally (as of Jan 13 market share). In 2020, there were 52,000 production families, 11,448 farms exported and 63,416 produced, with 165,000 jobs created.

In 2018 Colombia signed up for the Cocoa and Forest Initiative, with the commitment of ending deforestation in the cocoa sector. The G4Green Fund has been working to support this initiative through its project Tropical Agriculture (CAT). This found that cocoa cultivation is not linked with deforestation, but that its production is, in many cases, associated with other crops such as coffee and sugar cane, which also happen to have a high rate of deforestation. Cocoa could be an agent of forest conservation, while also serving peace as an alternative income from illicit cultivations. However, most cocoa farms in Colombia are small-scale family farms, and the majority of women are not involved in cocoa production. There needs to be developed and made profitable in order to exploit its environmental and social benefits.

As in all other value chain analyzed, the strong perception of gender roles defines the participation of men and women in cocoa cultivation. Women are excluded from productive spaces that excludes them from productive and community spaces, causing an imbalance: participation in the productive space is only 22% for women, compared to 58.4% for men; while men participate in the decision-making process more than twice as much (60%) as women (27%). This gender balance needs to be less imbalanced, with an almost equal representation, which might be a way to reduce gender violence and the role of women in the cocoa sector has proven to be essential for advancing sustainability.

- They consistently and creatively adopt technical recommendations to improve crop productivity and sustainability;
- They share knowledge acquired in training sessions on the technical, environmental and social aspects of cultivation to their families and the wider community; and,

## Plantation forestry

A study conducted by GEAM in 2017[30] found that almost 50% of all timber produced in Colombia is illegally sourced. Colombia holds the potential to plant 24 million hectares of forests, against the $11,000 that have currently been planted[30]; there is therefore considerable growth potential in this sector.

The GGreen Fund has also worked with the Colombian government on the Forest Pact for Legal Timber (PRLM), which was supported by FAO EU Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) programme and involved the Colombian Ministry for Environment (MCDS). The PRLM is a multi-stakeholder agreement between the public sector, civil society, public and private sector organizations, and has become a model sustainable forestry in the region.

Plantation forests require intensive management, but they grow faster than natural forests; in 2011 FAO reported that although they accounted only for 5% of the total wood forest area, it produced 35% of all the world’s wood product.[30] Forestry and forest management, however, is not gender-neutral. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes due to their underrepresentation, resulting in women having less access and control over forest resources and economic opportunities.[31] However, women are powerful agents of forests conservation and management; they have a different approach to forestry than men: less focused on income (timber, mining, monocultures) and more on agroecology, including food security, medicinal plants, and transmission of invaluable knowledge from one generation to another.[32]

The GGreen Fund will therefore mainstream gender into the forestry value chain, maximizing the impact women can have on conservation and sustainable exploitation of plantation forests, as well as advancing their participation in decision-making, access to resources and efforts for land ownership.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 108

The World Bank[1] finds that monetary poverty increased in Colombia from 34.7% in 2018 to 35.7% in 2019, with an additional 662,000 thousand people falling into poverty. Inequality continued to increase, as the GINI index[2] reached 52.7 in 2019: among the highest in Latin America.

* Colombia is one of the most unequal countries in the world, with a GINI index of 52.7 and income inequality historically very high in Colombia.
* According to the World Bank, in 2017, 10% of the population received 39% of national income; by 2018 this figure had fallen to 10%, but still represents a significant portion of Colombia's income distribution. Despite economic growth, there is still positive compared to the reduction in average income per capita.
* In rural areas, where poverty is more concentrated than in urban centers, female poverty remains higher: 49.4% compared with 44.6%.
* Income inequality causes significant social and economic hurdles, and it can impede upward mobility for women and girls. Women are disproportionately affected by poverty, violence against women, the poor, indigenous groups and children.[3] It is important to remember how in the case of Colombia, as indicated at the beginning: intersectionsality can aggravate inequalities.
* In the last decade, the gender gap in employment remains a challenge, with higher unemployment rates and lower earnings for women.

Female-headed households have historically experienced increased levels of poverty compared to male-headed households, especially in rural areas.[4] The situation has been aggravated by the pandemic; poverty in 2019 was 58.3% in 2019, and it rose to 60.7% in 2020. In comparison, in the same period, poverty among male-headed households increased by 5.7%.

## Colombia has made significant progress in the field of education, as observed by the World Bank: the literacy gender gap has been negligible in the last two decades, and women now outnumber men in the enrollment rate for secondary and tertiary education. The OECD finds that Colombia's female literacy rate is 95% compared to a male literacy rate of 94%, which is higher than the OECD average (4.9% in 2018). However, expenditure per student remains significantly lower among all educational levels compared with OECD average. Gender dynamics can be observed in the education system.

### In Colombia, the proportion of children from the bottom quartile of the PISA index of economic, social and cultural status (ESCS) achieving at least PISA level 2 in reading in 2018 was 56% lower than that of children from the top ESCS quartile, a larger share than the OECD average.

### In Colombia, Young women are less likely to be employed than young men, particularly those with low levels of education. Only 37% of women aged between 15 and 24 years old who were upper secondary attainment were employed in 2020 compared to 82% of men in Colombia. This gender difference is larger than the average across OECD countries, where 43% of women and 69% of men with below upper secondary attainment are employed.

Colombia adopted a healthcare system based on universality in 1993. Since then, citizens are obliged to join one of two possible insurance schemes: a contributory plan for employees and self-employed workers with contributions subject to a subsidy from the government; or an informal workers and low-income self-employed scheme. Members of the two schemes are entitled to the same benefits, with a single service package available. The system had a positive impact on maternal mortality in Colombia between 1993 and 2005, but since then, both maternal and neonatal mortality rates have fallen by 40%. However, there are significant challenges and risks for women and girls in Colombia:

## Colombian labour market was hit harshly by the pandemic, and its effect were felt in particular in the urban centres, where is concentrated 80% of the overall country population. While some of the damage to women's employment has been reversed since March 2021, the gender gap remains significant compared to pre-the pandemic employment levels.

### Women's unemployment has historically been higher in Colombia, and women were particularly affected by the pandemic shock to the labour market: In March 2022, unemployment rate was 16.5% for women, compared with 10.3% for men.

#### The World Bank[24] finds that civil status, children's education and economic background are all factors in determining employment rates. Women who were in a union (divorced, widowed or separated) are most likely to be working (75.6% of all women workers), followed by women in a union (57.8%) and by single women (38.5%). Women without children are more likely to work than those with one child (49%), but this decreases with number of children. Women who have completed tertiary or primary education are much more likely to find work than women who complete the secondary cycle (64% vs 20%). Women in the lowest income quintile (18%) are less likely to be working compared from the highest income quintile (61.2%) are also found to be working more frequently than those from the lowest quintile (39.7%).

#### According to the National Survey on Time Use[25], women spent on average 7 hours and 46 minutes per day in work, compared with 8 hours and 6 minutes for men. The World Bank, in 2017, estimated women dedicate approximately 53% of their time to unpaid care work, compared with 29% for men. In 2017, it was estimated that unpaid care work amounted to as much as $48 billion per year in Colombia.

#### The amount and type of unpaid care work influences the level of participation to work and to community life, access to economic opportunities and social inclusion.

# 8.4 Côte d'Ivoire

Côte d'Ivoire is the world’s largest producer and exporter of cocoa (30% of the world production), one of the three biggest producer and exporter of cashew, and a major exporter of palm oil, cotton and coffee. The country's economy is mainly based on agriculture. The primary sector contributes USD 12 billion to GDP, or 21%, and employs 40% of the country’s active population. For the G6Green Fund, all palm oil producers are part of the value chains in Côte d'Ivoire, where the fund aims at establishing a JICA agreement.

## 8.4.1 Supply chain sectors

### Palm Oil

Côte d’Ivorie is West Africa’s second largest palm oil producer after Nigeria and eighth-largest overall. After annual production fell from 45,000 tonnes in 2010 to 360,000 in 2012, the country has successfully stabilised its annual production at around 400,000 tonnes per year, with local news reporting that the industry is worth CFAF58bn (£82m) and affects upwards of 2m Ivorians. The government’s National Agricultural Investment Programme (PNIA) aims to increase palm oil plantations responsible for up to 60% of the country’s production, through the National Agricultural Investment Programme (Programme National d’Investissement Agricole, PNIA). Launched in 2016 for the period 2017-2025, the PNIA aims at increasing production from around 400,000 tonnes per year to 600,000 tonnes.

As the fourth-highest income generator in the Ivorian economy, the palm oil industry is poised for growth over the next few years, thanks in large part to the government’s PNIA. Home to Africa's largest palm oil producers, Côte d'Ivoire has a unique advantage compared with other major producers located in villages as opposed to Indonesia or Malaysia, where large-scale industrial plantations are more common. Through investment from the government, Côte d’Ivoire’s palm oil industry will be able to compete with larger producers in other countries and, given its proximity to European countries, a market very much in demand given observed shortages.

Approximately 85% of palm oil plantations are between one to five hectares, highlighting the importance of small-scale production. The remaining 15%, or “large scale” of Ivorian palm comes from small village plantations. Ranging in size from 3-5 ha, small plantations are a strong source of sustainable employment for Ivorians. The remaining 40% of production is handled by large-scale industrial producers, which account for the lion’s share of the country's total output and its largest palm oil producers. Such industrial groups include Dekeoil and SIFCA. SIFCA is one of the largest producers of palm oil in West Africa; its local subsidiary, PALMCO, holds 20,000 ha of land for industrial palm oil production. The company also has a strong presence in small-scale production through its village plantations, enabling it to account for the production of up to 300,000 tonnes of palm oil per year.

Palm oil is a key commodity for women in Côte d’Ivoire; traditionally, it is them who manually produce it, with some growers selling part of their produce to artisanal women processors. *A*

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

recent study[26] found that the productivity gender gap related to export crops, including palm oil, has reduced significantly between 2008 and 2016, to the point of being nearly closed. While this gain is partly driven by specific efforts by the Government to include women in agricultural production activities, it also reflects a general trend towards more equitable land distribution within female-headed households; the gender gap reduced also due to lower landholdings by female headed households.

The cocoa sector faces similar challenges as palm oil: women are underrepresented at all levels of decision-making and face significant challenges in land tenure. The 2015/16 census showed that only 8% of women hold a land title, compared with 22% of men. Moreover, while widow’s right to inherit land is protected by law, actual inheritance practice varies geographically.[27]

## Cocoa

At the world's largest cocoa grower, Côte d'Ivoire relies heavily on the commodity as a key economic export and major contributor to GDP. In 2020, cocoa accounted for nearly USD 4 billion export revenue. Recent research from UNDP found that the introduction and expansion of agroforestry measures in cocoa production areas has increased women's landholdings by 15%, doubling it to 20% by 2030.[28] Moreover, the World Cocoa Foundation found that agroforestry measures such as introduction or increase of forest trees on 1.8 million hectares of low shade cocoa farms could help achieve the goal of increasing forest cover from less than one million hectares across cocoa, oil palm and rubber combined[29].

The cocoa value chain is gendered in Côte d'Ivoire; an assessment led by Fair Labor Association found that women are underrepresented at all levels of decision-making. Women face significant challenges: Women have a crucial role throughout the production chain, from field creation up to the drying process, which goes largely unrecognized in the Ivorian society. In fact, women have considerable knowledge and skills related to cocoa production but their contributions are also uncompensated, lack adequate access to representation and collective bargaining, as well as to proper health and safety equipment. This is compounded by risks posed to health by hard physical labor, long working hours (most women interviewed reported working on average 16 hours a day) and insufficient access to water and sanitation facilities. In addition, the cocoa-producing household has a high illiteracy rate (80%), and that, plus traditional gender norms within the household, her husband, does not contribute to women's access to technical and educational opportunities, as well.

The G4Green Fund will take these barriers and challenges into account, to fully acknowledge women’s role in the cocoa supply chain, supporting efforts to increase access to land, training and inputs, supporting a safe and equal division of labor. Community-based initiatives to raise awareness of women's rights, equal decision-making and fair-participation will also be explored.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 207

Cote d'Ivoire has experienced sustained economic growth in the last decade, which has been linked to considerable poverty and inequality reduction. Poverty (at national poverty level) went from 44.4 in 2015 to 39.5 in 2018. Similarly, inequality has also decreased, with the Gini Index going from 47.6 in 2015 to 45.8 in 2018. However, despite these improvements, poverty is mostly driven by retails and construction, while employment remains concentrated in agriculture and non-agriculture self-employed occupations, mostly taken by women with no formal occupation.

- In 2013, the World Bank reported[268] official statistics data which indicated 75% of rural women lived in poverty. Gender inequalities, including lack of or insufficient access to land, education, and financial services, as well as adherence to traditional gender norms and roles, all contribute to prevent women from escaping poverty.

- As observed in a recent study on agricultural gender gap for the World Bank, "households headed by women are more likely to be food insecure than those headed by men. This is true though they use more female and child household laborers. The dependency ratio in female-headed households is approximately 39 percent higher; they also use less fertilizer and pesticides and are 38 percentage points less likely to be engaged in export crop cultivation"[269].

Côte d'Ivoire has invested in education, and has witnessed improved outcomes in the last decade. However, the education gender gap persists, and women and girls encounter numerous challenges in their educational pathways.

- According to official government data, while progress has been made since 2011, data in 2018 still showed a considerable gap in terms of enrolment and completion throughout the school cycle.
- The gross primary school enrolment rate for boys was 95.3%, compared with only 74% for girls; the net primary school enrolment rate for girls in primary education in 2018 was 82.3%, compared with only 34.6% for the first cycle of secondary school, where girls represent 44% of students, for a gender parity gap of 59.6.
- Quality of education is also a key concern, and tightly linked with poor performance: in 2021 the achievement rate of girls at the primary cycle exam (CEPE) was 53.1%, whereas at the secondary level it was only 48% (CEPES). The average score for boys on CEPES is 29.6, while that for girls is 27.5.
- 29.4% of girls managed to obtain their Baccalauréat certificate (high school diploma).
- While access to education has improved in recent years, the quality and relevance of education still skewed towards jobs and topics that are perceived as "feminine". According to UNESCO, only 16% female students in Côte d'Ivoire are enrolled in STEM faculties, and boys perform better than girls in math both in early and in late primary. Financial literacy is also a key obstacle: 40% of women are numerate, compared with 92% of men.

The healthcare system in Côte d'Ivoire has suffered from years of conflict and political instability, which destroyed 42% of health facilities. While it was on the mend, then, the country was also significantly affected by the COVID-19 pandemic. However, improving health outcomes is one of the focus areas for the G4 Fund. The maternal mortality ratio in Côte d'Ivoire has decreased from an increase from 1.8 billion USD in 2016 to 2.3 billion USD in 2021.[35] Constraints and challenges remain for women and girls.

- Maternal mortality ratios: **MRs**: MMR in Côte d’Ivoire is 617 deaths per 100,000 live births (2017), the highest in the world; only 74% of births are attended by skilled medical personnel[35], and this ratio is remarkably higher in rural areas compared with urban areas. In 2019, Côte d'Ivoire reported 278,801 unattended births in rural areas, compared with 12,538 in urban areas.[34]

- Abortion and SRHR: Abortion is illegal in Côte d’Ivoire, except to save a woman’s life. Unsafe abortion is the leading cause of maternal mortality in Côte d'Ivoire.

A recent study[36] found that the average one-year likely-abortion incidence in Côte d'Ivoire was 27.1 per 1000 women of reproductive age for respondents and 48.7 per 1,000 based on a sample of 590 women. The abortion rate among women aged 15–19 years is classified as most unsafe: 71.5% for respondents and 79.5% for confidants. Among both groups, women living in rural areas, women with lower levels of education and poorer women are more likely to have had an abortion.

The prevalence for all women aged 15–49 is 22-50%, and the adolescent fertility rate is very high because many girls do not complete their primary or secondary school. A significant increase from the previous monitoring (2007/2008), when 1,292 became pregnant.[36]

Access to employment and economic empowerment is challenging for Ivorian women. They face discrimination and a number of obstacles in all productive sectors:

* According to official data, in 2014 women's salaries were on average 125 USD, compared with 240 USD for men - a 50% wage gap.[38] Participation is also a key issue, as women represented less than 50% of the labour force in 2016. Ivorian working women tend to reside in urban areas and are more likely to work in informal sectors or in rural areas.

* Agriculture is the sector in which women tend to be employed (most (42%), followed by retail (31%) and domestic work (9%)).[38] Access to land, training and education, as well as male dominance in the cultivation of cash crops are key obstacles to full economic empowerment for Ivorian women. Social norms and beliefs, and again specific training and

education, are some of the challenges women face when they try to enter the industrial sector, which is a key driver of the country's economic growth and yet still employs a very small percentage of the population.**69**

Women represent only 30% of employed individuals in Côte d'Ivoire: the vast majority of these enterprises are micro-businesses, with limited potential for growth. Access to financial services is also an issue. Women are four times more likely than men to have no bank account and be as likely to have an account in a bank or a formal non-bank institution, and 4 times more likely to be an active user of a bank account. On the other hand, women are 50% more likely to have an account with a mobile money provider compared to men, but only 12% use it at a non-bank institution.**70** Moreover, while mobile banking drove an increase in financially involved adults (from 34% in 2014 to 41% in 2017), women are 45% less likely to own a mobile account and 6 times more likely to be unaware of mobile money providers. This is reflected in a gender gap in mobile ownership, which is 15% at the national level and 23% in rural areas.**71**

See next page for Section 8.5 on the Democratic Republic of Congo.

8.5 Democratic Republic of Congo

The Democratic Republic of Congo (hereafter DRC) is the largest country in Sub-Saharan Africa, and it is estimated to have its third largest population of poor people globally. The country is rich in resources, including minerals, metals and agriculture commodity. Agriculture, forestry and fisheries account for approximately 26% of GDP and employ 64% of the population. Key crops include cocoa, coffee, rice, palm oil and cassava.

For the G8 Green Fund, plantation forestry and grains staples are priority value chains in DRC, where the fund aims at establishing a SCA agreement.


### Supply chain sectors

#### Plantation Forestry

60% of the Basin Congo, Africa's most important rainforest, is located in DRC. While it is mostly untouched, production of forestry resources is often the only source of livelihood for many of the 45 million poor people (1999) who live within the country. DRC is one of the least electrified countries in Sub-Saharan Africa and has a high dependence on firewood as its primary energy source for domestic energy consumption. The need for plantation forestry, which can provide wood for biomass projects, as well as create livelihoods and jobs opportunities for local populations, is therefore very high.

In 2019, the Government of DRC signed an agreement with the European Union and the European Union in the Yangambi Biosphere Reserve, achieved the plantation of 1 million tree in 2021, with a goal of restaining 2000 hectares of land by 2022. Since the beginning of the project, more than 3500 ha have been planted.

Forestry, as in many other countries, is gendered in DRC. Access to and ownership of land is severely restricted to women: formal land titles giving access to individual ownership are rare, and property rights are often based on customary systems. Women's rights to land are generally attributed to the head of household, who are stipulated as having to be a male. In the case of customary systems, land is usually attributed through belonging to a clan or lineage, which is established by men, as women do not have access to this system. This means that, in many cases, it is not women but their sons and grandsons who inherit land. Furthermore, that the inter-generational transfer of land takes place through patrilineal inheritance: women who go and live with the husband's family cultivate their land, but they do not inherit or acquire the rights to this land.

Division of labour and allocation of time is also influenced by gender norms, with women confined to subsistence crops, while men monopolise cash crops, and the income they produce. Women are also tasked with chores that are considered physically lighter, but more constant and time-consuming.

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

## Grains and staples

As one of the largest countries in the world by area, including the second largest in Africa, it produces an enormous volume of agricultural commodities, with half of its consumed domestic food produced on smallholder farms. In 2019, the country had a total of 3 million hectares of arable land were produced in 2020 and 2.3 million tonnes of rice across 1.3 million hectares, yet neither commodity is an export revenue generator.

In spite of the significant production, the sector falls to ensure food security and generate sufficient income for smallholder farmers. With millions of hectares of arable land, one million hectares of irrigated land, and many rivers with important fishery resources, DRC has the potential to become a global agricultural power. For the moment, though, the sector lacks commercial agriculture at scale with modern inputs either in production or distribution.

A large proportion of Congolese women are involved in agricultural activity (more than 70% at the national level), but they are significantly discriminated within the sector. As discussed above, women face a number of barriers to accessing land and credit, including their status as landless smallholders in DRC, as well as accessing financial services: only 25% of women manage to access credit, which leaves 42% of women taking loans from friends and family, to be refunded with exorbitant interest rates. Women are also underrepresented in the agricultural extension labour, and are systematically excluded from cash crops - they might play a role in the production chain, but have no access to the income generated.

Several programmes and projects have focused on the last few years on improving the living conditions and livelihood opportunities for women farmers in DRC, such as the UNHOMWEN flagship programme "Women's Empowerment through Climate-Smart Agriculture". The programme was launched in 2019 with a budget of $5.4 million to support 37,000 smallholder women farmers in DRC increase women farmers' access to finance, increase women farmers' access to higher value markets." The G4Green Fund will leverage on the progress achieved so far, and will continue to work on the issues and challenges that women farmers face on a daily basis in DRC, with a focus on those four areas.

Poverty remains widespread and pervasive in DRC. The World Bank estimates that despite strong economic growth led to a 2% decline in the poverty rate between 2010 and 2012, high rate of population growth and marked regional disparities indicate poverty incidence is still very high: one in six poor persons in Sub-Saharan Africa live in DRC. Inequality is also very high, and virtually unchanged over time.

The healthcare system in DRC has been under long-term pressure due to conflict, limited resources, insufficient allied medical personnel and medical facilities and equipment. While improvements have been noted in the last few years, thanks to a higher level of stability, many constraints remain, in particular for women and girls:

- Maternal mortality ratios or MMR: MMR in DRC is 437 per 100,000 live births, which is lower than the SSA average (528) but still high. The goal set by the government of DRC is to reduce MMR to less than 70 deaths for every 100,000 live births. Under-5 mortality rate is also higher than the SSA average: at 63 deaths for 1,000 live births against 76. A study reports that only about one-third of women in DRC have access to adequate and timely maternal healthcare coverage, particularly among the poorest women.[28]

- Abortion: The abortion rate is very high due to lack of family planning services. This means safe termination is largely inaccessible throughout the country. A study estimated that in 2015, 146,700 abortions took place in Kinshasa. In the same year, 33,870 women received treatment for complications from unsafe abortion at public and private facilities. Poor women were found to be twice as likely to develop moderate and serious complications than better-off women.[29] USAID estimated that in 2019 approximately one-third of all pregnancies among women aged between 15 and 49 years old wanted to avoid pregnancy but were not using modern contraception methods.[30]

- Adolescence: Fertility rate is also very high in DRC, according to the National Institute of Statistics. In the country, the crude birth rate for girls aged 15–19 years old, and the median age at first sexual intercourse is 16. Almost 27% of women aged 20 to 24 have given birth by age 18, and almost 35% by age 15, according to the Demographic and Health Survey 2013/2014.[31]

## Women in DRC experience a significant gender gap in accessing economic opportunities, economic independence and empowerment.

Nationality: women are 6.25 to 8.25 less likely to work than their male counterparts, partly because of the higher number of dependants and, therefore, unpaid care work performed by women. This results in lower earnings and lower income.

- Women who farm agriculture are more than half of the workforce, and yet men own three quarters of the plots. Women also tend to own smaller plots, which contributes to lower agricultural productivity, estimated to 11% lower than men, for an overall level of production 18% lower.
- The female dependency ratio is higher (205% compared with 143%), the female dependency ratio, lower ownership of land and cell phones, lower number of workers and lower capital equipment per worker in agriculture.

In the private sector, women's business profits are 67% lower than men’s, and women's business have 70% fewer paid workers. Lower number of workers and low level of capital equipment per worker in agriculture.

Less than 10% of women are wage and salaried, compared with 25% of men. In terms of wage gap, the national average gap in earning is 77%, and even more pronounced in the private sector (83%). Women have lower educational attainment, lower level of educational attainment, skills and concentration in lower paying sectors.

# 8.6 Ecuador

Ecuador is a middle-income country which has made significant progress to reduce poverty and inequality in education: the GDP grew on average by 4.3% between 2006 and 2014, poverty went from 37.6% to 22.5%, and the GINI coefficient decreased from 0.54 to 0.47. However, the COVID-19 pandemic and its fluctuation in oil prices, of which Ecuador is a major exporter (the sixth largest in Latin America), have had a negative impact on income growth: after 2030, reduce 37%. IFAD estimates that rural population still accounts to approximately 36.5% of the total, and that 61% of rural women works in agriculture and livestock. Agriculture, forestry and fishing represent 9.8% of GDP and 40% of employment.

Ecuador is the largest exporter in the world, as well as maize, manioc, potato, soybean, coffee, palm oil, cocoa and more. For the G6Green Fund, forestry is a priority value chain in the country, where the Fund has an active JICA.

## 8.6.1 Supply chain sectors

### Plantation forestry

Ecuador is one of the 17 megadiverse countries of the world, and its forest cover almost 13 million hectares - by comparison, an area larger than Greece.240 Forests can be divided into 4 main groups: Amazon rainforests (approximately 80% of the total), mountainous forests, tropical coastal rainforests and mangroves.

Out of the total forest coverage, 11.5 million hectares constitute natural forest and 78,000 hectares plantations, representing 42% of the total national surface area.240 Despite its rich biodiversity, deforestation in Ecuador is 1.35 between 1990 and 2005 and increased to 1.9 between 2005 and 2010 due to various factors: urbanization, industries, cattle ranching and agricultural expansion. Approximately 2 million m³ of industrial wood are produced in Ecuador per year, with a value of US$348 million in exportation growth.240 The timber industry amounted in 2019 to $304 million USD of exportations.

The main activity of women in rural areas and, in particular in the Amazon, is agriculture and livestock; they maintain a tight link with their territory and, as observed in other countries, they lead community development. According to the National Institute for Statistics (INEC), it was estimated that rural women poverty rate was 35.1%, against the 15.5% national average, and while they are present in productive processes, they are excluded from decision-making and access to

The World Bank** finds that the combination of three crises (COVID-19, a fall in oil prices and constraints in accessing international financing) caused Ecuador to lose on the last decades gains in terms of gender equality. The report shows that preliminary results of economic analysis indicated poverty would increase from 35% to 40%, and multidimensional poverty would rise from 47.3 to 50.

The COVID-19 pandemic had catastrophic consequences on the labour market and on access to social services, with unemployment going from 3.8% in December 2018 to 13.3% in May/June 2020, and three quarters of families reporting a fall in their income.

* Multidimensional poverty**: In the post-2015 SDG and Agenda 2030 Framework, SDG 1 targets poverty elimination. In all countries, multidimensional poverty is more prevalent than monetary poverty. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has developed a methodology to measure multidimensional poverty in line with the Sustainable Development Initiative (SDI), an important tool it uses. It complements the international poverty line statistics by statistical offices and other organizations.

According to IFAD, the poverty rate in rural areas in 2018 stood at 43%, almost triple than in urban areas (15.9%). This is attributable to high unemployment and underemployment, low salaries, limited access to productive assets such as land, water, credit, and technology, and deficient social services.

There is a direct correlation between female-headed households and poverty, as female participation in the labour force is usually less stable, informal, less paid with and less access to associated benefits. The Rural Income and Wealth Study (RIWS) associated with no additional income from their partners or former partners, leading to employment for work outside of the home. # Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 223

Gender gaps in terms of enrolment and attainment are very small in Ecuador, but there are still challenges for women and girls, especially indigenous women and women living in rural areas, to access and complete their education:

- The World Bank reports that the reverse gender gap exists favouring girls in primary and secondary education, while slightly more men enroll in tertiary education than women. The gap is reversed, however, at attainment level, where 14.2% of women compared with 12.5% of men have completed tertiary education.

- Up to 51% of indigenous girls are not enrolled in secondary education, and there is a large disparity that favours men in secondary enrolment rates.[38]

- Women are underrepresented in the following fields: law, education, social work, health, economy and finance), whereas three quarters of science and engineering students are men. Moreover, reasons to not be in school are very different between boys and girls; while the former cite lack of interest or problems as main constraints, the latter also report dedication to household chores as equally important. Girls also indicate family prohibition as a reason, which boys do not report.[38]

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The BGreen Fund

Page 234

Between 2007 and 2017 Ecuador initiated an ambitious healthcare reform, introducing comprehensive insurance policies, gradually making all Ministry of Health services free of charge.**46** Health coverage was expanded, especially in rural areas, and health inequality reduction could be observed.**46** However, critics notice that the cost recovery mechanisms established by the new law were not fully implemented, leading to a decrease in public spending on healthcare and that the opportunity to establish a single health system was gradually fading.**47** There is a clear gendered dimension to access to health, despite significant gains in the last two decades.

- **Maternal mortality rates or MMRs:** Ecuador has been very successful in lowering maternal mortality from 96 deaths per 100,000 births in 1978 to 25 in 2018. Access to antenatal care and prenatal care services have improved significantly, reducing the gap between indigenous and non-indigenous women. In 2018, MMR for indigenous women was 117 deaths per 100,000 births, due to a variety of reasons, including that indigenous women are more likely than non-indigenous women to give birth at home or in traditional birthing practices.*48* By 2014, only 14% of indigenous women received the appropriate minimum five antenatal check-ups.**49**

- **Abortion:** In 2016, Ecuadorian law allowed abortion when the life of the mother is in danger.

Very recently, following a constitutional court ruling, Ecuadorean legislators voted to de-criminalize abortion for women under 18 years old who are pregnant and married or cohabiting. President, the Law would allow women above 18 years old to terminate pregnancies in case of rape up to 12 weeks, whereas girls under 18, as well as indigenous and rural women, could terminate their pregnancy if they were victims of domestic violence.

- **Reproductive health:** approximately 79.4% of women’s demand for modern family planning methods is satisfied, according to WHO. The report indicates some critical gaps for the fulfillment of reproductive rights in Ecuador: lack of access to sexual and reproductive health services, availability of essential medicines and commodities in Public Health facilities - this reflects the lack of pharmacists among the healthcare professionals, who are 0.42 per 10,000 population.*50*

leading to a significant poverty increase. The GDP contracted in 2020 by 7.8%, after registering no growth in 2019. Confirming a regional trend, Ecuadorian women were affected the most:

- According to the National Annual Survey on Employment, Unemployment and Sub-Employment (INEC), female unemployment rate is 8.7% compared with 4.2% for male unemployed workers. Women are more likely than men to be employed in informal jobs without adequate employment conditions.
- The gender pay gap is higher among women living in rural areas. INEC census found that the median income of men in 2021 was $476 USD against $391 USD for women. Median income for men was $355 USD, compared with $251 USD for women. Salaried women in 2021 were on average 8% less paid than salaried men.
- The gender pay gap is also higher among independent workers: the gap was immanent: 39% of women against 37.8% of men.

INEC also found that 22.6% of unpaid workers were women, against 9.15% of men in a 2012 survey. Women are more likely to be involved in domestic work and care for children or elderly people than men (40 hours per week on average), whereas men report education as the main reason for inactivity. Women's labour force participation rate is 39%, compared with 58% of men, but participation decreases as the number increases; participation decreases. As the age increases, so does participation. On the other hand, men’s participation is unaffected by children in the household.

# 8.7 Gabon

Gabon is an upper-middle-income country in Africa. The fifth-largest oil producer on the continent, it has had strong economic growth over the past decade, driven by its production of oil and manganese.462 Gabon is a highly forested nation, with 85 - 88% forest cover, and houses approximately 18% of the Congo Basin Forest. These forests remain relatively intact and are home to one of the largest remaining tracts of tropical rainforest in Africa.

Employment in agriculture remains high in Gabon about 30% of the working population, and attracts a large proportion of women.463 The government has introduced several reforms to satellite services around extractives in the country. Gabon has adopted a series of reforms to its civil and penal codes designed to give women more rights in the home and at work. Among other things, these reforms allow women to own property without their husband's permission or with his permission after he has been informed.

The forestry sector is a significant contributor to Gabon’s economy.465 In 2016, GDP for forest exploitation was CFAF 91.1 billion (+21.5%), or 1.1% (0.9%) of national GDP at current prices. GDP increased by 38.7%, or 3.3 percentage points above the average growth rate in the national GDP.

The entire sector represents CFAF 240.1 billion, or 2.9 percent of national GDP.

Timber exports are Gabon’s second main export after oil and manganese, accounting for roughly nine percent of the country's total value of exports. Gabon exports USD 248 million in sawn wood annually to Europe, which represents about one-third of its total timber exports.467 The main destinations for sawn wood are China (USD 218 million), Belgium (USD 16.8 million) and the Netherlands (USD 15.9 million).468 In addition, approximately 30% or 1.2 million hectares of Gabon’s 21 million hectares of rainforest are allocated as concessions for commercial timber production.
