# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 238

Currently, Gabon-specific gender studies on plantation and agroforestry is limited, although the Congo Basin Forest Partnership (CBFP) has recently launched gender consultations on women's rights in the forestry sectors of Gabon and Congo. Research is currently underway (conducted around three sites in Gabon) to assess the role of women in the production and marketing of non-timber forest products (NTFPs), which reveal that women-headed household rely on this as an important source of revenue.

Kiprot and Franelz (2012) explore these issues broadly in Africa from a gender perspective, examining women's participation relative to men and the challenges and successes they experience.

Particular agroforestry practices examined include fodder production and utilization, soil fertility management, woodlots and indigenous fruit and vegetable production and processing. The review shows that women are more involved than men in these activities, particularly in terms of their participation is low enterprises that considered men's domain, such as timber and hives in enterprises that have little or no commercial value, such as collection of indigenous fruits and vegetables.

Furthermore, the degree of women's involvement relative to men in technologies such as soil fertility management, fodder production and woodlots is fairly high in terms of proportion of female participation. Women are also more involved than men in agroforestry activities and the number of trees they plant. Data on whether women are able to manage agroforestry practices as well as men are mixed, although it is clear that women do most of the work but have less access to resources such as land, tools, credit and extension services. This is mostly due to scarcity of resources. In marketing, women are also confined to the lower end of the value chain (retailing), which limits their control over and returns from the productive process.

As the World Bank highlights, Gabon stands out as having a relatively low percentage of the population under international poverty line in comparison to other sub-Saharan African countries. This is largely due to its high per capita income and relatively high Gini index. Income are relatively high (Gini Index of 38) and are compounded by a large urban-rural divide which exists despite the fact that 89% of the population lives in urban areas.**c** With regard to the feminization of poverty, it is important to note that women are more vulnerable than men.

* Gender plays a limited contribution to urban inequality but plays an important role in shaping opportunities in rural areas.**d**

Gabon spends approximately $3.445 of its GDP on health care (2014) which is comparatively less than neighboring countries. Despite this level of spending, Gabon has managed to expand its health coverage through the national healthcare system (the Caisse Nationale d'Assurance Maladie et de Sécurité Sociale). However, the country still faces challenges in attracting and retaining skilled personnel from abroad. Despite this social protection system, Gabonese women continue facing significant challenges to their health which include:

- A high rate of maternal mortality (252 in 2017) which is far higher than the rate of 20 maternal deaths per 100,000 live births that the WHO deems acceptable. Here again, there are large rural-urban difference with respect to coverage with 94% of urban women delivering in a health structure compared to 78% of rural women.
Prior data from 1998 on MMR suggests that most hospitalizations occurred due to malaria which is endemic in Gabon.

# Indonesia

Indonesia is a developing economy with abundant natural resources, and has employed its forests as a means towards development and improved economic benefits. This is reflected in Global Forest Watch's forest change data: In 2001, Indonesia had 93.8 million hectares of primary forest, which was reduced to 75.4 million hectares by 2016. In fact, it lost 18.4 million hectares of forests, equivalent to 157M of CO₂. Activities that contributed to deforestation include unpermitted felling of natural forest timber at concessions; the conversion of forested areas due to agricultural expansion and infrastructure development; illegal logging; poor land-use planning; inadequate forest management; illegal logging; encroachment and illegal land occupation in forested areas. Deforestation and forest degradation in Indonesia, therefore, are inseparable from Indonesia's political economy.

The Indonesian government’s commitment to include gender mainstreaming in national development was sealed in 2000 through a mandate designed to trickle down into all policies and programs in all sectors. Even before it merged with the Ministry of Environment, the Ministry of Forestry recognized gender as an important component of its policies and activities that has since continued. Women are encouraged to participate in projects through the Government’s Gender Action Plan (GAP) and other mainstream gender into forest and environmental policies, including those targeting forest tenure.

The country's political economy, however, continues to experience gender barriers: Indonesia has made significant progress on several dimensions of gender equality, including achieving gender parity at all levels of education. However, women continue to be underrepresented in politics and have not yet translated into increased economic participation of women. Indonesia’s female labour force participation rates have remained low and stubbornly stagnant over the last two decades. The country has a large population with 260 million people, which is also the world's largest archipelago - approximately 6,000 islands are located among the total land area of 1.4 million square kilometers. Indonesia’s population is diverse in terms of ethnicity, and religion.

Supply chain sectors

Palm oil

Indonesia is the world's number one producer of palm oil - and has witnessed zero-deforestation commitments in this particular supply chain, although implementation problems remain free. For example, there are two groups or consortia that produce palm oil products with overlapping memberships/egements: the Indonesian Palm Oil Producers' Club (IPPI) and the Sustainable Palm Oil

## Manifesto (SPDN).**1** Aside from deforestation, they have attempted to address social issues explicitly as well land rights, conflicts and smallholder issues *which indirectly impact forest loss in Indonesia*. Research undertaken by World Rainforest Movement has revealed that gendered exploitation of palm oil plantations is a major issue. The extent of this exploitation and the extent it is difficult to estimate. These dynamics can range from lower r/ho pay for the same work, sexual harassment, lack of access to violence at the workplace, and splitter effects of environmental impacts of palm oil plantation.

In Indonesia, women and children are often forced to work for free to help men meet the production targets that the company sets as a condition for them to receive the full wage for the day. WfR**2** Women are also subjected to sexual harassment and violence in the workplace, including spraying of pesticides. These are tasks that are extremely dangerous for women and their reproductive health.**3** Gender-based violence in the palm oil supply chain has remained insidious.**4** Women have reported being beaten by male workers, having their wages withheld, and having their children unattended unpunished. In addition, violent arrests or searches have been conducted by private sector entities arising from women’s refusal to work under these conditions. This comes with women having their traditional oil production taken away or destroyed, in many cases.

Research conducted by Stockholm Environment Institute in the Central Kalimantan Province has revealed that local communities have severe grievances about the impacts of oil palm plantations on *water resources and water quality*. The expansion of oil palm plantations has led to the pollution of drinking water and drying community wells. Women are often responsible for household water provision, and strain on water resources often force them (and girls) to travel long distances to access clean water. This increases their workload in increasing their drudgery, as well as higher exposure to the risk of sexual assault or violently searched.

## Rubber

Rubber is the world’s second-largest producer of rubber, and has the highest acreage under rubber cultivation globally. The land-use change that has fueled this expansion has had significant impacts on Indonesian forests (particularly in Jambi and other parts of Sumatra, Kalimantan of the Borneo region) as well as socioeconomic features.

When rubber was recognized as an increasingly lucrative commodity, the division of labour in agricultural production became more pronounced. Women were responsible for subsistence agriculture in rice fields and homesteads, while men were responsible for rubber production and marketing**5** Rubber trees are usually tapped every day, so labour input is constantly required. Male and female household members are both involved in rubber cultivation

# Plantation forestry

Plantation forests require intensive management, but they grow faster than natural forests.

In 2011 FAO reported that although they accounted only for % of the total forest wood area, it produced more than half of Indonesia's world's wood production. Indonesia has made several attempts to promote sustainable management and commercialisation of forests, and currently it is one of several countries participating in the REDD+ (Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation) programme.

Indonesia is in the second phase of implementing policy reforms and REDD+ pilots and transitioning towards the third phase of performance-based payments. Indonesia has also established the community plantation forests (CPF) programme in 2007 by the Ministry of Forestry with the aim of increasing community involvement in forest utilisation.


## Latest Global Gender Gap Index score

Indonesia has closed 68.8% of its overall gender gap, corresponding to a rank of 10th globally, although this gain is just 1 percentage point higher than in the previous edition. This decline has resulted mainly from wider Economic Participation and Opportunity gaps where the country has lost 4 percentage points since last year, regressing to a gap currently closed of 64.7%. This can be attributed to the fact that female participation in the labour force has been halved from 59% to 29.8% just in one year. Beyond the performance of this indicator, women participate less than men in the formal sector (30.1% vs. 64%), and in education (81.8%) and wage and income gaps remain large (78.9% and 51.7%, respectively). The gender gap in the women's employment is in the informal sector (compared to 79.4% of men).

Indonesia's GGDI score in 2021 was 0.688, which means it ranks 10th at the regional level and 101 globally. Sub-scores are as follows:

- Economic participation and opportunity: 0.537
- Educational attainment: 0.942
- Health and survival: 0.952
- Political empowerment: 0.688

* Multidimensional poverty is the post-2015 SDG 10 Agenda Framework, SDG 1: Target one poverty elimination - In all forms and dimensions. This mandate requires tools to enumerate quantitative and assess qualitatively poverty levels (World Bank, 2020). The World Bank’s Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) is a tool that measures multidimensional poverty in line with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), an important tool as it complements the international poverty line statistics by showing how many people are poor and what they lack.

Tertiary education has been expanding in the last decades, and, in 2020, 25-34 year-old women were more likely than men to achieve tertiary education in all OECD countries. In Indonesia, 21% of 25-34 year-old women had a tertiary qualification in 2020 compared to 16% of men. The gender gap is even larger when looking at the proportion of women with a bachelor’s degree or higher: among young women and 39% among young men. UNESCO found that, despite the fact that women in Indonesia are well represented in STEM disciplines, women tend to concentrate in specific fields, such as chemistry and medicine, and be underrepresented in others, such as physics and chemical engineering.

Youth employment rates for women have improved from 2015 to 2020 across all levels of education. Only 47% of 25-34 year-old women with lower secondary attainment were employed in 2020 compared to 89% of men in Indonesia. This gender difference is even larger for those with upper secondary attainment, where 63% of women and 69% of men with below upper secondary attainment are employed.

The World Bank indicates that, according to projections, there might be large losses in lifetime earnings due to learning losses during widespread school closures in 2020 and 2021.157

| • Abortion: abortion is allowed in Indonesia only in case of medical emergency, or severe anomaly of the foetus (for which, however, consent from both husband and wife is required). The law prohibits abortion for any other reason, including those under 24 weeks of gestation. The Guttmacher Institute reports that most abortion takes place outside of these strict conditions, and the majority are unsafe. The Institute also reports that in the case of induced abortion, 10% die from complications. | SRHR: It should be noted that from 1970 to 2002 Indonesia experienced significant gains in access to modern contraception. However, contraception prevalence has been stagnant since then. This is particularly true for young people (ages 15-19), adolescents and unmarried couples, to whom the law forbids the purchase of contraceptives. The lack of information on SRHR contributes to hamper contraceptive use and access, with only 43% of married or sexually active adolescents reporting as used contraception. Child marriage is also widespread, with one in ten girls being married before age 18: the eighth highest number of child marriages in the world. |
| • Gender-Based Violence: violence against women is prevalent in Indonesia, at home, in school and on public transport. In 2018, 37% of women aged between 15 and 49 were victims of domestic violence in the last 12 months. The labour force participation rate for women has remained stagnant, despite improvements in education, decline in fertility rates and changes to the economy. Participation rate to the workforce in Indonesia is lower compared with the average Asia and Pacific (64%), with more women in the workforce in Viet Nam, Cambodia, China and Thailand. Younger cohorts, however, report a higher participation rate than older cohorts.

Childcare and childbearing are critical factors in female labour participation, with 40% in wage employment no longer working after marrying and having children. The gender gap is greater during childcare years (15-29) compared to other ages. Women with children under the age of five highlight childcare as a major barrier.

Occupation segregation by gender is prevalent in Indonesia, with women concentrated in low productivity jobs, in services (where women represent 52% of the workers) and in agriculture.. Women represent 47% of entry-level professions, but only 13% of senior management positions. 53 out of board members, and only 23 out of CEOs. UN Women and ILO estimate a 23% gender pay gap [1].

# 8.9 Lao PDR

The Lao People’s Democratic Republic (hereinafter, Laos) has obtained significant development progress between 2000 and 2016, halving poverty, reducing malnutrition, and improving health and educational outcomes. However, since 2013 economic growth has slowed, and the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic might reverse some of the gains achieved so far.

Agriculture accounts for about 45% of GDP and 78.2% of employment in Lao PDR in 2020. Approximately two-thirds of the population live in rural areas, most of which are employed in agriculture. The main sources of agricultural export revenue are bananas, cassava and coffee beans. For the B4Green Fund, coffee and plantation forestry are a priority value chain in Laos, where the fund aims at establishing a DCA agreement.

## 8.9.1 Supply chain sectors

### Coffee

Coffee has become one Laos’ three main agricultural exports and has contributed to reducing poverty in parts of the country[56]. The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry has set as goal of increasing coffee production to 1 million tons by 2025.

There are several actors involved in the supply chain of the coffee sector, as the local and regional demand for coffee are increasing. Viet Nam imports on average 50% of Laos’ total Arabica coffee beans every year, and China and India have also come on board as new potential market opportunities[57]. The number of coffee shops / cafes as well as small-scale roasters in recent years is an indicator of the increasing popularity of Laotian coffee among locals. Some established coffee brands such as Sinou Coffee, Lao Muang Coffee, and Bao Coffee are marketed locally, hoping that the booming tourism can boost their sales[58].

The quality of Laos’ coffee has been improving and is slowly gaining market recognition. This has been achieved through a partnership between the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, Agence Française de Développement (AFD) and GIZ. The private sector is also playing an important role in introducing and developing clean agriculture to be able to export Laotian coffee through large retail networks such as Coop[59]. In 2018, the government launched a program to support farmers’ income from selling coffee beans, and more than 300,000 people (or about 6 per cent of the total working age population) engaged in jobs linked to the coffee industry.

Coffee value chain actors in Laos include:

- Agricultural input providers: supply the main agricultural inputs (machinery, equipment, fertilizers and seedlings). They also perform other roles, including collecting, trading and exporting coffee and other crops.


---
**Page 247**
---

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 243

* Coffee producers can be divided into two groups: i) independent or individual smallholder farmers, groups and cooperatives; and ii) large-scale commercial coffee producers (many of which were established by foreign investors).
* Coffee companies in Vietnam are increasingly playing an important role in collecting and selling coffee to big companies like Dau Hong Group, Thin Ngha, Cat Que, and Paksie. It has been reported that Viet Namese companies are becoming increasingly active in coffee trading in the province.

Coffee production is generated in Laos, as observed in similar countries: a study focusing on Arunachal Pradesh (India) found that women were more involved than men in all aspects of coffee production. Women included in the production chain, the division of labor is shaped by gender norms. Men tend to focus on pruning and on other tasks involving machines, while women on picking and sorting, which is physically demanding.

The study examines how the introduction of the New Economic Mechanism (NEM) - which started the economic liberalization process in 1986 drove a shift in agricultural production, which in turn had the effect of engaging women more in farm work. While economic growth has brought gains in terms of income and access to education for women, it has also led to an increase in gendered division of labor. Women have gained power on land acquisition, technology use, fertilizer application, labour hiring, harvesting, and sorting. However, men still dominate the decision-making process regarding farm production.

The G4Green Fund will focus on expanding the role of women in coffee production, strengthening their decision-making roles by increasing access to training and resources to avoid a perpetuation of gendered division of labor. It will be important also to acknowledge and address the dual burden of women in the fields and in the household, decreasing the time poverty of women farmers in order to give more relevance to their role in the community and in the production chain.

### Plantation forestry

Lao has a policy target of reaching 70% of forest cover by 2030. In 2018, the cover stood at 58%. Plantation forests, which have considerably expanded in the last few years, represented 1.7% of the overall forest coverage. Plantation forests are mostly made of teak, rubber, eucalypt and acacia. The majority of plantation forests (90%) is owned by private individuals, while only 9% are owned by smallholder farmers, whereas 48.7% of rubber plantations are owned through company concessions, 15.3% through contract farming and the remaining 36% by smallholder farmers. Finally, 88.3% of eucalypt and acacia plantations are owned through company concessions, 10.4% through contract farming only and only 1.3% by smallholder farmers.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 244

Gender norms shaping the Laoi ethnic society are diverse, and vary depending on the main ethnic groups (Lao-Tai, Mon-Khmer, Hmuun-Lu-Men, Chinese-Tibetan). Non-Laoi women seem to experience additional challenges compared with Lao-Tai women, which are the predominant group in the region. The G4Green Fund recognizes that there is no single solution for all ethnic groups and three main groups. When it comes to access to land, cultural norms are key as customary law will be respected by the government. In addition, there are different rules regarding the inheritance of women to inherit and own land) or patrilineal (which only transfers land to men, meaning women can only access it through marriage, and only sons inherit it). Laoi and to some extent Mon-Khmer ethnic groups have a strong tradition of matrilocality, and its customary law empowering women to negotiate and to uphold their rights.

Among other key barriers preventing women from participating equally and fully to forestry community life, the following can be listed: i) women are reported as having limited representation in decision-making processes at all levels; ii) women's participation is often not considered without consideration of women's needs; iii) although in 2021 Laa, with support from SIDA, unveiled a new forest code, it has not yet been fully implemented and its provisions are still very uncommon, due to security risks for crossing long distances; iv) Social and linguistic barriers, especially for women of non-Laoi-Tai ethnic groups, can hamper participation. Also, women's role in the household is often limited to domestic work and childcare, with men dominating the value chain, highlighting a lack of power and knowledge of women in terms of pricing, negotiation, and expansion of markets.

On the other hand, women's involvement in forestry has been linked with: i) a decrease in illegal activity; ii) increased representation in decision-making, as well as a tendency to sanction and exclude men who are involved in illegal activities; iii) improved forest management; iv) mobilization of people for collective action, and better access to information from external agents; v) Increased commitment and interest to protect the forest.

The G4Green Fund will strive to acknowledge these barriers, and to work on sustainable forestry increasing agency, decision-making power and knowledge of women, leveraging on their specific skills and awareness of non-timber forest products and sustainable use of forestry environments.

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The 6Green Fund

Page 247

Lao has made some remarkable progress in educational attainment and performance in the last two decades, achieving universal access to primary education. However, in 2019 the public spending on education was 2.6% of GDP, considerably lower than the 4.7% global median, and below regional neighbours like Viet Nam and Malaysia.### Specific constraints and disparities remain:

- While universal access to primary education has been achieved, completion rate for basic education is still low. In 2019, only about half (53%) of students in grade 6 met the literacy standards for promotion to grade 4, and only 1 in 5 met the standards in math. Similarly, an assessment in 2019 found that students at the lower level of secondary education were not performing well either, with only 7% reaching upper secondary.
- For every 100 students that start Grade 1, only 83 reach Grade 5, 53 reach Grade 6/Vihatham 4, and 33 reach Grade 12/Vihatham 6. However, Lao has recently made progress in improving the completion rate of secondary education.
- Only 60% of children of lower secondary age attend school, and at least 1 in 6 children (17%) do not complete primary education. This is particularly true for vulnerable groups including ethnic minorities, households where mothers have low or no educational levels, and the poorest quintile. In all these communities less than half the children between 11 and 14 years old are enrolled.
- At secondary level gender parity has not been achieved, and fewer girls than boys are enrolled. In vulnerable groups, with the exception of Chinese-Tibetan, boys attend school proportionally more often than girls.

The selection of subjects is still skewed towards occupations traditionally considered more "feminine", such as tailoring, basic business administration and hospitality.

## Lao has made some remarkable progress in health in the last fifteen years. Life expectancy continuously increased until reaching 74 in 2015, maternal mortality was decreased by 73%, therefore meeting the MDG target, immunization rate has increased, and the Health Sector Reform (HSR) has been implemented with the establishment of a new national health insurance system and the introduction of an essential service package. However, women and girls still face challenges and constraints:

* Maternal mortality rates or MMR: although MMR decreased to 185 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2017, compared with 90% in 1990, it is still above the regional average and far from the WHO and MDG goal of less than 70 per 100,000 births. Only two thirds of births are delivered by skilled health personnel (68%) as opposed to 95% in the region.
* Bank reports that the proportion decreases significantly in rural villages without road access (38%), among the poorest wealth quintile (44%) and ethnic minority groups (43%) for Child Health Services. In addition, while 70% of pregnant women receive antenatal care, only 52% receive post-natal care; the proportion is lower in the aforementioned groups: 24%, 27% and 23%, respectively.**


## Aggregate data on lifetime probability are not available for Lao, however the Lao PRB Violence Against Women Prevalence Study (2015) found that 68% of women aged 15–49 years experienced physical violence in their lifetime, and 45% have been subjected to sexual violence. The study also found that 37% of women had experienced intimate partner violence from a current or former partner; 24% of women experienced controlling behaviours by their partners in their lifetime, and 6.8 percent of them experienced such control during the past year. The study also found that 15% of women aged 15–49 years were subjected to sexual violence by an intimate partner in their lifetime.

# 8.10 Liberia

Liberia is rich in natural resources, which include: iron ore, diamonds, gold, fertile soil, fishery, and forestry. About 50% of Liberians live within 2.5 kilometres of a forest - and each day, these households spend over three hours collecting forest products for subsistence and for sale, earning about $3.4 per week. The Ebola crisis in Liberia (2014-2016) had a significant impact on the country's economy: it provided a safety net, offering resources to recover from the shocks. Informal and largely unmeasured, forest activities provide an important source of jobs, income and food security. Despite the increase in cocoa trees playing in sustaining livelihoods, 12.5% of Liberia’s forest cover was lost from 1990 to 2010.

Women - and men - in Liberia are employed primarily within the agriculture and service sector. In some areas, women grow crops for participation in the informal economy. However, women in agriculture are largely considered to be uprooted because of key constraints like: limited autonomy in production. Insecure land ownership and land tenure continue to limit women’s access to productive resources. The Ebola crisis (followed by COVID-19 pandemic), the drop in global commodity prices, and increases in the cost of living that occurred from 2014 to 2015 have had a lingering impact on the Liberian economy. Liberia is currently experiencing a period of economic recovery. The country has an active JICA in Liberia and will be ensuring that gender issues identified in this assessment are addressed within the ambit or the investments proposed in the country.

## 8.10.1 Supply chain sectors

### Cocoa

Cocoa is the second largest agricultural revenue generator accounting for more than USD 31 million in 2020. Liberia has a favourable climate and fertile soil for cocoa production. There has been substantial investment in the rehabilitation of cooperative and smallholder cocoa farms. The country’s international partners, such as IFAD, continue to invest in cocoa smallholder producers to improve their livelihoods through income by modernizing cocoa farming, increasing production, and developing market access.

Research by SolidaR West Africa® has revealed the need mainstream gender equality issues in the cocoa sector. According to the study, women are underrepresented at all levels of decision-making in major players in the cocoa sector, that is investors and private companies, are willing to implement measures towards gender inclusiveness to overcome the status quo. According to a gender assessment conducted by Dilm, huge gender inequalities especially against women exists in the sector. The study revealed that an estimated 75% of the tasks in cocoa cultivation are


---
**Page 256**

# Palm oil

Palm oil is another significant cash crop in Liberia. Traditionally, it is a domestically consumed product but there has been some export development with small-scale and large investments experiencing delays due to the COVID-19 pandemic. Nevertheless, the Central Bank of Liberia’s 2020 report showed a slight decline in palm oil production (from 22,465 metric tons to 22,200 metric tons) due to limited labour mobility because of COVID-19 related restrictions.

The Government of Liberia (GoL) and the World Bank (WB) launched the Palm Oil Development Strategy aimed at enhancing the productive capacity in the palm oil sector, particularly at the smallholder level through the outgrowers’ scheme. The strategy proposes the establishment of an integrated research and development centre to provide training for skilled and semi-skilled labour to improve business services and extension services support.

An important issue that needs to be addressed to mobilize this strategy relates to gender equity and inclusion – the roles and responsibilities that men, women and youth play in every aspect of the oil value chain. Both men and women play critical roles in the oil palm value chain. However, women contribute more to processing and marketing, while men do the harvest at the smallholder level. One of the strategy’s objectives is to ensure that the outgrowers’ scheme allocates land to women. The strategy should address appropriate land rights and utilisation mechanisms to reduce land conflicts between concession holders and smallholders. It also needs to provide training for women on how to protect their land. There is an urgent need to develop gender-sensitive indicators that specifically identify, document and close the data gap on women’s activities along the palm oil value chain.

**Page 257**
---

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

## Rubber

Rubber is a dominant revenue generator, accounting for 13.5 percent of total export receipts in 2020. Liberia is the largest producer of natural rubber in Africa, accounting for USD 102 million in 2020 and producing nearly 65,000 tonnes on 87,000 hectares. Various estimates put the number of people involved in rubber production at between 34,000 and 22,000 and the number of unskilled households involved in growing rubber trees at 38,000.

Women and youth constitute a significantly proportion of the labour force on the rubber plantations and private rubber farms across the country. They provide labour for clearing, planting, harvesting, and processing rubber. The gender equality concerns regarding rubber are embedded in the concession agreements with large producers which do not clearly address women's rights to land ownership or access to credit.

## 8.10.2 Latest Global Gender Gap Index score

Liberia has closed 69.3% of its overall gender gap, achieving a rank of 94th globally. Only 54.4% of Liberia’s literacy gender gap has been bridged with a literacy rate of 34.1% for women against 60.7% for men. The largest gaps are in the Political Empowerment sub-index (the overarching Educational Attainment gender gap category (83.9% of the gap has been bridged).

Liberia’s GGGI score in 2021 was 0.693, which means it ranks 15th at the regional level and 94th globally. Sub-scores are as follows:

## 8.10.3 Country-level gender data indicators

Gender disparities in Liberia are political participation, economic autonomy, and access to and control over resources such as land and finance. Liberia’s Gender Inequality Index (GII) value of 0.656, ranking it 156 out of 162 countries in the 2019 index. In Liberia, 11.7 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women, and 18.5 percent of adult women have reached at least a secondary level of education compared to 40.1 percent of their male counterparts. For every 100,000 live births, 641.0 women die from childbirth.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 255

In Liberia grew from USD 250 to USD 370, peaking at USD 650 in 2013[6]. Poverty levels have also decreased over the last decade, with the percentage of Liberians at or below the national poverty line falling from 63.8% in 2007 to 59.9% in 2016. Inequality has decreased marginally, with the Gini index decreasing from 45.5 in 2007 to 44.5 in 2016[6]. However, despite these improvements, many Liberians still struggle to meet their basic needs and live below the poverty line.

Despite this progress, Liberia's economy is rebounding, with economic growth of 4.9% projected for 2022-23. Per capita GDP is expected to return to pre-COVID-19 levels by 2023. Despite this, poverty levels are expected to increase in the short term due to the impact of the pandemic.

The following data indicates a gender disparity in Liberia's progress towards achieving SDG1:

* In Liberia, women and girls (aged 15+) spend 6.7% of their time on unpaid care and domestic work, compared to 2.8% spent by men.
* At 43%, the maternal mortality rate is higher than the international poverty line, compared to 43.1% of men.
* In the World Economic Forum Global Gender Gap Index (2021) - Liberia ranked 94th / 156 states.

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 256

In the last two decades Liberia has made progress in improving the health and quality of life prospects for its citizens. Life expectancy at birth has risen from 52 years in 2000 to 64 years in 2020.

* Between 2009 and 2019, Liberia’s Human Development Index value increased from 0.435 to 0.480, an increase of 10.3 percent. Despite this increase, Liberia still only ranks 175 / 189 countries. In the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap (2021) Liberia ranked 136 / 156 countries for Health and Survival.

* While maternal mortality rates have decreased between 15 - 19 decreased from 150 women per 1,000 in 2015 to 128 per 1,000 women aged 15-19 as of 2018[47] maternal mortality and women’s access to reproductive health services remain a challenge. The majority (63%) of births are by women who are far from health facilities. Although more than 50% of births take place in a healthcare facility, children born in rural settings are twice as likely to be delivered by a relative or friend, rather than a skilled provider, as compared to in the greater Monrovia area.

* The issue of sexual and gender-based violence is an increasing concern both internationally and within Liberia. This issue is expressed most frequently as a primary family matter. According to the 2019-2020 Liberia Demographic and Health Survey, 60% of women age 15–49 have experienced physical violence and 9% have experienced sexual violence in their lifetime[48]. The most common form of gender-based violence is rape. Rape is one of the most frequently reported crimes in Liberia, and the rate of reported cases has increased from 23 per 100,000 women in 2015 to 67 per 100,000 women in 2019[49].

* Female Genital Mutilation (FGM) is also considered gender-based violence (GBV). It is widely accepted and practiced in Liberia. This remains controversial because it is practiced by Liberia’s traditional Sande Society and highlights the lack of harmonization between Liberia’s traditional and national laws and the impact that has on gender roles and women’s rights[50].

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The 8Green Fund

Liberia is still in the process of rebuilding its education system after 14 years of civil war, which resulted in the destruction of much of country's trained workforce and 60% of school buildings[43]. The 2014 school closure due to the Ebola Virus Disease (EVD) outbreak, has further inhibited the recovery of the education sector.

It has one of the world’s highest levels of out-school children, with an estimated 15 to 20 per cent of 6-14 year-olds who are not in class. Just over a third of pre-schoolers have access to early childhood learning programmes and only 34 per cent of children complete primary education[44]. For those children who do attend school, the quality is poor with many schools lacking basic infrastructure.

The vast majority of Liberia’s students are older than the appropriate age for their grade and are therefore unable to keep up with their peers. This is partly due to a lack of enforcement of late entry into grade one and lack of enforcement of the age appropriate enrolment policy[45].

In Liberia there are substantial educational disparities due to gender, rural-urban locality, and income inequality - with poor rural girls and women being at the greatest disadvantage. Poverty, discrimination, and violence against women and girls in Liberia have contributed to high levels of sexual and gender-based violence negatively affecting girls’ access, retention, and completion of secondary school in Liberia. In the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap (2021) Liberia ranked 143 / 156 countries for Educational Attainment[46].

The majority of Liberian women are employed informally and in low-productivity sectors, particularly the agriculture (40% of female employment in 2019) and service section. Very few Liberian women hold positions within the professional, managerial, technical or skilled labour sectors.

Despite recent improvements, Liberia's investment climate still ranks fairly low by international standards and was ranked 175 / 190 countries in the World Bank’s Ease of Doing Business Report in 2019. Women in particular encounter several obstacles to doing business in Liberia, including:

- Education: Many women (especially those with lower levels of education) lack the skills to conduct business successfully and often find themselves in low-productivity jobs.
- The prolonged years of conflict led to high rates of illiteracy, particularly among women.
- The ownership of land is a major obstacle for women. Women are not allowed to own or successfully operate a business. Also, lack of technical skills due to the breakdown of the

- Limited access to credit and low levels of financial inclusion: Despite recent improvements in the banking sector, women still face barriers to accessing credit in Liberia. Supply and demand factors compound women's difficulty in accessing credit in the post-conflict economic environment. Lack of a culture credit and overall repayment rates make it difficult for women to access formal credit sources. Women are also perceived as higher risk. In addition, limited vision and skills among entrepreneurs and issues with inadequate (or sometimes non-existent) financial skills and lack of collateral (partly as a result of the conflict) further exacerbate this problem.

- Low levels of financial inclusion: The overall rates of financial inclusion in Liberia are low - particularly for women - with only 28.16% of women have an account at a financial institution or mobile-money service provider, compared to 40.57% of men.

- Limited financial literacy and business training; and limited social protections or childcare options.

# 8.11 Zambia

Zambia is a large, landlocked and resource-rich country, with a sparse and mostly urban population. While poverty data are not yet recent, and only available until 2015, the World Bank recorded a significant contraction of the economy by 2.8% in 2020, with recovery expectation of 3.3% for 2021/22. The country’s poverty rate is estimated at around 64%, as per data from the World Bank expected poverty to increase slightly in 2021, reaching 60.8%. Agriculture accounts for approximately 19% of the GDP and employs roughly three quarters of the population. Main crops include maize, which is used both for food and animal feed; tobacco, groundnuts, soybeans, coffee, groundnuts, rice, and cotton as well as horticultural produce. For the G4Green Fund, grains and staples are a priority value chain in Zambia, where the fund aims at establishing a JICA agreement.

## 8.11.1 Supply chain sectors

### Grains, staples

Maize is the main staple cultivated in Zambia, with approximately 3,387,470 tonnes produced in 2020 and almost 39 million USD export revenue. According to FAO™, supplies for 2021/22 marketing year are satisfactory: to give an example, in this period, Zambia recorded its largest corn crop since 2015. However, the country’s maize production is highly dependent on rainfall and also the main cause for lower export demand from neighbouring countries.® However, FAO also flags that poorer rains than expected are hampering 2022 crop prospects, and that while food insecurity has reduced slightly since the same period last year, 1.6 million people are still in need of assistance.

While agriculture accounts for a smaller share of GDP compared to other African countries, mostly due to its low productivity, it is the main source of livelihoods on the continent, with women providing 70% of agricultural labour.® It is also a driver of deforestation, especially in the Eastern Province.®

### Farming is gendered in Zambia, with female farmers encountering a number of challenges.

Women suffer from insecure land tenure, both in customary and statutory systems.® Studies show that land tenure insecurity translates into reduction of climate smart agricultural practices: In Zambian households where women have less tenure security, they apply 13.18% less fertilizer, follow 4.5% less land areas, and use intensive tillage techniques on 3.5 fewer acres.® There is also evidence


---
**Page 265**
---

Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4Green Fund

Page 281

that women’s inequality over land and tree resources reduces agroforestry adoption.[69] The state now prioritises issuance of land to women by law.

Moreover, while representing the majority of the labour force, women farmers in Zambia work with little reward or income, and have traditionally less access than men to productive services, resources and markets. [70]

However, programmes which integrated a gender perspective in their design and implementation led to promising outcomes. For instance, the FAD-led and EU-supported “Conservation Agriculture Scaling Up Project” (CASP) in Zambia was designed with a gender perspective from its inception. It recognised the challenges and inequalities faced by women, and provided access to support and education, on both conservation agriculture and ICT techniques.

The G4Green fund will assess the specific needs of women farmers, aiming at addressing them and leverage on progress made successful initiatives in other programmes and projects. A specific emphasis will be provided on access to land, resources and education.

* The COVID-19 pandemic hit the Zambian economy hard, and increased inequalities and hardships already present for women and girls, by further increasing unpaid care work and exposure to hazardous working conditions. The poverty rate among the informal economic activity reduced the income of its labour force, of which 74% are women in Sub-Saharan Africa.

** Multidimensional poverty: In the post-2015 SDG era Agenda 2030 Framework, SDG 1 targets poverty elimination - in all dimensions. The World Bank defines multidimensional poverty as a state where people lack access to basic needs and services in different dimensions of their lives simultaneously, calculated by GIDP (Generalized Individual Deprivation Index) and Human Development Index (HDI). It is an important tool to identify the population below the poverty line statistically.

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The G4S Fund

The Government has identified education as a key priority for development, and included it in the 7th National Development Plan. However, expenditure on education has reduced to financial constraints, and the country has experienced gains as well as steps back.

- While the secondary net enrolment rate increased from 25% in 2016 to 42.9% in 2018, the primary net enrolment rate declined from 73.6% in 2016 to 87.9% in 2017.
- The proportion of Grade 1 entrants with pre-school experience also declined from 29.8% in 2016 to 26.1%.<sup>25</sup>

## Transition from primary to secondary school is still difficult, due to geographical disparities and the lack of infrastructure

Transition from primary to secondary school is still difficult, due to geographical disparities and the lack of infrastructure. There are only 340 secondary schools across the country, against 8,843 primary schools. Gender parity has been achieved for primary education, but inequalities reappear in grade 12, with a parity index of 75%. The gap is most pronounced in urban areas and between provinces, with Provinces recording the widest gap.
- Among the numerous reasons for this gap there are social norms around puberty and menstruation, associated with the need of isolation and with stigma. Girls drop out due to having to walk long distances to reach the schools, posing a significant risk of sexual abuse. The lack of toilets in schools is also a major issue as it is not a girl-friendly environment.
- Low quality of education and teenage pregnancy. Only 50% of all the 13,468 girls who dropped out of school due to pregnancy returned to school in 2017.

## PISA-D scores from 2017 indicate that in Zambia: 55% of students achieve the minimum level of proficiency in reading and 25% in mathematics; Girls outperform boys in reading by 14 percentage points, but only by 3 percentage points in mathematics. The majority of rural schools are reading with a performance difference of 56 score points, the equivalent of more than a year of schooling.
- Advantaged students perform at least 24 to 40 more than their disadvantaged peers and attain higher level of proficiency in mathematics.
- The percentage of the Zambian population that has attained at least grade 7 by age 15 in 2017 was 38%. The remaining 64% of 15-year-olds were either in grades below 7 or out of school.<sup>26</sup>

**Zambia's healthcare system is based on universal access, although there are significant constraints to funding and availability of care. The system is decentralized, and broken down in three levels: hospital (district), secondary (provincial) and tertiary (central). There is a clear gendered dimension to access to health, despite significant gains in the last two decades:**

* Maternal mortality rates or MMRs: Zambia has decreased MMR by 300% in 16 years, going from 729 deaths every 100,000 live births in 2002 to 278 in 2018. This has been mainly possible due to the reduction of maternal mortality ratio (MMR) from 435 per 100,000 live births in 2002 to 69 in 2018. **This is however still far from the target of reducing deaths to 70 every 100,000 births**, as enshrined in target 3.1 of SGC 3. Early marriage decreased from 45% in 2006 to 39% in 2018. **Adolescent pregnancy is also high,** and significantly higher in rural areas compared with urban areas (71% vs 20% in 2014) leading to school drop-out and exposure to health risks. Moreover, 56% of girls who became pregnant while in school are between 10 and 14 years old. **

**Aggregative data for lifetime probability are not available in Zambia; however the 2018 Zambia Demographic and Health Survey (ZDHS) shows that 39% of women aged 15–49 years old have experienced some form of physical violence at their lifetime.**

# 9. National policy environment and legal framework for gender-transformative investments

Around the world, gender and social inequalities impede women, girls, and other marginalized social groups in terms of economic opportunities, endowments, and access to assets, limiting their development potential. In Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC), countries are taking the lead in certain value chains (such as the niche sustainable coffee value chain in Peru), and put limiting rules in certain value chains (such as cactus in Mexico). Men—particularly indigenous men in Latin America—are agricultural workers, and are faced with complex gendered realities as well.

In terms of national policy and legal frameworks, although most countries have progress on gender equality, the reality is still to rely deeply on patriarchal norms that treat women and girls in respect to men and boys and unequal power relations. The formalization in laws and replication in practices and traditions perpetuates the cycle of discrimination, which becomes cemented into social norms and cultural values.

Decades of research have proven that promoting gender equality in education, employment and health not only delivers benefits for women but for whole communities and economies. The lever for improving gender equality is to strengthen policy frameworks at national levels as well as their percolation to diversified levels of governance.

This section uses the OECD Development Centre's Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) research. The SIGI is an innovative measure of discriminatory social institutions. The SIGI’s indicators are grouped into four thematic areas: family, work and property rights, political participation, and access to resources and assets. Each area has five sub-indexes: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties. Since economic empowerment is a key driver of social inclusion and social mobility, particularly in terms of income or education, the SIGI focuses on social norms, offering an analytical lens to explain persistent gaps in these outcomes between women and men.

This section also tabulates when the 17 target countries ratified the:

CEDAW: Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women; and the CERD: International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination, which remain milestone international commitments towards improved gender and racial equality globally. Further information can be found on the UN Treaty Body database.

24 Development note on the SIGI: https://www.oecd.org/dac/development/gender/background-paper_cover.pdf

| In principle, the state’s Constitution affords each citizen the right to own property individually or jointly with other (art. 17, 1992). The law also provides that a husband has no right over his wife’s property (Ibid.). However, these rights are hindered by other discriminatory laws, both in civil and customary legal regimes that do not recognize the equal status of women as men. In the case of marriage under civil law, marital property is under the authority of the husband and can be disposed of at any time without the consent of his spouse (Ordinance 81-02, 1981). In the case of divorce, marital assets are divided in accordance with the property regime chosen at time of marriage: community or joint ownership – which is the default regime (Emery, 2013). |

## Financial rights

Under customary law, only men have the right to own the land; women are often regarded as the property of the husband, and thus are excluded from inheritance (US Department of State, 2017). FADP. In the case of polygamous marriages, women who share the same man are required to cultivate land belonging to their husband and other wives, and are disproportionately affected with regards to discriminatory laws and practices.

Women and men have equal access to formal financial resources, including microfinance services and accessing credit (World Bank, 2016). These rights are entitled to each citizen under the State's Constitution, which stipulates those women and men have equal rights in terms of property ownership and inheritance (Constitution, art. 99). In practice, women often experience difficulties in accessing microcredit to engage in income-generating activities (CEDAW, 2014).

## Colombia

### Key Institution

Presidential Council for the Equality of Women

Additionally, other ministries have gender cells - for example the Ministry of agriculture has the Directorate for Rural Women.


### Land rights

Women have the same legal right as men to own, use and make decisions over their land and non-land assets (Civil Code, art. 181), which is guaranteed by the Constitution (art. 58) and the Civil Code (art. 669). The law 1440/2011 (art. 3–5) further guarantees rural women access to land and non-land assets, including credit for their production activities, credits and technical support for their production. After divorce, men are obliged to transfer the title of their collective property, and in case of conflict, the allocation of assets is decided by the judge (Civil Code, art. 1820 to 1834).

However, the armed conflict has had deleterious effects on women's land rights. The conflict has displaced millions of people, which has made land claiming and distribution more complicated. The law 1440/2011 on the assistance for rural women in the context of the armed conflict emphasizes the protection of women and other vulnerable groups (art.

## Financial rights

The CEDAW was ratified on 19 January 1982.

CERD

## Key Institution

**Ministry of the Family, Women, and Children**

### Land rights

Married women do not have the same rights as married men to own, use, make decisions and use as collateral land, property and other non-land assets (Law on Marriage, art. 79 & 81). All goods acquired, inherited or earned during the marriage are considered common goods between husband and wife (Law on Marriage, art. 79). However, spousal consent is required for major transactions (Law on Marriage, art. 81). The law provides for joint ownership of land used or acquired on informal leases (Law on Land, art. 10).

Unmarried women have the same rights as unmarried men to own, use, make decisions and use as collateral land, property and other non-land assets (Constitution, art. 11). After divorce or separation, women retain their rights of ownership regarding or deriving, using, making decisions and using as collateral land, property and other non-land assets (Law on Marriage, art. 108).

The law provides women with the same rights as men to open a bank account at a financial institution (Law on Marriage, art. 66) and to obtain credit (no restriction).

### Financial rights

Several initiatives have been implemented to increase women's access to credit by the government, such as a "Women and Microfinance" programme launched in 2014 and a microcredit programme to facilitate access to financial resources at a reduced cost for female entrepreneurs (Republic of Côte d'Ivoire, 2014).

# Democratic Republic of Congo

## Key Institution
The Ministry of Gender, Family Affairs and Children

According to the state’s Constitution, women and men are equally guaranteed the right to property in accordance with law or custom, as well as the right to inheritance (Constitution, Art. 10, 2011). Taken together with the law that prohibits discrimination against women, this requires the government to ensure women have equal access to land rights, financial rights and other resources (CERD, 2011). In principle, the Constitution recognizes customary law, but only to the extent that it does not contradict the Constitution.

## Land rights

Under statutory law, no distinction is made in the provision of land rights between women and men (Land Law 1973). For married persons, the state passed the Family Code in 1987 which outlines the division of property. The Family Code provides for equal rights for husbands and wives. These include the Separation of Property where each spouse keeps their own property. Common Acquisition is where both spouses keep the property they acquired before the marriage, but share the property acquired during the marriage. Joint Ownership is where both spouses share property (where both spouses share the property acquired before and during the marriage) (Family Code, Arts. 487-537, 1987).

## Financial rights

Amendments to the Family Code eliminate the need for a married woman to obtain her husband’s consent in order to open an account in any legal entity (Law 16-008, 2016). In principle, the amendment allows women the right to open individual bank accounts as well as access formal microfinance services. However, in practice, there is a general resistance from the government enacted microfinance legislation which guarantees the right of each citizen to access microfinance services without any discrimination (Law 11/020, Art. 9-10, 2011). In addition, women have the rights to open bank accounts, access credit, and any other microfinance service (Law 11/020, Art. 9-10, 2011).

Despite the constitutional guarantees of equal participation, there is a general resistance toward women’s economic empowerment in the country, even if it serves to enhance the well-being of families and communities. Women have historically experienced economic discrimination, particularly in access to credit (US Department of State, 2017; CEDAW, 2013). This is particularly so

# Ecuador

## Key Institution

### Constitutional equality

Brazil’s Federal Constitution enshrines the principle of equality.

Women and men have the same rights to own, use and make decisions over land (Constitution, art. 66, 69, 321). In particular, article 321 of the Constitution guarantees that women and men are equal in all areas, including all forms of property ownership, whether public, private, community, State, or ancestral.

Since 2008, 40,100 hectares of land have been redistributed to 11,081 beneficiaries, 31% of which were women. Regarding land titling, ancestral communities have received collective titles to about 500,000 hectares benefiting almost 20,000 persons, 47% of which are women (Derec, 2017).

However, there are some discrepancies between the Civil Code and customary practices. Most land titles are attributed to the husband and remain under their disposition. In rural areas, the double signature for the acquisition of estate or property is not always respected. The wife’s signature is often omitted; often it is the husband, stipulated in the contract. Thus, women end up losing access to land and property (FAO, Gender and Land Rights database).

### Land rights

No restrictions could be found regarding women's right to open a bank account or obtain credit for agricultural purposes. The right of women to mortgages is guaranteed by article 375 of the Constitution in relation to the right to a healthy environment and decent housing (CEDAW, 2013).

The CEDAW Committee (2013) notes the existence of a credit programme to facilitate the access of women in extreme poverty to sustainable micro-enterprises. The law on rural land and ancestral

## Gabon

### Key Institution
The National Support Centre for Women's Organizations in Gabon

#### Land rights
The legal framework provides married/unmarried women with the same rights as married/unmarried men to own, use, make decisions and use as collateral land, property and non-land assets. The Civil Code stipulates that each spouse has a right of ownership over goods (buildings and other buildings) and can dispose of it as they see fit (art. 346). The right to property is enshrined in the Constitution, which states that property belongs equally to men and women (art. 1-10). After divorce or separation, the law provides equality for women and men in terms of matrimonial regimes and the separation of assets (Civil Code, art. 346).

#### Financial rights
The Civil Code stipulates that a married woman can open a bank account on her own and administer and enjoy the income she earns (art. 262). The Civil Code stipulates that a married woman can obtain credit from financial institutions without the consent of her husband but must be notified of the opening of the bank account and has to give permission for debt (art. 257). The legal framework also allows women who are not married or unmarried to obtain credit (African Union Commission, 2015).

# Indonesia

## Key Institution
**Ministry of Women Empowerment and Child Protection**

### Land rights
Married and unmarried women have the same rights as men to own, use, make decisions and use as collateral land and non-land assets (Civil Code, Article S/10; Marriage Law, Article 35; Constitution, Article 29). However, in practice, women are not able to register their property (Marriage Law, Articles 32 and 36) and both spouses must agree for the administration of marital property (Marriage Law, Article 36(1)).

Although the law formally adopts the concept of joint ownership of property purchased during marriage or marital property, only few women are aware that they have this right. It is customary for the husband to register marital property in his name alone. This combined by the fact that there is little awareness that title certificates for marital property can be issued in the name of both spouses.

### Financial rights
No legal restriction on married women's rights to open a bank account or access financial institutions could be found in the text of the Civil Code and there are no barriers to the acquisition of assets, as it prevents married women from executing a deed or agreement on their own. However, women need the consent of their husband for any transaction involving property.

It is customary for the husband to register marital property in his name alone. This combined by the fact that there is little awareness that title certificates for marital property can be issued in the name of both spouses.

### Land rights
Access to land is regulated by the amended Land Law (2003). The State owns all land but may grant the right to possess, use, transfer and dispose of land in accordance with the law. Joint land registration and titling are authorised for married and de facto couples (art. 43 and 49). Vacant land and land abandoned by owners can be used for construction, development, cultivation or animal husbandry (art. 55).

## Financial rights

## Liberia

### Key Institution
The Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection

According to the Liberian Constitution, "every person shall have the right to own property alone as well as in association with others." This law ensures that women have the same rights as men to individually or jointly hold property. These privileges are also extended to married women (Marriage Law, 1973) and unmarried women who hold property individually or jointly (Domestic Relations Law, 1973; Descendants Estates Law, 1973).

### Land rights

Women are reported to have benefited from increasing rates of female and joint land titling (ADB, 2012). The nation-wide process of land registration and regularization is ongoing though, meaning that women and men risk eviction or expropriation without compensation if they do not hold a formal land title law (ADB, 2012).

Matrimonial property is divided equally among spouses after divorce. However, in the event of adultery or bigamy by one spouse, all matrimonial property may be awarded to the parent to whom child custody is given (Domestic Relations Law). Land law is silent on land division and use among spouses after divorce.

The banking laws of Lae PDR (e.g., 1995 Law on the Bank of Lae PDR, 2007 Law on commercial banks, 2012 Decree on microfinance institutions) allow women to open bank accounts and access credit. However, this is limited by their marital status.

## Financial rights

There is however a gap in the law that leaves women in consensual informal or de-facto unions unprotected. In these cases, property (even if jointly acquired), is assumed to be held by the man.

Financial resources are more often decided under the man's name. In customary law, unmarried women are granted the right to use land by virtue of affiliation with a customary group.

While women and men are afforded the same rights to open a bank account in Zambia, access to formal financial services is limited due to lack of collateral and other factors. The availability of financial resources is debatable. Several factors limit women's access to financial resources including: gender stereotypes; discrimination against women; and the perception by some financial institutions that women pose a higher risk or corruption that at times comes in the form of sexual harassment. This has been highlighted in the report by IFC (2014). As such, women have relied on informal lines of credit such as credit or loan clubs, otherwise known as susu (IFC, 2014).

## Zambia

### Key Institution

**Ministry of Gender and Child Development**

In Zambia, there is a two-tier system of land ownership. It may be administered under statutory and customary tenure (CEDAW, 2010). Customary tenure gives women the right to use land but not the rights as men to own, use, make decisions over land and non-land assets and use as collateral. Customary lands are governed by traditional laws and customs. Women have no legal rights of disposal of property; wife or children may have access to it (CEDAW, 2010). Customary land accounts for the majority of total (94%) agricultural land in Zambia (Zambia National Agriculture Investment Plan 2014-2018). The law on customary land centres on Tenure, Land Rights and Evictions, 2004. Therefore, most of the people in Zambia are subjected to customary law.

The Zambia National Agriculture Investment Plan 2014-2018 provides that at least 30% of beneficiaries shall be women (Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, 2015). It recognises the dual role of women as farmers and household heads. The recognition of land rights, thus adequate support services may lead to positive outcomes.

### Financial rights

There is no legal restriction located to suggest that women and men do not have equal access to formal financial resources. Married and unmarried women have the same right as men to open a bank account.

at formal institutions or obtain credit. However, in practice gender gaps in financial inclusion are persistent and women are less likely to use credit or access financial services (SADC, 2013; Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit, n.d.). 

The main constraints women are facing in regard to economic empowerment are: lack of supportive policy and legal framework; lack of awareness about the importance of financial inclusion for women’s empowerment; lack of understanding of financial institutions and services; lack of products and services tailored for women (Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit, n.d.; United Nations Children's Fund/Child Development, n.d.). Women often are not treated as serious borrowers by financial institutions. In some societies, the lack of collateral impedes women’s access to credit that is considered a major requirement by most financial institutions (CEDAW, 2010). In some countries, women need their husbands’ signature in society; some institutions still demand a signature of their husbands to obtain a loan.

# Conclusion and recommendations

This gender assessment has demonstrated that considering gender differences in the use of, access to, and benefits from commodity supply chains forest landscapes can lead to more fair and effective design of interventions and institutional arrangements. This is because gender-differences in land tenure, resource rights, and decision-making processes within households often mean that women and men have different needs, opportunities, priorities, and concerns.

Although women tend to commercialize forest products less than men, the sale of forest products can be an important source of income for women, especially in rural areas where it is a major generating cash income commonly available to men. However, women’s role in agriculture and their vulnerability to the impacts of land degradation are usually neglected.


The BGFund, focused on transitioning global supply chains from extractive to nearly sustainable forest landscapes, has been able to draw lessons from its program results and successes in addressing deforestation in many countries. In doing so, the Fund aligns with GCF’s private sector and financing strategies. The climate fund recently adopted two new decisions intended to support the transition of global commodity supply chains towards sustainability, as well as to support forest restoration and conservation in developing countries through:

- Improved role in financing development of policies and preparatory activities in developing countries; and
- Improved policy related to making payments for verified emission reductions achieved through sustainable land management.

The Fund will scale a portfolio of investments delivering quantifiable social, forest and climate benefits as well as solid, risk-adjusted returns for investors. One of the pathways to achieve these benefits will be through diminishing gender inequality through the Invested portfolio. In principle, BGFund investments can contribute to addressing gender barriers by increasing women’s access to land, labour, decision making process and access to financial services; drawing action plans specific to the local context; and enhancing cooperation in local communities and raising awareness of women’s empowerment.

If approved, the Fund will take the following approach to addressing gendered barriers:

- The BGFund’s Gender Approach will provide a framework to accelerate gender-transformative investments (as appropriate for each deal) investments through the BGFund structure.
- The Gender Approach will be mobilized through the Development and Social Action Plans (DSAPs), which are part of the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment, self-assessment and conditions of the loan contracts of the BGFund. The Gender Analysis has influenced the logical framework (in Section E of this Funding Proposal) and has identified entry points for gender transformative investments that will lead to sustainable social benefits, which will be detailed project-level investment stage, as reflected in the Gender Action Plan (presented in Appendix A).

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan - The B4Green Fund

## Post-Investment TA

In the pre-investment phase, the B4Green Gender Framework will be applied, aiming at contributing towards gender impact targets. For gender transformative impacts, the gender assessments will be integrated in the due diligence activities. In post-investment transactions will furthermore include a more elaborate gender assessment that can be supported by the TA facility.

## 2.2.2 Post-Investment technical assistance

Post-investment technical assistance can be applied to support companies to maximize social and environmental impacts, and secure scalability and "durability" of impact, and to enhance engagement with stakeholders in the wider commodity production landscape. Post-investment TA can be mobilized as and when needed to help the company to maximize gender impacts.

## 2.2.2 Number of TA projects supported by the TAF

Number of TA projects supported by the TAF to hire external expertise to support the implementation of gender mainstreaming targets set out in The ESAP, Target: 8

Gender-related TA support incorporated in TA agreements with companies.

# Appendix B. Tentative gender and social inclusion activities

At a fund level, BGreen could be expected to engage in the following activities, based on needs assessments:

- Capacity building of SAIL Ventures staff, through internal and external training
- Inclusion of the BGreen gender approach in fundraising marketing materials
- Inclusion of the BGreen gender approach in the Fund's ESMs
- Implementation of the BGreen gender approach in the fund's investment processes
- Inclusion of gender disaggregated data in monitoring, reporting and communication of the portfolio
- Publication of ESMS, which will have the gender analysis embedded, on the BGreen website
- Publication of Landscape Protection Plans (LPPs) and Environmental and Social Action Plans (ESAPs), with inclusion of a gender lens, on the BGreen website.

At a portfolio company level, BGreen could be expected to engage in the following activities, depending on the context and supply chain/s with the company/ies:

- Adoption of a gender lens throughout the investment process, as per the BGreen gender approach
- Allocate Technical Assistance to gender-focused assessments as part of the due diligence process
- Assess and actively pursue gender transformative investment opportunities, working closely with portfolio companies to achieve gender inclusion targets as part of our transformational change ambitions
- Crowd in private sector financing for gender-sensitive and gender-transformative investments

Options appraisal for gender activities, for companies (depending on their context/business focus) as part of their ESPA at BGreen's future portfolio may include:

- Inclusion of women in smallholder supply chains
- Promotion of women entrepreneurs in the landscape
- Women-focused stakeholder engagement processes
- Women-friendly and inclusive governance structures
- Training producers on gender-balanced practices
- Training of households on (financial) decision-making, family planning, nutrition, hygiene and sanitation, with a focus on the impact on gender equality
- Development of policies related to gender equity, prevention of Gender-based Violence and SEAH.
